(b). China.
Sino-Japanese relations were somewhat eclipsed by
5
the increased tension in Soviet-Japanese relations and by
the internal situation in Japan which arose from the
incident of February 26. The only important development
in the relations between the two countries was the decision
to increase the Japanese  arrison in North China. According
to the NICHI ICHI of March 12 it is contemplated that
about ¥1,000,000 will be spent on the reinforcement of the
Japanese garrison in North China. The four-day conversa-
tions between Ambassador Hachiro Arita and Foreien Minister
Chang Chun appeared to have accomplished very little,
judgine from the communiqué issued on March 19:
"The talks were intended to pave the way for
future negotiations to adjust relations between
the two countries. It followed, therefore, that
no definite date had been set beforehand, nor any
limit set to the topics.

"Freely launching on various phases of the
Sino-Japanese situation, the conferees have not
attempted, however, to come to any special con-
clusions during the conversations."
Of more interest is the interview given to newspaper
correspondents by Major General Rensuke Isogai, Military
Attaché to the Japanese Embassy in China, after his con-
versation with General Sung Che-yuan, Chairman of the
Hopei-Chahar Political Council. According to a Domei
(news agench) despatch from Peiping dated March 14, the
conversation dealt with the relations among China, "Manchu-
kuo", and Japan in North China and with the Chinese Com-
munist invasion of Shensi Province, and Major General
Isogai afterwards stated in his interview that he was
greatly disappointed with the lack of improvement in
general conditions in the North of China since his visit
of February, l935; that he had anticipated real improve-
ments in general conditions in that region as well as in
relations among Japan, China, and "Manchukuo" as a result
of the establishment of the Hopei-Chahar Political Council;
that the Japanese military authorities should firmly
determine to arrive at effective measures to improve the
situation, which in his opinion they were fully prepared
to do; and that General Sung Che-yuan must drastically
revise his opinions concerning the general situation in
the Far East and the relationship among China, Japan, and
"Manchukuo".
As early as March 14 it was reported by the press that
Mr. Hachiro Arita, Ambassador to China, would be chosen
as the Minister for Foreign Affairs to take the portfolio
held

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
held temporarily by Hirota, the Premier. Mr. Arita left
Shanghai for Japan on March 23 and before his departure
was reported by Domei of March 2l to have declined to
express en opinion as to what extent the Chinese were
prepared to cooperate with Japan. In any case, the
Japanese newspapers reported that Mr. Arita had been
unsuccessful in persuadine the Chinese to fall in with
Mr. Hirota's three-point policy. This policy was again
outlined to the Embassy as follows on March 13:* Cessation
of anti-Japanese activities and propaganda; recognition of
the fact of "Manchukuo" through regularization of such
relations as communications, transit, customs, etc.; and
cooperation between Japan and China to combat the expansion
of communism in the latter country. Although Premier Hirota
told the Ambassador that this program had been accepted in
principle by the Chinese Government, reports emanating from
China continued to deny that the Nanking Government was in
accord with such a policy.
In a word, the gradual advance of Janan into North
China seemed to have been held in abeyance during the
month.
(c). "Manchukuo".
General Jiro Minami, retiring Japanese Ambassador to
"Manchukuo" ana conjointly Commander-in-Chief of the Kwan-
tung Army, paid a farewell call on the "Emperor Kangte"
on March 7 preparatory to his withdrawal from "Manchukuo".
General Minami departed for Japan on March 29. General
Kenkichi Ueda, who was appointed Japanese Ambassador to
"Manchukuo" and Commander-in-Chief of the Kwantung Army
on

on March 6, 1936, to succeed Minami, presented his
credentials to the "Emperor of Manchukuo" on March 30.
The reason for General Minami's retirement was his formal
assumption of responsibility for the February 26 incident
as the senior General in the Army and as a member of the
Supreme War Council.*
(d). Great Britain.
WE
On March 2 the British Ambassador addressed a letter
to the Minister for Foreign Affairs expressing his pleasure
over the news of the safety of Premier Okada (the con-
dolences of the British Government over the death of the
Premier had been conveyed to Mr. Hirota on February 27).
Sir Robert Clive also expressed his admiration for the
"tact and skill" with which the situation arising on
February 26 had been handled and his appreciation over
the measures which had been taken to protect the British
Embassy.**
On March 14, the Japanese authorities deported Mr.
Gerald Sampson, a British writer who had been arrested
on February 27 on a charge of spreading harmful rumors
during the incident. Upon his arrival in Shanghai on
March 17, he expressed the thought that his trip to Janan's
mandated islands in l934, which had been disapproved of
by Mr. Amau beforehand, may have made him an object of
suspicion.***
(e). Germany.
When asked by foreign correspondents on March 9 to
comment on Germany's unilateral abrogation of the Locarno

(f). Ethiopia.
WE
The JAFAN ADVERTISER of March 28 reported that Tomo-
yoshi Sumioka, the Japanese attorney who was the negotiator
in the attempt to arrange a marriage between Miss Masako
Kuroda and Prince Araya of Ethiopia,* had been awarded
the Commander Class of the Order of Menelik II by Emperor
Haile Selassie. In his letter of thanks to the Ethiopian
ruler Mr. Sumioka commended the activities in Japan of
Mr. Ato Daba Biru, the self-styled diplomatic negotiator
for Ethiopia who arrived in this country on September 13,
1935,** and who, according to Sumioka, had been able to
"push negotiations with the Japanese authorities to a
point where agreement on principles" had been reached.
The neture of the "ncgotiations" was not revealed. Mr.
Sumioka also remarked on the good will of the Japanese
people tovard Ethiopia and his own conviction that the
brave army of Ethiopia would defeat its enemy. In addition,
the Japanese attorney addressed a letter to the Ethiopian
Minister for Foreign Affairs urging him to establish a
Legation in Tokyo as soon as possible.
(g). Siam.
An economic mission headed by Mr. Yunosuke Yasukawa,
former Managing Director of the Mitsui interests, left

suitable for Japan's needs.
(h).
International.
(1).
The New Government's Forein Policy.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of March 14, on
794.10
the previous day Mr. Hirota, Premier and Minister for
Foreign Affairs, informed the Soviet, French, and American
Ambassadors that the foreign policy of the Japanese Govern-
ment had not undergone any basic change but that changes
in the internal conditions of Japan as well as in the
situation

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
situation in East Asia might cause the Government to be
more positive in its policies and that Japan might act
vigorously in negotiations with foreign Powers who showed
a lack of sincerity by delaying settlement of issues
pending with Japan.* Mr. Hirota's reference to changes
in internal conditions is explained by the Army's dis-
satisfaction with what they term the "weak-kneed" diplo-
matic methods employed before the February 26 incident
in dealing with China and the USSR. The Premier made
similar remarks to foreign newspaper men whomhe enter-
tained at tea in his Official Residence on March 25.
He stated that although there was no change in Japan's
foreign policy the process of adjusting relations with
other countries would be speeded up to meet the criticism
that diplomacy had been conducted with slowness. He
added that this was the meaning of a "positive" foreign
policy and that the meaning of an "independent" foreign
policy was that Japan had no alliances with foreign
countries and would therefore conduct its foreign policy
in an independent manner.
(2).
Count Baillet-Latour's Visit in Con-
nection with Japan's Bid for the 1940
Olympic Games.
Count Baillet-Latour, Chairman of the International
Olympic Games Committee, arrived in Japan on March 19.
Upon his arrival he stated, according to newspaper reports,
that he had come to Japan to survey the possible site for
the Twelfth Olympic Games and that he personally hoped
that the Games would be held in this country. In an
interview with a representative of the JAPAN ADVERTISER
on March 25, Count Baillet-Latour was quoted as having
said

to the International Olympic Games Committee.
(3). Foreigners Suspected of Anti-Japanese
Activities.
The ASAHI of March 13 reported thet the Foreign Office
desired the adoption by the competent authorities of a
firm attitude in dealing with foreigners found engaged
in anti-Japanese activities and that foreigners whose
thoughts and actions were considered dangerous to peace
and order in Japan would be advised by the Japanese con-
sular officers in their respective countries not to come
to Japan.
This newspaper attributed the Foreign Office's
attitude to the recent case of Mr. Gerald Sampson.*
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). Formation of the Hirota Cabinet and its
Statement of Policy.
The Okada Cabinet resigned en bloc on the evening of
19)
February 26 but continued provisionally in office until
March 9 in accordance with the orders of the Emperor.**
The Acting Premier during the first nine days of March was
Admiral Okada whose resignation, submitted to the Emperor
on February 28, was not accepted until the formation of
the Hirota Cabinet on March 9. On March 4 Prince Konoe,
President of the House of Peers, declined the Imperial
Command to form a Cabinet,, alleging ill health as his
reason, and on the followine day Mr. Hirota was given
the same command. On March 6 Mr. Hirota tentatively
chose General Count Juichi Terauchi as Minister for
War and between these two men negotiations took place
from March 6 to March 9. Although the newspapers reported
that the Army was opposed to Mr. Hirota as Premier, the
real
See Section III (d) of this report.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1736 of March 18, 1936.

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
real hindrance to him in forming a Cabinet was the
objection that he met with on the part of the Army over
certain individuals chosen for posts in the new Cabinet.
According to the press, the Army objected to Mr. Shigeru
Yoshida as Minister. for Foreign Affairs because of his
well-known liberalism and because of his being a son-in-
law of Count Makino, one of the senior statesmen; to Dr.
Hiroshi Shimomura as Overseas Minister because of his
position as Vice President of the ASAHI, which was con-
sidered to be too capitalistic and which had been attacked
by the mutineers on February 26; to Takukichi Kawasaki
as Minister for Education because he was a political
party man; to retention of Mr. Naoshi Ohara as Justice
Minister because he had not been sincere in efforts to
clarify the national polity; and to Mr. Chikuhei Nakajima
as Commerce and Industry Minister because of his interest
in the manufacture of munitions. The Army authorities,
represented by General Terauchi, agreed to cooperate with
Mir. Hirota in the formation of a new Cabinet if the latter
acceded to the four principal demands of the Army, which
were: (1) clarification of the national polity, i. e.
the complete suppression of the Minobe theory and of
liberalism in Japan; (2) an increase in the Army budget
for the building up of a better air force, for the com-
pletion of the mechanization of the Army, and for the
strengthening of the Japanese forces in "Manchukuo" and
at home; (3) stabilization of the livelihood of the people,
thet is, economic and political reforms, the establishment
of state control of industries and finance, and readjust-
ment of taxation with an increase of taxes on capitalists
and a decrease cn the poorer classes; and (4) the adoption
of a "positive" foreign policy. Although these policies
were

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17 -
were labelled "Army demands" by the press generally, it
should be noted that they were supported by large elements
in and out of Government circles who have no affiliations
with the Army. These elements also were opposed to a
certain extent to the appointment of the men to whom the
Army is reported to have objected. Mr. Hirota finally
succeeded in choosing a Cabinet acceptable to the Army.*
Apparently the only point on which he resisted the demands
of the Army was his insistence that the political parties
be substantially represented in the Cabinet. He was
formally installed as Premier by the Emperor on the evening
of March 9 and the complete Cabinet was announced in an
Official Gazette extra of that day as follows: Prime
Minister and Minister for Foreign Affairs, Koki Hirota;
Minister for Home Affairs and concurrently Minister for
Education, Shigenosuke Ushio; Minister for Finance, Eiichi
Baba; Minister for War and President of the Manchurian
Affairs Bureau, General Count Juichi Terauchi; Navy Minister,
Admiral Osami Nagano; Justice Minister, Raisaburo Hayashi;
Minister for Agriculture and Forestry, Toshio Shimada;
Minister for Commerce and Industry, Takukichi Kawasaki;
Communications Minister, Keikichi Tanomogi; Railways
Minister, Yonezo Maeda; and Overseas Minister, Hidejiro
Nagata. Ushio, a member of the House of Peers, had held
a number of important positions in the Home Ministry and
had the reputation of being a very capable administrator.
Baba, a well-known banker and for several years President
of the Hypothec Bank, had no decided affiliations with
any political or financial group and was well versed in
the
* Embassy's despatch No. 1736 of March 18, 193s.

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.98)
-18-
the agricultural land situation. General Terauchi was
popular in Army circles and had the reputation of having
affiliations with no Army faction and with none of the
political parties. Nagano was known for being a highly
capable naval officer, a direct and patriotic negotiator
(he was one of the two delegates to the London Naval
Conference which opened in December, 1935), and a man of
independent leanings from the political point of view.
Hayashi had had a distinguished career in the Ministry
of Justice.* The remaining members of the Cabinet had
not had outstanding careers and were chosen for their
respective posts largely for reasons of expediency and
compromise. The Minseito appointees to the new Cabinet
were Commerce and Industry Minister Kawasaki and Communi-
cations Minister Tanomogi, while the Seiyukai appointees
were Railways Minister Maeda and Minister of Agriculture
and Forestry Shimada. Mr. Hachiro Arita, recently appointed
Ambassador to China, was prominently mentioned by the news-
papers as Mr. Hirota's probable choice for Minister for
Foreign Affairs.
The statement of policy issued by Premier Hirota on
March 17 contained in essence the four basic demands of
the Army which were listed above.**
Furthermore, the statement of policy clearly reflects
the views of the February 26 insurrectionists because
these views were also held by a very considerable part
of the Army and by a large part of the population. The
fundamental desire of the insurgents was a change in the
political status quo. This was brought about.***
Several

of the aims of the rebel officers were accomplished and
others appeared definitely to be a part of the new Govern-
ment's program. At the end of the month it could be safely
predicted that the new Government would be more national-
istic than previous Governments, that it would endeavor
to effect many of the economic, political, and social
reforms so eagerly desired by the February 26 mutineers
and by a large part of the Army, and that on the other
hand, no hasty or radical reforms would be adopted.*
Mr. Takukichi Kawasaki, the Minister for Commerce and
Industry, a member of the Minseito and the only hold -over
besides Mr. Hirota in the new Government, died on March 27
and was succeeded by Dr. Gotaro Ogawa, one of the leaders
of the Minseito, a member of the Diet, and Parliamentary
Vice Minister in the Hamaguchi Cabinet. The post of
Education Minister, which had been held concurrently by
Mr. Ushio, Home Minister, was taken over on March 25 by
Dr. Hachisaburo Hirao, a Member of the House of Peers,
President of the Kavasaki Dockyards, and the Chairman of
the Japanese Economic Mission which went to Brazil last year.
Count Yasuya Uchida, who had five times held the
portfolio of Minister for Foreign Affairs and had also
been President of the South Manchuria Railway, died in
Tokyo on March 12 at the age of 71. As Minister for
Foreign Affairs he was known for his chauvinist tendencies.
(b).
Consequences of the February 26 Incident in
their Relation to the Army.
Almost normal conditions prevailed in Tokyo on the
afternoon of March 1, the insurgent movement having been
suppressed on the day before. On March 1 the Cabinet
announced
Embassy's despatch No. 1733 of March 19, 1936.

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
- 20 -
announced that Imperial Sanction had been obtained for
depriving the younger officers involved in the February 26
revolt of their Court ranks and medals and on the same day
the Martial Law Headquarters announced that these officers
had been expelled from the Army on February 29, that the
non-commissioned officers and enlisted men who had sur-
rendered had been confined to their barracks, and that
the majority of the officers who had participated in the
revolt had been put in prison, together with the ex-officers
Koji Muranaka, Isaichi Isobe, and Zensuke Isobe.* It will
be recalled that the three above-mentioned ex-officers had
been closely connected with the trial of Lieutenant Colonel
Aizawa.** On March 3 General Senjuro Hayashi called on the
War Minister, General Kawashima, and handed in the resigna-
tions of himself and the following members of the Supreme
War Council, all of whom assumed responsibility for the
incident: General Nobuyuki Abe, General Jinsaburo Masaki,
General Baron Sadao Araki, General Kenkichi Ueda, General
Giichi Nishi, and General Juichi Ueda. General Giichi Nishi,
a member of the Supreme War Council, was on March 4 appointed
to the important post of Inspector General of Military
Education to succeed General Jotaro Watanabe, who had been
assassinated on February 26. General Kenkichi Ueda was
appointed Ambassador to "Manchukuo" and Commander-in-Chief
of the Kwantung Army on March 6 to succeed General Minami.
Of the 1,360 enlisted men who took part in the military
revolt and were subsequently detained for examination in
barracks, more than 1,320 had been released by March 19.

Embassy's despatch No. 1721 of March 6, 1936.

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.98)

Twenty odd guilty officers were still under arrest at the
close of the month. Three of the rebel officers committed
suicide shortly after the incident.* An officer of the
Japanese General Staff informed the Military Attaché on
March 7 that more suicides by the insurgent officers were
expected later but that for the moment the officers under
investigation by the Military Police were prevented from
taking their own lives.
Details of the Army's demand for increased funds, which
was made as a condition for the Army's assent to the forma-
tion of the Hirota Cabinet, were not fully known at the end
of the month. However, Nippon Dempo reported that the Army
had asked for additional budget appropriations of about
¥2,000,000,000 to be spent from 1937 to 1942 and that this
money would be used for the expansion of the air force, for
the completion of the mechanization of the Army, for the
addition of several divisions to the Army, and for arrange-
ments to mobilize industries in war times. This news agency
further stated that the funds for the Army's program would
be obtained by the issuance of additional "red ink" bonds
and by an increase in taxation.** The press reported that
a consideratle increase in the Army's budget would take
place and some newspapers stated that the Army would
probably demand that funds be appropriated for the carrying
out of its program in a shorter space of time than five years.
It was reported by the JAPAN TIMES of March 26 that the
Japanese military authorities had decided to establish
additional aviation bases during the present year at
Shikaya, at Yokohama, at Kisaragu, and at Chinkai (in
Chosen).

It was reported by the press during the month that
Lieutenant-Colonel Aizawa, whose trial for the murder of
General Nagata on January 28, 1936, had been suspended
on February 26, would have to be completely retried in
accordance with the provisions of the Court Martial Law
and that no date for the retrial had been set. According
to information received by the Military Attaché from an
official of the War Office, the trial of the officers and
civilians involved in the February 26 incident will be
different from the Aizawa trial and from the trial of the
persons connected with the assassination of Premier Inukai
on May 15, 1932, in the following respects: the accused
will not have the benefit of counsel, no appeal will be
allowed on the decisions reached, and more emphasis will
be placed on establishing euilt and punishment with less
opportunity for the exposition of the political views of
the persons under trial.*
On March 26 the prefectural Governors were summoned
to Tokyo to receive instructions from the new Cabinet.
General Terauchi, Minister for War, made an address at
this meeting in which he asked the Government to cooperate
with the militery in eradicating the growing tendency
among the people to question their duty as regards con-
scription and support of the Army at all times; asked
for the "suppression of criticisms" which tended to
estrange the civilian population from the Army, such as
the rumor that the February 26 insurgents had been supported
by high Army officers or even by the whole Army; and
requested the local officials to cooperate with the Army
in
* Embassy's despatch No. 1746 of March 20, 1936.

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
- 23 -
in eliminating the lawless elements which were exhorting
the younger officers to direct action and also in
suppressing the "mysterious documents" which added to the
unrest in the Army.* (An example of these mysterious
documents was the secret pamphlet issued by Captains
Muranaka and Isobe in the sumner of 1935, in which they
defended General Masaki, Inspector General of Education,
who had been dismissed on July 16, 1935, and accused
General Nagata of being connected with the capitalists
and of being a spy for the senior statesmen.**)
The War Office announced extensive shifts in the
higher positions in the Army following the resignations,
referred to above, of Generals Hayashi, Masaki, Abe,
Araki, and Kawashima. It would appear that the purpose
of the shifts was to allow the hiher officers to show
their formal responsibility for the February 26 incident
by either being transferred to other posts or being retired
from the Army and also to freshen the atmosphere in the
Army. Among the changes made, Lieutenant General Hajime
Sugiyama, Vice Chief of the General Staff, Lieutenant
General Moto-o Furusho, Vice Minister for War, and Major
General Kiyoshi Imamura, Chief of the Military Affairs
Bureau, were transferred to other posts, and Lieutenant
General Toranosuke iashimoto, Commander of the Imperial
Bodyguard Division, was placed on the "waiting list" as
having been formally responsible for the February 26
incident. The posts of Vice Chief of the General Staff,
Vice Minister for War, and Chief of the Military Affairs
Bureau were filled by officers who had rendered meritorious
service

service in "Manchukuo" or North China; Lieutenant General
Hisazo Nishio, former Chief of Staff of the Kwantung Army,
was appointed Vice Chief of the General Staff; Lieutenant
General Yoshijiro Umezu, at one time Commander of the
North China garrison, was made Vice Minister for War;
and Major General Rensuke Isogai, formerly Military Attaché
to the Japanese Embassy in China, was chosen to be the
Chief of the Military Affairs Bureau. General Shigeru
Honjo resigned as Chief Aide-de-Camp and Lieutenant General
Okiie Usami was appointed to succeed him. It is understood
that the promotion of Usami was made over the heads of
many other Lieutenant Generals, his seniors in service,
and that the reason for this choice was to put new blood
into this high command.
Martial law was in effect throughout the month.
(c). The Throne.
Mr. Kurakei Yuasa, Minister of the Imperial Household,
resigned from that post and was appointed Lord Keeper of
the Privy Seal on March 6 to succeed Viscount Admiral
Saito, a victim of the incident. Mr. Yuasa was replaced
as Minister of the Imperial Household by Mr. Tsuneo
Matsudaira, former Ambassador to Great Britain, on March 6.
Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma, Vice President of the Privy Council
and well-known for his chauvinism, was appointed President
of the Privy Council on March 13, 1936, to succeed Baron
Ikki, who had resigned several days previously, alleging
ill health as the reason. It may be remarked that Baron
Ikki is advanced in years, that he had been severely
criticized for the past year because of his alleged
sympathy with the Minobe theory, and that he was one of
the liberal leaders disliked by a large part of the Army.
These

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
- 25 -
These may perhaps have been contributory causes of his
desire to resign.
The JAPAN TIMES of March 20 reported that Admiral
Kantaro Suzuki, Court Chamberlain, who was badly wounded
by the February 26 assassins, had expressed to Imperial
Household Minister Tsuneo Matsudaira his wish to resign
from his post. According to this newspaper, the reason
given by Admiral Suzuki for the desired resignation was
that anyone who had been attacked, whatever the cause,
should not remain in such an important position.
Embassy is unaware of Admiral Suzuki's real intentions
regarding his position of Court Chamberlain.
(a). The Navy.
In order to assist the Cabinet in formulating its
major policies, on March l2 the Navy Minister presented
a memorandum for national reform to Finance Minister Baba,
Chairman of the Policy Drafting Committee of the Cabinet.
The memorandum contained the following points: although
by no means opposed to parliamentary government and not
desirous of the establishment of a military régime, the
Navy maintains that political parties should be purified
and that the accumulated evils of the past should be
removed; the Navy hopes for a new financial program
commensurate with the present advanced state of Japan,
including the finding of new sources of revenue to meet
increasing defense expenditures; Japan must augment its
defense to cope with the international relations that
will arise after the expiration of the naval treaties at
the end of the year l936; foreign policy must be consolidated
under

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
- 26 -
under the official control of the Government and every
care must be used to avoid lack of coordination between
the Foreign Office and the authorities stationed abroad.
According to tne OSAKA MAINICHI of March 2O the Navy
desires a strengthening of national defense all along
the line and a peaceful advance southward.
(e). Folitical Parties.
Statements were issued on March 27 by the Seiyukai,
the Minseito, and the House of Peers, all of which endorsed
the intentions of the Government as set forth in the
addresses made on March 26 by the Premier and the Finance,
War, Justice, and Home Ministers at the conference of
prefectural governors. All three statements, however,
pointed out that actions were more important than words.
the KOKUMIN DOMEI and the Social Mass Party condemned the
addresses of the Cabinet members as vague and lacking in
promise, the former stating that Premier Hirota did not
know even today the real causes of the "current emergency"
and the latter emphasizing that the speeches contained no
prospect of policies that would further the interests of
the masses.
(f). The Diet.
An Imperial Rescript in the Official Gazette of
March 20 ordered the convocation of the Imperial Diet on
May 1 of this year for a session of twenty-one days.
The press reported that a fundamental reform of the
House of Peers was being advocated by its President,
Prince Konoe, and by Prince Sanetake Ichijo, Marquess
Yukitada Sasaki, and Baron Yoshiro Sakatani. According
to reports, the plan of reform proposed by Prince Konoe
includes the abolition of the heredity seats held by
Princes

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
- 27 -
Princes and Marquesses in the Upper House; a reduction
of the number of Counts, Viscounts, and Barons who are
members; revision of the system of membership by the
highest tax payers, members of the Imperial Academy and
Crown appointees; and elimination of the influence of
political parties in the House of Peers. It was stated
that an improvement in the system and operation of the
House of Peers was necessary for the execution of the
Hirota Cabinet's proposed reform of the administrative
system.
(g). Chauvinists.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of March 28, seventy
military, naval, political, and business leaders met on
the previous day and organized a new "cultural society",
the object of which will be to apply to the present emergency
situation the spirit of the Kenmu Restoration, which means
the restoration of the Emperor's powers in 1334-1335. The
leaders of this project include such noted chauvinists as
Generals Araki and Masaki and Mr. Mitsuru Toyama and also
men of more liberal leanings, such as General Senjuro
Hayashi and Prince Konoe.
The OSAKA MAINICHI of March 28 reported that Mr.
Kiyoshi Akita, former Speaker of the House of Representatives,
had decided to organize a political club of rightist
character with enough Diet members to become an influential
body in the Diet. Mr. Akita was said to have started
negotiations with members of the Kokumin Domei, the Tobokai
(Far Eastern Policy Association), the Meirinkai (Club for
the Fulfillment of the Imperial Spirit), and the Kodokai
(Club for the Promotion of the Imperial Way).
(h). The Press.
The Minister for Communications announced to the Cabinet
on

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
-28 -
on March 31 that Nippon Dempo had given its assent to
amalgamate with Domei Tsushinsha.* The purpose of the
merger of the two news agencies was the unification of
all news agencies under rigid Government control in order
that only the strictly patriotic point of view might be
presented to other countries.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Most of the bills reported by the press to have been
drafted by the various Ministries and submitted to the
Le
Cabinet for consideration were described in such general
and nebulous terms that their true nature could not be
ascertained. Among the more important bills said to have,
been drafted are the following: bills for the control by
the Government of the electric, iron, steel, coal, sugar,
gas, and fertilizer industries; a bill for the establish-
ment of a semi-governmental company for the liquefication
of coal; a bill providing for the adoption of positive
measures for the refinancinE of farm debts; a bill for the
maintenance of peace and order, with particular emphasis
on the prevention of the circulation of secret or sub-
versive publications; a bill for partial revision of the
income tax law. These bills indicate the beginning of a
reform movement in the present financial and economic
structure.**
V.
GOVERNMENT FIANCE.
It appeared from newspaper reports and from other
494.51
information obtained by the Embassy during the month that
the Finance Ministry was planning an increase in revenues
through
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1756 of April 1, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1758 of April 1, 1936.

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
-29 -
through a raise in taxation, improvement and expansion
of national defense, resuscitation of farm economy, con-
tiruation of the issuance of "red ink" bonds, equalization
of the tax burden, enforcement of a policy of low money
rates, and, in general, an increase in national disburse-
ments. These plans were set forth on March 9 in a statement
issued by Mr. Baba, the Finance Minister, who admitted that
his ideas differed considerably from the policies of the
previous Cabinet. Mr. Baba mede it clear, however, that
the chanes contemplated would be brought about gradually
and that no radical alteration would be made, for the
reason that any sudden or violent alteration of the
financial policy might invite chaos. According to Domei,
in a supplementary statement issued on March 10, the
Finance Minister emphasized the marked progress that was
being made in Japan's industry and trade but at the same
time remarked that he was not protecting the interests of
the magnates or neglecting the problem of the tax burden.
In a conversation with the Embassy's Commercial
Attaché on April 24, Mr. Takeo Kawagoe, the Vice Minister
for Finance, stated that he was afraid that some people in
the United States had gained the impression that the
February 26 incident was a sort of revolution; that this
idea was not correct, since the real cause of the disturb-
ance was the long-existing defects in Government policies
relating to industry and finance and since the purpose of
the insurrection was to bring about much-needed reforms;
that the present Government realized the need of such
reforms but expected to bring them about gradually. Mr.
Kawagoe indicated that general Government expenditures
would be increased and added that some form of increased
taxation

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.99)
- 30 -
taxation would be put into effect, that if possible red
ink bonds would be reduced, but that it would be impossible
to stop the issuance of such bonds and at the same time
provide funds for national defense and for the relief of
farmers. The Vice Minister for Finance further stated
that the foreign exchange policy of the late Mr. Takahashi
would be followed by the present régime, namely, the main-
tenance of the yen-dollar rate at around 0.28 to $0.30.
The Minister for Finance made a speech before the Pre-
fectural Governors on March 26 in which he reiterated the
ideas already expressed by him in his statements of March 9
and 10.* All in all, it may be said that the statements
that
mentioned above indicate the new Government plans definitely
to depart from the Takahashi financial policy by gradual
steps.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Japan's exports for March amounted to ¥215,473,000
and its imports were ¥276,019,000, as compared with February
exports of ¥208,420,000 and imports of ¥283,305,000. These
figures represented an unfavorable balance of trade of
¥50,546,000 as against an unfavorable balance for February
of ¥74,885,000.**
(b). Financial Trends.
The stock and bond markets were weak owing to the
prevailing uncertainty over the new Government's future
financial and economic policy. The average yen selling
rate was $0.2895, i. e., slightly lower than the average
rate for February of 0.290l.**
(c).
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1758 of April 1, 1936.
**
Information obtained from Commercial Attaché.

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.98)
-31-
(c). Shipping.
The JAPAN ADVERTISER of March 27 reported that, in
accordance with a decision already reached by him,* Mr.
Koichiro Ishihara, President of the Ishihara Industry and
Marine Transport Company, had founded the Nanyo Koro Kaisha
(South Seas Service Company Limited) with a capitalization
of ¥2,000,000, and that the new company would maintain a
bi-weekly steamship service between Japan and the South
Seas, including Singapore and the Netherland East Indies.
As reported in the Embassy's political report for February
it is understood that the new steamship company will
attempt to compete with both the Java-China-Japan Line
and the Nanyo Kaiyun Kaisha (South Seas Marine Transport
Company).

X.
MISCELLANEOUS.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of March 14,
Onisaburo Deguchi, "Grand Master", and seven other leaders
of the Omotokyo (Great Basic Cult) were formally indicted
on the day before on charges of either lèse majesté or
viol ation of the Peace Preservation Law. In a statement
issued on the same day by the Justice Ministry, it was
asserted that these persons had been arrested and indicted
for the reason that their cult contained principles which
ran counter to the national polity.

=== Page 33 === (confidence 1.00)
I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). American Oil Interests.
In reply to the proposal made in March by the
American and British oil interests that a contractual
894.6363
arrangement of sorts be made between the foreign oil
companies and the Mitsui interests so as to avoid the
necessity of storage of non-commercial stocks by the
foreign firms, the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha (Mitsui Products
Company) told representatives of the foreign firms that
if the oil companies intended to utilize Mitsui simply
as a storage or warehousing medium the Japanese company
was definitely not interested in the proposal for the
reason that Mitsui Bussan Kaisha was organized for trade
purposes. However, the Mitsui representatives expressed
a willingness to consider a broader arrangement which
would involve trading on their part and requested that
the oil companies make a definite proposal. Mr. Yoshino,
the Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, implied in
a conversation with representatives of the foreign oil
interests on April 24 that Mitsui might be more willing
to negotiate if the Japanese Government expressed a wish
that some agreement be reached but added that it would
first be necessary for the oil companies to draw up a
definite proposal for the approval of the Japanese Govern-
ment.*
(b).
Exchange of Good Will Messages between
Certain American Citizens and the Premier
of Japan.
711.94
Twenty-seven American citizens addressed a cable
message to Premier Hirota on April 14 in which they
expressed their hope for the inauguration of a "new era"
in
* Embassy's despatch No. 1800 of May 1, 1936.

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
in Japanese-American relations and the wish to depart
from the tradition of provocative statements. The signers
of the message included Dean Carl W. Ackerman of Columbia
University's School of Journalism; Bishop William T. Manning,
of the Episcopal Diocese of New York; Mr. James W. Gerard,
former American Ambassador to Germany; Mr. Grove Patterson,
President of the American Society of Newspaper Editors;
Mr. William Allen White, publisher and editor of the
EMPORIA GAZETTE; Mr. Clark Howell, publisher of the ATLANTA
CONSTITUTION; and Mr. Frank A. Vanderlip, former Assistant
Secretary of the Treasury. On April 17 Premier Hirota
sent a cable to Dean Ackerman expressing his thanks for
the communication from the American citizens, stating that
the Japanese people shared the desire for the inauguration
of a new era of American-Japanese relations, and pledging
his own earnest efforts on behalf of the Japanese people
to improve these relations. There was little editorial
comment on the exchange of messages. However, the JAPAN
ADVERTISER of April 19 remarked that presumably the state-
ment sent by the group of Americans had been inspired by
Premier Hirota's recent statement to foreign correspondents
that there would be no war while he was Premier and the
JAPAN TIMES of April 16 expressed gratification over the
desire of the Americans, who represented "the leaders of
the nation".
(c).
Problem of Land Leases Held by Japanese in
Davao Province, Philippine Islands.
A certain amount of space was given by the newspapers
to the question of possible cancellation by the Philippine
Commonwealth Government of the leases on land held by
Japanese residents of Davao Province on the ground that
such

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
such leases were held illegally. On April 13 the Foreign
Ofrice spokesman stated that he hoped that the Philippine
Commonwealth Government would recognize the vested interests
and rights of the Japanese residents, adding that the
investments of the Japanese had proved beneficial to the
Philippine Islands as a whole. Mr. Kiyoshi Uchiyama,
Japanese Consul General at Manila, was reported by certain
Philippine newspapers to have warned President Quezon of
the "grave consequences" that might follow should the
pending land problem be dealt with inadequately. According
to a Domei despatch from Manila dated April 16, Mr. Uchi-
yama emphatically denied to the press that he had made
any such warning and added that in his recent interview
with President uezon he had expressed the desire that
the problem would be solved amicably in view of the growing
friendship between Japan and the Philippine Commonwealth.
Such Japanese newspaper comment as appeared deplored the
probable effect of the controversy on Philippine-Japanese
relations and expatiated on the contributions made to the
prosperity of Davao Province by Japanese timbermen and
hemp-growers during the past thirty years.
(a).
Japanese Delegation to the Sixth Conference
of the Institute of Pacific Relations to be
Held at Yosemite, California.
It will be recalled that in March Mr. Horinouchi,
then Chief of the American Affeirs Bureau of the Foreign
Office, denied press reports of a proposed good will mission
to the United States and stated that there would naturally
be Japanese delegates, with no official capacity, at the
meeting of the Institute of Pacific Relations to be held
at Yosemite, California. The OSAKA MAINICHI of April 26
reported

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
reported that the Japanese delegation to the conference
of the Institute of Pacific Relations to be held from
August 15 to August 29 would comprise the following men:
Mr. Kenkichi Yoshizawa, former Minister for Foreign Affairs
Dr. Tadao Yamakawa, former President of Tokyo Imperial
University and at present Vice President of the Nippon
Kokusai Kyokai (Japan International Association); Lieu-
tenant General Rihachiro Banzai, an authority on Chinese
affairs; and Professor Hiroshi Nasui, of Tokyo Imperial
University.
(e).
. Visit to Japan of Philippine Assemblymen
and Journalists.
For the avowed purpose of seeking a better knowledge
and understanding of Japan, a party of three Philippine
Assemblymen and another party of eicht Philippine journalists
visited Janan during the month under review, arriving at
Kobe on April 6. One of the Assemblymen, Mr. Manuel
Alzate, stated in a press interview on April 17 that he
and his fellow Assemblymen wanted to study economic and
educational conditions in Janan and that they also hoped
to bring about mutual understanding and Eood will between
Japan and the Philippine Government.
At a dinner civen in honor of the visiting Philippine
newspapermen on April 18 by the Foreign Office spokesman,
the latter stated that the inauguration of the Philippine
Commonwealth Government in November, 1935, had been a
cause for great rejoicement on the part of the Japanese
people and that the present was the time to set to work
earnestly for the promotion and perpetuation of the
friendly relations that had existed between Japan and the
Philippines for centuries past.
(f).

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
(f). Visit to Japan of Veterans of Foreign Wars
Veteraus
of the United States.
43
Wars
Representatives of the Veterans of Foreign Wars of
the United States, headed by Commander-in-Chief James E.
Van Zandt, arrived at Yokohama on April 21 and were
extensively entertained during the succeeding week. This
unofficial delegation came to Japan on the invitation of
the Japan Ex-servicemen's Association, members of which
were the guests of honor at the annual convention of the
Veterans of Foreign Wars held at New Orleans in the autumn
of 1935. Among the functions attended by the visiting
delegation during the month were a dinner given by the
Japan Ex-servicemen's Association on April 25, a reception
given jointly by the Tokyo Prefecture and the Tokyo Muni-
cipality on April 26, an afternoon reception at the
American Embassy on April 27, a luncheon given by the
America-Japan Society at which the Ambassador spoke, a
dinner given by the American Military and Naval Attachés
on April 28; and a military review on April 29. The
editorial comment in general was decidedly favorable
toward the visit of the Veterans of Foreign Wars and the
statements given to the press by Mr. Van Zandt were well
received.
II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
EE
(a). Soviet Russia.
Soviet-Japanese relations during the month followed
761.94
the usual course, with border clashes, protests and counter.
protests, arguments from both sides concerning the settling
of border disputes and the establishment of a border com-
mission, mutual mistrust, and statements by both the Soviet
Russians and the Japanese to the effect that while the
situation

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
EE
situation was serious there should be no anxiety over
the question of war between the two countries. According
to news despatches from China, the Japanese Consul General
at Nanking called on the Chinese Minister for Foreign
Affairs on April 1 and stated that Japan could not remain
indifferent to the recently signed Soviet-Outer Mongolian
treaty, to which the Chinese official replied that his
country was as powerless to resist Russia by force as it
was to resist Japanese occupation of Manchuria. The first
serious incident of the month occurred on April 9 at
Suifenho on the Soviet-"Manchukuoan" border when, according
to reports, a Japanese lieutenant was killed and five
Japanese soldiers were missing as a result of a skirmish
between small detachments of Soviet and Japanese soldiers.
The "Manchukuoan" Foreign Office filed a protest with the
Soviet Government through the latter's Consul General at
Harbin and Ambassador Ota also protested on behalf of the
Japanese Government to the Soviet Foreign Office against
the alleged invasion of "Manchukuo's" territory.
The
Soviet Vice Commissar for Foreign Affairs was said to have
replied that the Japanese soldiers had invaded Soviet
territory and had started the fighting. A second clash
took place on April ll near Huilin on an island in the
Ussuri River between twenty Japanese and fifty Soviet
soldiers, according to the Japanese version. No progress
was made toward concluding a new treaty to regulate fishing
in northern Japanese and Soviet waters to take effect
after the expiration of the present treaty on May 27.
However, both the Soviet and Japanese newspapers professed
optimism over the chances of reaching at least a temporary
agreement

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
agreement before the date of the treaty's expiration.
Mr. Arita, the Japanese Minister for Foreign Affairs,
told foreign press correspondents on April 25 that he
did not believe that the alarming rumors concerning the
"Manchukuoan" border situation would lead to war but that
it would be helpful if the USSR would decrease its border
armaments. In reply to questions, Mr. Arita also said
that Japan did not think that the time had yet arrived
for the conclusion of the non-aggression pact which the
USSR had asked for some time ago.
In conclusion, it may be said that the "independent
and positive foreign policy" of the new Japanese Cabinet
appeared at the end of the month to have made no very great
progress in settling outstanding Soviet-Japanese issues.*
(b). China.
There seemed to be some uncertainty during the month
as to whether Mr. Hirota's three-point policy toward China**
would be carried out in the near future by Mr. Arita,
former Minister to China, who was appointed Minister for
Foreign Affairs on April 2. For example, a Domei despatch
from Nanking dated April 4 renorted that Mr. Arita had been
induced by the Chinese to give up the three-point policy
and the OSAKA MAIINICHI of April 10 stated that the Minister
for Foreign Affairs had decided to relinquish this policy
in favor of the following program: the conclusion of
economic and reciprocal tariff agreements with China
and the formation of an economic bloc among Japan, China,
and 'Manchukuo" in lieu of pressure on China to secure
the recognition of "Manchukuo".
Contrary to such
reports, the Minister for Foreign Affairs told Tokyo
newspaper

to follow Mr. Hirota's three principles in adjusting

On the other hand, there appeared to be no weakening
in the attitude of the Japanese military in China,
judging from remarks attributed by the ASAHI of

Attaché to the Japanese Embassy in China, who left
Japan for his post on that day. Major General Kita
was quoted as having said that he would endeavor to
have China abandon its futile dependence on Europe
and the United States and to rectify its mistaken
notions about Japan; that he would urge the Chinese
to cooperate with Japan in eliminating warlike Communist
influence from the Far East; that China could have no
objections to Japan's three-point nolicy; and that Japan
could not consider economic assistance or any other
form of aid to China unless China could be persuaded
to define clearly its attitude toward Japan.
A good-will mission from the "East Hopei Anti-
Communist Autonomous Government" to "Manchukuo" arrived
in Hsinking on April 14. It was reported that the
purpose of this mission was to initiate negotiations
for the conclusion of a pact for mutual aid in com-
batting Communism and for mutual economic development.
Mr. Hsu Shih-ying, the new Chinese Ambassador to
Japan, presented his credentials to the Emperor on
April 6. The Japanese Government reportedly decided
to appoint Mr. Shigeru Kawagoe, who had served since

whom he had been in close contact in Tientsin and
previously in Hsinking.

industries in "Manchukuo" and such a policy is significant
in that the former Finance Minister was disinclined to
extend Government aid for Manchurian investments.

In a conversation with the Counsellor of the Embassy

It was reported by the OSAKA MAINICHI of April 19

Amazon River Valley. According to this newspaper, Mr.
Shinji Tazaki, President of the Kobe University of

the group of emigrants on a six months' tour of South

(i). Ethiopia.
WE
At a press conference on April 17 the spokesman of
the Foreign Office stated that in the event of Italian
subjugation of Ethiopia Japan would act independently to
protect its rights and interests in that region, pointing
out that Japan had an agreement of friendship and trade
with Ethiopia and that commerce between the two countries
had been increasing recently.
Mt. Ato Daba Biru, soi-disant diplomatic negotiator
for Ethiopia, who had been in Japan for almost seven
months, sailed for his homeland on April 2.*
(j). Australia.

were reported by the press to be considering the advisa-

Australia on account of the latter's plan to increase the
duty on Japanese rayon textiles and cotton cloth.
The
Japanese Government was said to be indignant over the
stand taken by Austrelia, especially in view of the

(k). The Dominican Republic.
LA
Mr. Manuel G. Soriano, the first Consul General of
the Dominican Republic to be stationed in Japan, arrived
at his post in Yokohama on April 7. According to the
JAPAN ADVERTISER of April 8, Mr. Soriano stated that the
principal object of his mission in this country was to
increase the trade between the Dominican Republic and
Japan, which had greatly improved in recent years, his
country having imported Japanese products to the value
of ¥3,749,982 and Japan having imported Dominican products
worth ¥3,742,509 in the year 1935.
(1). Siam.
The Japanese economic mission headed by Mr. Yunosuke
Yasukawa, which left Tokyo on March 13 for Siam, departed
from that country on April 2l. In a radio telephone
interview from Batavia with the Tokyo ASAHI on April 22,
Mr. Yasukawa was reported to have made the following
statements: that the Siamese are friendly toward Japan
but apparently wish to avoid any excessive cordiality
because of the closeness of their relations with Great
Britain and France; that before his visit to Siam he had
been under the impression that the balance of trade in
favor of Siam was about ¥35,000,000 but that he had dis-
covered that this balance was only ¥4,000,000 or ¥5,000,-
oo0; and that Japanese sundry goods almost monopolized
the Siamese market.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
In the wake of the February 26 incident, a number
of developments took place during the month of April
894.002
which affected the Cabinet. On April 10 the Cabinet
decided to abolish the Cabinet Deliberative Body, which
was organized under the Okada Government and had been
closely associated with the senior statesmen and was
therefore deemed objectionable, in the new order of
things.* It was reported that a plan had been proposed
to reduce the number of Ministers of the various Depart-
ments from the present thirteen to seven and to make them
all Ministers without Portfolio, directly responsible to
the Emperor and entrusted with the formulation of policies
which would be executed by the Ministers with Portfolio.
This proposal met with opposition in the Cabinet, probably
on account of its similarity to the defunct Cabinet
Deliberative Body.**
Mr. Hachiro Arita, Ambassador to China, was formally
installed on April 2 as Minister for Foreign Affairs in
place of Premier Hirota, who had held this post since the
formation of the new Cabinet on March 10. On April 10
Mr. Mamoru Shigemitsu resigned from his post of Vice
Minister for Foreign Affairs and was placed on the waiting
list. Although Mr. Shigemitsu had previously been known
as a friend of the military, the press reported that one
of the reasons for his resignation was the Army's opposition
to him. He was succeeded as Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs by Mr. Kensuke Horinouchi, formerly Chief of the
American
Embassy's despatch No. 1735 of March 19, 1936.
Embassy's despatch No. 1784 of April 17, 1936.

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
American Affairs Bureau of the Foreign Office.
At its meeting on April 10, the Cabinet decided
to reprimand the following officials for their con-
nection with the February 26 incident: Kazuo Oguri,
former Chief of the Metropolitan Police Board; Toshiki
Karasawa, former Director of the Folice Bureau of the
Home Office; Sei Homma, Chief of the Police Affairs
Department of the Metropolitan Police Board; and Katsu-
roku Aikawa, Chief of the Peace Maintenance Section of
the Home Office. In keeping with the announced policy
of the new Government to clarify the national polity,
the new Minister of Education set forth his views as
follows to a representative of the JAPAN ADVERTISER on
April 29: Japan has for a number of years been adapting
to its needs the modern arts and sciences of the Occident;
in this process greater emphasis has been placed on the
materialistic phase of culture to the detriment of the
spiritual side; Japan is therefore suffering at present
from "unbalance", a situation which must be corrected
through the "humanization of education".
On April 24, the Cabinet decided that meetings
would be held by the Ministers for Foreign Affairs, War,
and Navy every Friday; that discussion would not be
limited at these meetings to specific topics but would
embrace everything releting to diplomacy and national
defense; and that the ultimate purpose of the meetings
would be to attain understanding among the three ministers
concerned for the formulation of national policies. The
Minister for Foreign Affairs explained in this connection
that the purpose of such meetings was the unification of
Japan's foreign policy through complete coordination
between the Foreign Office and the defense services.
(b).

=== Page 47 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15-
(b). The Army.
Army officials conducted investigations for the
894.20
purpose of determining the identity of and eliminating
all officers known to advocate direct action methods.
It appeared that the moderate elements in the Army were
in control and were making an effort to decrease the
possibility of further incidents such as those of May 15,
1932, and February 26, 1936. A rumor was current during
the month to the effect that the intemperate element in
the Army was discontented with this state of affairs and
was determined to cause more trouble.* At the conference
of garrison and division commanders held on April 8,
General Terauchi, the Minister for War, emphasized the
need of enforcing military discipline and at the same
time edmitted that the clarification of the national
polity and augmentation of armaments were equally im-
portant. Although largely responsible for putting down
the movement of insurgent officers at the end of February,
Lieutenant General Kohei Kashii, Commander of the Tokyo
Garrison and the Martial Law Headquarters, was relieved
of hi s posts and placed on the waiting list on April 2
because of his formal responsibility for the February 26
incident.
There was much discussion by the press during the
month concerning the necessity for an increase in Army
personnel, an improvement in the air force, and augmenta-
tion of the defense forces in "Manchukuo" and North China.
(c). The Navy.
It became known that the Navy had under consideration
794.3
the
* Embassy's despatch No. 1784 of April 17, 1936.

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.98)
-16-
the establishment of a "Navy Organization Investigation
Council", the functions of which would be to draw up a
new naval defense policy, to suggest structural reforms
in Navy organization, and to compile recommendations for
submission to the Government.* According to newspaper
reports, this new organization will establish a policy
of "home defense combined with an advance southward".
It is said that the "advance to the south" will be peace-
ful and economic in nature.** It was reported that the
Navy Ministry was opposed to the idea advocated by certain
Government officials of abolishing the South Seas Govern-
ment and placing the Japanese Mandated Islands under the
administration of the Formosan Government, because the
South Seas Government should be strengthened to promote
the Navy's policy of southward advance.
(a). Political Parties.
Reportcdly, the Shakai Taisuto (Social Mass Party)
was critical of the attempts being made to reform the
House of Peers on the ground that the reform movement
was prompted by insincere motives and was a make-believe.
to divert the attention of the people from the urgent
necessity for reform in the social and economic system.***
The peculiar character of this so-called proletarian
party is illustrated by the report that the party advocates
the payment by the Government of yl8o.00 annually to the
families of enlisted men in the Army in order to stabilize
social conditions during the present period of tension
over the Soviet-"Manchukuo" border and general labor
conditions.

On April 22, the Minseito Party announced its policies
as follows: (1) establishment of an independent foreign
policy and peace in the Far East; (2) formulation of a
national defense program for the maintenance of peace in
the Far East; (3) stabilization of the "living of the
masses"; (4) reformation of financial and taxation policies;
(5) establishment of a new parliamentary system to meet
all the national needs; (6) fundamental reorganization of
central and local administrative systems; (7) reformation
of the judicial system, and (8) a reorganization of the
system of education to make it more practical.
The press reported that a section of the Seiyukai
Party desired the resignation of its President, Dr. Kisa-
buro Suzuki, on account of his alleged responsibility for
the party's loss of popular support, as evidenced by the
general election of February 20. However, Dr. Suzuki
was said to be adamant in his determination to retain the
presidency of the Seiyukai.
(e). The Diet.
Premier Hirota told newspaper correspondents on
April 15 that he expected a quiet and peaceful session of
the Diet to be opened on May 1 for the reason that the
present Cabinet represented all the major political parties.
The newspapers stated that for the purpose of allaying
public unrest the Government would guarantee freedom of
speech at the coming extraordinary session of the Diet
despite the fact that the District of Tokyo would still
be under martial law. The representation of the various
political parties in the House of Representatives, as
reported by the OSAKA MAINICHI of April 28, will be as
follows:

(f). Reactionaries.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of April 10, the
Home Office has decided to increase the political police
force assigned to watch the activities of reactionaries
as a means of preventing the recurrence of such outbreaks
as that of February 26.
(g).
. The Throne.
The JAPAN CHRONICLE of April 22 stated that Admiral
Suzuki, Court Chamberlain, having recovered from the
wounds that he received at the hands of the February 26
insurgents, had resumed his official duties.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of April 22, the
Home Office has drafted an employee pension bill which
would apply to all factories and mines that cmploy more
than thirty persons and which provides for the establish-
ment of retirement allowance funds by contributions from
both workers and cmployees. The same newspaper reported
that the Communications Ministry would submit at the
coming extraordinary Diet session a shipping service
control bill providing that when the Government recognizes
the existence of ruinous competition in a steamship
service it will have the authority to advise thc interested
parties to stop the competition and in the event of refusal
the Government will be empowered to prohibit navigation.
V.

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.98)
- 19 -
V.
GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
On April 6 the Bank of Japan and the semi-official
banks lowered the discount rate on commercial bills and
894.5161
the interest rates on loans and overdrafts by an average
of about .37% annually. The Finunce Minister stated that
this was the first step toward the general lowering of
interest rates in Japan. The Governor of the Bank of
Japan explained that another reason for the step was to
make the interest rate on Government bonds more attractive
than those of bank deposits and in this way to create a
market for future issues of Government bonds. Indications
13202
became even clearer during the month that Mr. Baba, the
Finance Minister, would abandon the Takahashi policy of
gradually reducing Government bond issues. On April 18
the Finance Minister converted a quantity of 5% Government
bonds into 3.5% bonds,* this move having been made possible
by the prior lowering of the discount rate on commercial
bills and the interest rates on loans and overdrafts by
the Bank of Japan and the semi-official banks.
In an interview on April 6 with a correspondent of
the ASAHI, the Finance Minister was quoted as having said
that he doubted whether he would be able to give an ex-
planation of taxation readjustment at the coming Diet
session and that his taxation program would be completed
by next October. This newspaper stated that the Finance
Minister hoped to have taxation measures ready to be
applied in the fiscal year 1937-1938.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANC IAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
April exports amounted to ¥205,295,000 and imports
were
Embassy's despatch No. 1784 of April 17, 1936.

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.97)
- 20 -
were ¥247,057,000, as compared with exports for March of
¥215,473,000 and imports of ¥276,019,000. Thus the
unfavorable balance of trade decreased from ¥60,546,000
for March to ¥41,762,000 for the month under review.*
Mr. Shikao Matsushima was appointed to the post of
Chief of the Commercial Affairs Bureau of the Foreign
Office to fill the vacancy caused by the appointment of
Mr. Kurusu on April 10 as Ambassador to Belgium. As
reported by the JAPAN TIMES of April 21, in a speech given
before the Foreign Trade Association of Japan, Mr. Matsu-
shima emphatically stated that Japan would retaliate
22m220
against unfair restrictions on Japanese commodities by
any nation. The significance of this remark was said to
lie in the present troubled trade negotiations with
Australia, Egypt, India, and the Netherland Indies.
(b). Industries.
According to the JAPAN TIMES of April 23, at a
meeting in Osaka on April 22, the Japan Rayon Association
decided to establish strict control of rayon production
with a view to having equilibrium between the demand for
and the supply of rayon yarns. It was stated that the
principal reasons for the adoption of this measure by
the Japan Rayon Association were the preaviling low
prices of rayon yarns and Australia's plan to raise tariff
rates and to adopt import restrictions on Japanese rayon.
(o). Aviation.
It was reported that military circles were advocating
Government management and operation of all major branches
of the aviation industry, or, failing that, strict control
1n
* Figures furnished by the Commercial Attaché's Office.

- 21 -
in order that Japan's civil aviation may be up to scratch
in the event of war.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Cotton Textiles.
The announcement on May 2l by the United States
Government of increased duties on certain cotton imports
into the United States brought forth editorial comment
in the Japanese press much less acrimonious in tone than
the newspaper criticisms directed against the Australian
increase in the duties on cotton and rayon textiles.*
With regard to the United States, the Japanese press
generally ascribed the increased duties to pressure on
the American Government by New England textile interests.
The increase in the American tariff seemed to be looked
upon in this country as a relatively minor matter, since
Japan's cotton textile exports to the United States amount
to less than 2 per cent of its total exports of this com-
modity. However, the Jananese manufacturers appeared to
feel that they had a claim to consideration from the
United States because of the considerable excess of
Japanese purchases from the United tates over Japanese
exports to that country. For example, two leading Japanese
manufacturers of cotton textiles were quoted by the OSAKA
MAINICHI of May 23 as having saii that the American atti-
tude was unreasonable in view of Japan's large purchases
from the United States. On May 22 the Foreign Office
spokesman expressed his personal regret over the step
taken by the United States from the point of view of
the principle of free trade and the constantly improving
commercial relations between Japan and the United States.
According to the JAFAN TIMES of May 23, certain Japanese
manufacturers held the view that the American action had
been
Embassy's telegram No. 113 of May 25, 1936, and Embassy's
despatch No. 1861 of May 28, 1936.

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
been caused by the "weak diplomatic policy" of the
Japanese Government and that the Trade Protection Law
should be invoked against the United States.
(b).
American Approach to the Japanese Government
regarding Smuggling in North China.
Acting under instructions from the Department, the
Ambassador called on the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
Su
on May 13 and made the following oral and informal approach
regarding smuggling in North China: reports supported by
substantial evidence have stated that Japanese subjects
have taken a prominent part in such operations, that there
has been little evidence of a disposition on the part of
the Japanese to curb these activities, that Japanese
authorities in North China have obstructed the enforcement
of preventative measures by the Chinese, and that the
Eastern Hopei "autonomous" régime has reduced import duties
in an arbitrary fashion and has permitted the goods con-
cerned to enter China for general commercial purposes.
The Ambassador also stated that the American Government
was concerned not only with the deleterious effect of
this smuggling on American imports into China but also
with the fact that the impairment of the Chinese Government's
income from customs revenue tended to endanger that Govern-
ment's capacity to fulfill outstanding financial obliga-
tions to American citizens.* Similar and parallel repre-
sentations, also informal, had been made previously by
the British Ambassador on behalf of his Government. In
the case of both approaches the replies of the Vice
Minister for Foreign Affairs and of the Minister for
Foreign Affairs were specious and illogical, since these
officials
* Embassy's telegram No. 94 of May 6, Department's telegram
No. 52 of May 8, and Embassy's telegram No. 104 of May 13,
1936.

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
officials attempted to blame the smuggling on "unreasonably
high tariffs" prevailing in China and on the lack of zeal
of the local Chinese authorities to ensure the collection
of customs duties and asserted that. the Japanese Government
was unable to control smuggling for these reasons, although
Japanese merchants were as much concerned with the adverse
effects of such operations on their business as the British
and American merchants.* The fact that Japan actually
controlled the Eastern Hopei "autonomous" régime never
entered into the picture, obviously because the Japanese
Government had consistently maintained that this régime
was purely autonomous. Toward the end of the month the
Foreign Office evinced an interest in the American Govern-
ment's concern over the smufgling in North China yet gave
no promise whatsoever of attempting to put a stop to an
easily eradicable situation.** In fact, the Foreign Office
feigned indignation over the imputation of any political
motive on the part of the Japanese Government in connection
with the North China smuggling.***
(c). Automobile Industry Control Law.
The Automobile Industry Control Bill was approved by
both Houses of the Diet. As already reported to the
Department,
this law will permit the American automobile
assembling plants in this country (the Ford Motor Company
and General Motors, Japan) to continue operations up to
the limit of their production on August 9, 1935, but not
to enlarge their business in Janan.

(d). American Oil Interests.
On May l6 the representatives of the American and
British oil companies presented the following plan to Mr.
Yoshino, Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry, concern-
ing cooperation between the Standard-Vacuum Oil Company
and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company on the one hand and
the Mitsui interests on the other: Mitsui would carry
all the non-commercial oil stocks required by the Japanese
Government; in return Mitsui would receive the entire
compensation paid by the Governnent for stock-keeping,
as well as some share of the foreign oil companies' business
in Japan; and the oil companies would make a permanent
supply contract with Mitsui, in return for which they would
ask that the present volune quotas granted by the Japanese
Government be changed to percentage quotas. In their
conversation with Mr. Yoshino the oil company representatives
implied that unless some arrangement of the sort could be
reached the companies would be compelled sooner or later
to withdraw from Japan, to which Mr. Yoshino replied that
the Japanese Government wished the foreign oil companies
to remain in Japan, promising that he would endeavor to
induce Mitsui to expedite matters so that some arrange-
ment could be reached before July 1, 1936 (the date on
which the oil companies are required to possess six
months' stocks in Japan). On the same day the American
and British oil company representatives interviewed one
of the managing directors of the Mitsui interests along
similar lines. The Mitsui representative received the
proposals favorably.* Although negotiations were
continued
* Embassy's despatch No. 1860 of May 29, 1936.

continued toward the close of the month no definite
agreement was reached.
(e).
Problem of Land Leases Held by Japanese in
Davao Province, Philippine Islands.
In a long interpellation in the House of Peers on
May ll Mr. Korekiyo Otsuka asked the Minister for Foreign
Affairs what action would be taken to solve the Davao
land problem.* Mr. Arita replied that he believed that
the problem would be solved satisfactorily, since Presi-
dent Quezon understood that the Japanese residents in
Davao had only followed the local custom in cultivating
the land in that province. Questioned by foreign news-
paper correspondents on May 22, the Foreign Office spokes-
man stated that the Japanese Government did not consider
itself at present to be in a position to take up the
Davao question with the American authorities, that con-
versations were in progress in the Philippines between the
Japanese Consul General there and the authorities of the
Commonwealth, and that the Japanese Government expected
that the matter would come to a mutually satisfactory
settlement based upon justice and equity.

Admiral Orin G. Murfin, successor to Admiral Frank B.
Upham as Commander-in-Chief of the United States Asiatic
Fleet, arrived at the port of Yokohama on May 25 aboard
his flagship, the U. S. S. AUGUSTA, for an official visit
of eleven days. There were the usual entertainments for
a visiting Commander-in-Chief of the United States Asiatic
Fleet, including an informal dinner and an official luncheon
at

Visit to Japan of the Representatives of

An unofficial mission comnosed of eight members of

organization, who arrived in Japan on April 2l,* remained
in this country until May 16. They were widely and hos-
pitably entertained and in return appeared to have created
a favorable impression among the Japanese.**
II.
RELATIONS WITII OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
79319
After the comparative lull that followed the February
26 incident, Sino-Japanese relations again became tense
during the month of May, principally due to Japan's part
in the large scale smuggling operations in the East Hopei
region and to the decision of the Japanese Jar Office on
May 1 5 to increase the Japanese North China garrison.
China protested ineffectually against Japanese hampering
of the Chinese customs enforcement and it was reported
at the end of the month that the Chinese Government was
going to protest against the increase in Japan's garrison
in Tientsin.
(1). Smuggling in East Hopei.
At a press conference on May 15 the spokesman of
the
*
Embassy's Monthly Report for April.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1845 of May 15, 1936.

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
the Japanese Foreign Office asserted that the Japanese
Government was doing everything possible to prevent
smuggling in North China, emphatically denied that the
smuggling was due in any way to the Tangku Truce or the
Ho Ying-chin-Umezu agreement, and also denied any know-
ledge on the part of the Foreign Office of pressure being
exerted by the Japanese military authorities against
Chinese customs officials. Newspaper correspondents who
were present at the conference stated that Mr. Amau had
been ill at east throughout the interview and had dis-
played both hesitation and irritation when questioned.*
According to the press, on May 15 the Nanking Govern-
ment filed with the Japanese Government a second repre-
sentation regarding the smuggling in North China, charging
that the Japanese authorities in that region had made it
impossible for the Chinese customs authorities to take
effective measures to check the smu gling, asserting that
the Japanese were conniving at smuggling operations by
taking advantage of Japan's extraterritorial rights in
China, and requesting the Tokyo Government to put a stop
to intervention oy Japanese and to take effective measures
to check Japanese and Korean smugelers. The protest also
reportedly expressed dissatisfaction at Japan's failure
to send any reply to the Chinese Government's first
representation against smuggling. Apparently the Japanese
Foreign Office remained indifferent to the Chinese repre-
sentations, to all practical intents and purposes.
(2).
Increase in the Japanese North China
Garrison.
On May 15 the War Office formally announced that the
Japanese

Japanese North China garrison would be increased.
According to the estimate of the Military Attaché's

(3). Statements by Japanese Officials.
Upon landine at Moji on May 7, Mr. Kawagoe, Japanese
Consul General at Tientsin and Ambassador-designate to
China, was credited with the following statement by the
OSAKA MIANICHI of May 8: "The logical conclusion of the
current Chinese situation, political and economic, is
that China's only way out of her present difficulties
lies in marching arm in arm with Japan." Mr. Kawagoe's
formal appointment as Ambassador to China took place on
May
15.
Answering an interpellation in the House of Peers on
May 17, Premier Hirota stated that encouraging results
had not attended "Japan's amicable and discreet policy
toward the Soviet Union and China" but that in time China
might come to appreciate Japan's attitude. A far more
emphatic and significant statement was said to have been
made
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1865 of May 28, 1936.

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.98)

made by Mr. Suma, the Japanese Consul General at Nanking,
in an interview with newspaper correspondents at Kobe on

Suma had stated that he had told Chiang Kai-shek that "China

fresh military action in China.
(b). Soviet Russia.
761.
No border clashes occurred during the month to increase
the already existing tension between the two countries,
only a few minor incidents taking place. The principal
developments in Soviet-Japanese relations were the decision
of the War Office to increase the strength of the Kwantung
Army, the conclusion of a provisional fisheries agreement
on May 25, and the filing by the Soviet Foreign Office with
the Japanese Ambassador at Moscow of a protest against the
arrest and "persecution" of the Japanese employees of the
Soviet Embassy in Tokyo.**
There had for some time been under consideration by
the War Office a plan for strengthening the Kwantung Army
in view of the large number of Soviet troops on the border
of "Manchukuo". In a speech delivered on May 12 the War
Minister reminded the members of the Diet that the USSR
was
*
Embassy's telegram No. 117 of May 30, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1866 of May 29, 1936.

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10-
was actively continuing its policy of extending communism
throughout the world; that upon the completion of the
double tracking of the trans-Siberian Railway the Soviet
forces in East Asia might soon be increased to 300,000 men,
a force greater than the entire standing Japanese Army;
and that attention must be given to the rapid increase of
the number of Soviet submarines at Vladivostok and of
Soviet airplanes in the Maritime Province. According to
press reports the War Office finally came to a decision
to increase the Kwantung Army's forces beginning in the
early part of l937 and also to promote the emigration of
large numbers of Japanese settlers to "Manchukuo" with the
idea of eventually obtaining military reinforcements from
them.*
The Japanese Ambassador at Moscow was reported to hawe
succeeded on May 25 in negotiating a provisional extension
of the existing fisheries treety with the Soviet Union
until the end of the present calendar year.**
According to news despatches from Moscow, the Soviet
Vice Commissar for Foreign Affairs protested to the Japanese
Ambassador against the "persecution" of the Japanese em-
ployees of the Soviet Embassy in Tokyo who had been arrested
on charges of espionage, insisting that the arrested trans-
lators had merely been doing their legitimate work.
