(2)
British Intention to Increase its
Navy's Destroyer Strength.
During the first part of May the Japanese Government
received from the British Government two memoranda which
set forth the latter's desire to increase its Navy's
destroyer strength from 150,000 to 190,000 tons and to
convert three Class-A cruisers into Class-B ships and
one Class-A cruiser into a training ship, thereby gaining
a legal right to construct four new Class-A cruisers.
According to press despatches, on May 26 the Japanese
Chargé d'Affaires in London, acting under instructions,
delivered a somewhat vague renly from the Japanese
Government to the British naval memoranda to the effect
that Japan had no objection to Great Britain's proposed
increase of destroyer strength by the cessation of the
scrapping obligation but that if Great Britain were allowed
to do so the other Signatory Powers should also be granted
the right to waive the scrapping obligations and make
similar increases.*** The reference in this case is
probably to Japan's intention not to scrap over-age
destroyers and submarines, which this country is required
to do by the end of the year 1936, in accordance with the
provisions of the Washington and London Naval Treaties.
While firmly resolved to retain its over-age auxiliary
craft

(3)
Departure for London of Mr. Shigeru
Yoshida, Newly Appointed Ambassador
to Great Britain.
Mr. Shigeru Yoshida, who was appointed Ambassador
to Great Britain on April 10, 1936, left Japan for London
on May 2l. An interesting report published by the NICHI
NICHI of May 19, which the Embassy has been unable to
confirm, stated that Mr. Yoshida carried with him the
following four-point program which the Foreign Office
had instructed him to communicate to the British Govern-
ment upon his arrival in London: (1) Japan will respect
British rights and interests in China in return for
recognition of its position on the Asiatic continent;
(2) Tokyo is prepared to conclude parallel naval limita-
tion treaties with London and Washington; (3) negotiations
with Great Britain and its dominions on wholesale readjust-
ment of trade relations are desired; and (4) Japan will
make its "real intentions" known to Great Britain so that
they may be relayed to other countries. Mr. Amau, the
Foreign Office spokesman, told newspaper correspondents
on May 20 that the new Ambassador to London took with
him no special instructions.
(a). "Manchukuo".
According to a Hsinking news despatch dated May 9
from the staff correspondent of the OSAKA MAINICHI, the
Central Bank of "Manchukuo" announced on that day a
reduction of its deposit and loan-advancing interest
rates

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)

rates, probably with the idea of being in conformity with
the trend of the Baba finance policy of Japan.
(e). A1stralia.
The increase in cotton and rayon textile duties in
Australia was bitterly criticized by the Japanese press.*
This acrimonious press comment can be partly explained
by the fact that Australian imports from Japan have amounted
in recent years to less than one-third of Japanese imports
from Australia. On the other hand, in 1935, for example,
Japan took over lo% of Australia's total imports, accord-
ing to figures furnished the Embassy by the Australian
Trade Commissioner in Tokyo. The Australian Trade Com-
missioner set forth his Government's point of view in a
statement published by the JAPAN TIMES of May 27, pointing
out that the new Australian duties were not aimed at Japan
as they applied to all foreign nations alike. It did not
seem likely that the Japanese would be pleased with this
statement inasmuch as they appeared to believe that the
new Australian tariff had been inspired by the British
Government and to fear that the same tactics affecting
Japanese exports would be adopted in other British dominions
and regions under British influence.
While the vernacular newspapers reported that the
Japanese Government would invoke the Trade Protection Law
against Australia, the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
told the Counselor of the Embassy on May 27 that his
Government had not yet come to a decision on the exact
measures to be adopted. The press reported that the
Japan Industrial Woollen Association had decided to
decrease annual purchases of Australian wool and to
curtail the production of woollen textiles in Japan.

(r). Germany.
On May 1 the Embassy of "Manchukuo" in Tokyo
announced the conclusion of a German-"Manchukuo" trade

conducted by Japan, "Manchukuo", and the German Economic

According to the OSAKA MAINICHI of May 14, on the

in Ethiopia and added that the call to be made by Mr.
Suzuki would be informal and would in no way signify the
Japanese Government's recognition of Italy's annexation
of Ethiopia.
(h). The Union of South Africa.
The JAPAN ADVERTISER of May 23 reported that the
Government of the Union of South Africa had decided to
send a trade mission to Japan in June and that Japanese
business men were enthusiastic over this decision, parti-
cularly in view of the strained commercial relations
between Australia and Japan and the latter's decision to
buy wool elsewhere, possibly from the Union of South Africa.

(i). Latin America.
Various Japanese newspapers reported that the Japanese
private commercial mission sent to Central and South
America in December, 1935, for the purpose of makine
trade agreements with Latin American countries had failed
to accomplish anything of importance.* The Japanese
press seemed to be of the opinion that the greatest
obstacle to the adjustment of trade relations with Latin
American countries was the prior conclusion of reciprocal
trade agreements by the United States with those countries.
The press in this country apparently ignored the fact that
Japan had not evinced any willingness to agree to purchase
enough products from the various Latin American countries
to make commercial treaties profitable for the latter.
(j). International Naval Issue.
Replying to an interpellation by members of the Diet
Budget Committee on May 13, the Navy Minister reportedly
expressed Japan's decision to "lead the world" in attempting
to bring about another arms limitation conference, yet at
the same time Admiral Nagano asserted that there would
be no change in the Japanese thesis of "non-menace and
non-aggression" and parity among Japan, Great Britain, and
the United States (a thesis that had proved to be un-
acceptable to Great Britain and the United States at the
last Naval Conference)**. While there are indications
that Japan might perhaps be disposed to be more conciliatory
in its naval stand than previously, such indications
were almost completely nullified by Admiral Nagano's
assertion and by the pamphlet recently issued by the
Navy
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1848 of May 15, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1874 of May 30, 1936.

=== Page 76 === (confidence 1.00)
-16-
Navy Department entitled "The International Situation
and the Japanese Navy with Special Reference to the
Results of the Russo-Japanese War", which reiterated
Japan's insistence upon naval parity and stressed the
need for Japan to build a navy strong enough to protect
it from aggression and to continue in its role of "peace-
maintainer" in the Far East in the face of the proposed
naval building plans of the United States and Great
Britain. There was evident during the month a certain
amount of apprehension in naval circles over the danger
to Japan of a future race in naval construction. However,
it was not clear what line of action the Japanese would
take to remedy a situation arising out of future naval
competition.
III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Diet.
The 69th Session of the Diet opened on May 1 and
was formally closed on May 27. As already reported to
the Department*, Diet members were unusually frank and
bold in criticizing both the Army and the Government.
There were dignified and well-founded attacks on the
prevailine chauvinism, notably the speech delivered by
Mr. Takao Saito on May 7.** The Army was pertinently
charged with responsibility for the February 26 assassina-
tions and the Government's lack of concrete measures for
the social reforms promised by it came in for considerable
criticism.
Judging from the character of the Diet interpella-
tions and the statements issued by the five principal
political
*
Embassy's despatches No. 1830 of May 14, 1936, and
No. 1869 of May 29, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1826 of May 13, 1936.

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17 -
political parties of the Lower House after the close of
the Diet session, it would appear that the action of
the political parties in unhesitatingly criticizing the
Government and the Army and at the same time passing the
Government's bills was prompted by a genuine desire for
general economic and social reform and for purging the
Army, coupled with a willingness to give the newly formed
Government time to carry out its announced program in the
future.
(b).
Aftermath of the February 26 Incident.
At a conference held on May 2l, Mr. Jiro Mitsuyuki,
the Procurator General, told gendarmerie chiefs assembled
from all over Japan that there must be no toleration of
thoughts or deeds in the realm of patriotism that ignored
the prestige and the laws of the nation and that there
must be no relaxation in criminal administration. In an
interpellation in the House of Representatives on May 21,
Mr. Genkuro Eto, Independent, asked the Government why
the February 26 offenders were not allowed the benefit
of counsel in their court martial and why an appeal would
be denied them, to which the War Minister replied that
the stipulations for the present court martial had been
determined by the nature of the case and the circumstances
prevailing. The ASAHI of May 25 reported that Army
leaders were in the process of preparing a set of pro-
posals for the renovation of Japan's administrative
structure, inasmuch as they were dissatisfied with the
progress made thus far for the attainment of sweeping
reforms needed to tide over the delicate aftermath of
the February 26 incident. The Police Bureau of the Home
Office was reported to be planning the expansion of its
detective

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.99)
- 18 -
detective and special political forces to meet conditions
that have resulted from the February 26 incident.
(c). Army and Navy.
Two Imperial Ordinances were published in the OFFICIAL
GAZETTE of May 18 and May 19, respectively, amending the
regulations with regard to the organization of the Army
and Navy Ministries and providing that appointment to the
posts of Minister and Vice Minister of the Army and the
Navy shall be confined to generals and admirals on the
active list.*
According to the press, the total budget of the
Navy Ministry for the fiscal year 1936-1937 has been
approved at ¥685,789,000, which represents a record for
Japan and an increase of ¥155,106,000 over the 1935-1936
budget and is more than three times the naval budget of
five years ago.**
(d). Japanese Foreign Service.
The newspapers of May 29 reported that Mr. Suemasa
Okamoto, formerly in charge of the Bureau of Accounts of
the Foreign Office, had been appointed Chief of the
American Section and that Mr. Kano Shiozaki, formerly of
the American Bureau of the Foreign Office, had been ap-
pointed Consul General at San Francisco.
According to the Japan CIHRONICLE of May 3O, the
Foreign Office has decided upon a plan to extend the
scope of its Intelligence Bureau, including the appointment
of press attachés to embassies and legations abroad, the
establishment of a bureau in the Foreign Office for
increased contact with foreign publicists interested in
Far Eastern problems, and the establishment of an organiza-
tion
* Embassy's despatch No. 1864 of May 38, 1936.
**Embassy's despatch No. 1874 of May 30, 1936.

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
- 19 -
tion which will act in conjunction with other Depart-
ments to deal more effectually with espionage cases.
(e). Reactionaries.
The JAPAN TIMES of May 28 reported that ten former
military cadets convicted for complicity in the May 15,
1932, Affair would be discharged from prison on June 1
upon the expiration of their sentences. Originally these
men were sentenced by court martial to imprisonment for
four years but their sentences were reduced to two years
and eight months by virtue of an Imperial amnesty granted
on February 11, 1934.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Forty-three of the forty-five bills passed by the
Diet were measures that had been drafted by the Okada
Cabinet, the other two drafted by the Hirota Cabinet
being the much-discussed bills for the control of
seditious publications* and the establishment of retire-
ment allowance funds for emnloyees by their employers
(the only important social measure presented in the
Diet). The present Government's bill for the protection
of national mobilization secrets failed of passage. Other
important bills were the following:
the Government's
general budget bill amounting to ¥2,310,529,000 for the
fiscal year 1936-1937, which represents an increase of
¥28,000,000 over the 1936-1937 budget drafted by former
Finance Minister Takahashi and an increase of ¥95,1l5,000
over the 1935-1936 budget; a bil1 for the control of the
automobile industry in Japan by the establishment of a
license system under which new foreien automobile firms
shall not be allowed to operate in Japan and already
existing foreign firms shall not be allowed to increase
their
Embassy's despatch No. 1876 of May 29, 1936.

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
their businesses unless Japanese subjects are admitted
to ownership of a majority of the stock; a bill pro-
viding for Government control of shipping in the event
二
of the existence of ruinous competition; three rice
control bills; and a bill for the control of exports
of cotton textiles, silk and rayon textiles, porcelain,
rubber shoes, matches, electric lamps, and other com-
modities.
GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
In answering an interpellation in the Lower House
Budget Committee during the first part of the month,
the Finance Minister reiterated his intention of revising
the system of taxation and stated that new taxes on the
masses would be avoided as far as possible but that an
excise tax might be imposed on special articles or luxury
goods. According to the YOMIURI of May 12, a bill will
be introduced in the next session of the Diet providing
for tax revision which will result in an increase of
from ¥400,000,000 t0 ¥500,000,000 in Government revenue.
On May 18 the Finance Minister repeated that he would
refrain from imposing taxes that would threaten the
livelihood of the poorer classes and that he would also
avoid taxing the necessities of life.
VI.
GENERAL EC ONOMIC AND FINANC IAL CONDITIONS.
(a)
Foreign Trade.
Japan's May exports were ¥222,628,000 while its
imports were ¥244,305,000, as compared with April exports
of ¥205,295,000 and imports of ¥247,057,000. The un-
favorable balance of trade thus decreased from ¥4l,762,000
for April to ¥21,677,000 for the month of May.*
(b).
* Statistics and information furnished by the
Commercial Attaché's office.

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.97)
-21-
(b). Financial Trends.*
The stock market, thoueh not active, was slightly
stronger than during the previous month; the bond market
was dull; the average yen rate was 0.29.
(c). Shipping.
According to the OSAKA MAINICHI of May 21, the
Ministry of Communications is drawing up a plan for
increasing Japan's merchant marine fleet from 4,000,000
tons to 6,000,000 tons within the next few years and
intends to introduce a bill to this effect in the next
regular session of the Diet.
VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). American Oil Interests.
With the authorization of their head offices, on
June 26 the local representatives of the Standard-Vacuum
Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company
made
the following statement to the Vice Minister for Commerce
and Industry, on the agreement that the Japanese authorities
would not enforce a reduction of the companies' import and
sales quotas: "The companies are now negotiating a special
arrangement to enable them to fulfill their obligation
under the Petroleum Law and have reached an agreement in
principle".* It appeared that the statement issued by the
oil companies would be accepted by the Japanese authorities.
The proposed plan for cooperation between the Standard-
Vacuum Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company on the
one hand and the Mitsui interests on the other hand appeared
at the end of the month to be acceptable in principle to
both parties, although there was some disagreement con-
cerning details.**
(b). Visit to American Ports of Japanese Naval
Training Squadron.
A Japanese naval training squadron consisting of the
over-age cruisers IWATE and YAKUMO under the command of
Vice Admiral Zengo Yoshida sailed from Japan on June 9
on a foreign cruise to include visits at ports on both the
Pacific and Atlentic seaboards of the United States.
The
information available to the Embassy did not indicate that
there was any particular political significance in this
naval cruise.***
(c).
*
Embassy's telegram No. 137 of June 23, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1914 of June 26, 1936.
***
Embassy's despatch No. 18e7 of June 11, 1936.

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
(c). Visit to Japan of Admiral Murfin.
Admiral Orin G. Murfin, successor to Admiral Frank B.
Upham as Commander-in-Chief of the United States Asiatic
Fleet, made an official visit to Japan from May 25 to June 5
aboard his flagship the U. S. 3. AUGUSTA. The entertain-
ments given for the Admiral by the Japanese were those
generally accorded a visiting Commander-in-Chief of the
United States Asiatic fleet. However, the welcome given
him was especially cordial and appuared to denote a desire
on the part of the Japanese Navy for friendship with the
United States.*
(d). Davao Land Problem in the Philippines.
There seemed to be less interest in Japan in the Davao
land question, which may possibly be explained by the
assurance given to the Philippine Commonwealth Assembly on
June 16 by President Quezon that there was nothing in the
"so-called" Davao problem to cause serious concern.
(e). Smuggling in North China.
It will be recalled that on May 27 the Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs asked the Counselor if the Embassy had
any data in its possession concernine the specific items
of American trede which were suffering from the smuggling
in North China.** In accordance with instructions received
from the Department,*** the Counsclor of the Embassy called
on the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs on June 3 and in
response to the latter's inquiry of May 27 orolly and
informally brought to his attention the Standard-Vacuum
Oil Company's assertion that the smuggling of kerosene and
gasoline

out that the sale and distribution of other items of

made no comment regarding any future action or the attitude

and Mr. Neville obtained a definite impression from the
interview that other countries could expect no cooperation
from the Japanese Government at the present time in the
suppression of smuggling in North China.

. Alleged Protests by the American and French

The JIJI of June 29 published inconspicuously as a
Domei report from Peiping an aocount of representations
made to the Japanese Embassy by the French and American
Embassies in Peiping which asserted that on June 23
Japanese guerds had threatened with bayonets American
and French officers in plain clothes.** According to the
JIJI, after consultin€ the military the Japanese Embassy
decided upon tho "rejoction" of these representations by
replying that Japanese soldicrs had not threatencd with
bayonets any foreigners and had had no intention of block-
ing traffic, and that groater caro would bc taken in
future to avoid similar regrcttablo misunderstandings
betwecn patrols and passers-by, which have occurred because
of language difficulties.

II. RELATIONS VITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
The tenseness which characterized Sino-Japanese
relations in May continued throughout the month of June.
New developments aggravated the situation created by
Japan's alleged aiding and abetting of the smuggling
operations in the East Hopei region and by the decision
of the Japanese War Ministry on May 15 to increase the
Japanese North China Carrison. On June 1 the Chinese
Ambassador in Tokyo was reported by the press to have
filed a protest from his Government with the Minister for
Foreign Affairs and Mr. Arita was said to have rejected
this protest. Upon his arrival in Tokyo on June 1, Lieu-
tenant-General Tada, former Commander of the North China
Garrison who had recently been transferred to the command
of the 1lth Division in Japan, was quoted by the JAPAN
TIMES to have asserted that the increase in Japan's North
China Garrison had to be taken for the protection of Japanese
residents in thc district and thct the hint of collusion
by Japanese troops in the North China smuggling was an
insult to the Japanese Carrison. In a conversation at
the War Department on Junc 24, the Embassy's Military
Attaché was informed that the movement of troops for the
reinforcement of the North China Garrison had been com-
pleted and that the Garrison had a strength of slightly
under 5,000 men.*
Mr. Suma, Japanese Consul General at Nanking until
recently on leave in Japan, continued to make statements
to the press, none of which were so blunt, however, as
the one made on May 3o to the effect that he had told
Chiang
* Embassy's telegram No. 139 of June 24, 1936.

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Chiang Kai-shek that "China must now choose between
mutual interdependence with Japan or war with Japan".*
Mr. Suma sailed for his post from Kobe on June 16, report-
edly remarking to a correspondent of the OSAKA MAINICHI
that if General Chiang Kai-shek wants to avoid the collapee
of China "the quicker he shakes hands with Japan and works
side by side with her the better it will be for China."
Mr. Shigeru Kawagoe, recently appointed Ambassador to
China who had been having conversations with Mr. Suma
on Sino-Japanese relations, left Tokyo for his new post
on June 16 and, according to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of
June 17, stated immediately prior to his departure that
political stability was needed in China before economic
assistance could properly be extended to that country.
With regard to the incipient civil war between the
Nanking Government and the Southwest factions, the JAPAN
MES of June 3 attributed to the Foreign Office spokesman
the statement that Japan is accustomed to such anti-Japanese
declarations as those made by the Southwest authorities,
which are apparently issued for "home consumption". The
general attitude of the Japanese press toward the controversy
between the Nanking Government and Southwest China was one
of derision rather than concern, particularly in view of
the contestants' alleged predilection for the use of tele-
grams instead of weapons.
Further developments during the month were the follow-
ing: the anti-Japanese student movement in Peiping and
the alleged anti-Japanese agitation in the Southwest,
followed by Japanese protests in both instances; a Japanese
protest against the bombing of a Japanese military train
at Tientsin in the latter part of May; and the firing unon
Japanese
* Embassy's telegram No. 117 of May 30, 1936.

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.96)
- 6 -
Japanese vessels reportedly engaged in snuggling by
Chinese patrol boats off Tientsin and Tsingtao, against
which the Japanese Embassy in Peiping is understood to
have filed a vigorous protest on the ground that the
Japanese vessels had not been engaged in smuggling.
Sensational reports appeared in the TOKYO NICHI NICHI
and tlhe HoCHI of June 7 to the effect that Creat Britain,
the Soviet Union, the Nanking Covernment, and the Southwest
factions were cooperatin to diminish Japanese influence
in China and that the anparent "revolution" of the Canton-
ese faction was merely an elaborate Britain-Nanking-Canton
plot to mobilize streneth in North China.
(b). Soviet Russia.
No serious Soviet-"Manchukuo" or "Manchukuo"-Outer
Mongolia frontier incidents took place during the month of
11
June and there seemed to be a temporary relaxation in the
1
tenseness of Joviet-Japanese relations.*
It was announced in the press on June 4 that Mr.
Ota, the Jananese Ambassador to the USSR, intended to
resien and it was rumored that his resicnation could be
attributed to the fact that he hed not been in favor with
the Army ever since his somewhat anti-milit ry speech in
Osaka about a year ago. Among the men mentioned to suc-
ceed r. Ota were Mr. To o, Director of the Europe and
Asia Bureau of the Foreign Office, Mr. Shigemitsu, former
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs, and Mr. Taketomi,
Minister to the Netherlands. Mr. Youreneff, the Soviet
Anbassador to Japan, had planned to take a thrce months'
leave, departing fron Tokyo on June 25. However, the
Soviet
Embassy's despatches Nos. 1894 of June 13, and 1920 of
Junc 26, 1936.

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
Soviet Embassy announced on June 13 that the Ambassador
had suddenly canceled his plans to take leave on instruc-
tions from Moscow and that he would remain indefinitely
at his post.
The points of view of the Japanese and Soviet Govern-
ments regarding the Soviet-"Manchukuo"-Korean borders
remained the same as they had been for the past several
years.
According to information obtained from the
Soviet Embassy, on June l0 Mr. Youreneff had an interview
with Premier Hirota, who brought up the question of the
EE
large number of Soviet troops stationed on or near the
Soviet-"Manchukuo"-Korean borders. The Soviet Ambassador
replied that his Government had in l931 proposed a non-
aggression pact, in reply to which Mr. Hirota shrugged
this suggestion aside with the statement, "We shall find
some other way".* Both the Foreign Office and the War
Ministry were reported to clinf to the hope for the
establishment of a demilitarized zone along the Soviet-
"Manchukuo" border. General Terauchi, the War Minister,
was stated by the press to have reiterated the Japanese
contention that in view of the strong Soviet forces along
the border a non-aggression pact between the two countries
would not provide sufficient security for Japan.
There appeared to be a growing tendency in Japan to
look with apprehension upon the improvement in recent
months of the Soviet Union's international position. The
Foreign Office was credited with the belief that Soviet
Russia was becoming more successful in its anti-Japanese

cannot be imported into Janan unless a permit of the com-

in retaliation against the tariff measure enacted by the
Australian Government which increased duties on cotton and

(n). The Navel Issue.
On June 23 the Cabinet approved the reconmendation of
the Ministers for the Navy and Foreien Affairs for the
refusal of the 3ritish invitation to adhere to the London
Three Tower aval Treaty. The reasons given by the press
for this decision were the folloving: Having withdrawn
from the London Navel Conference because of the rejection
of its demands for quantitative restriction and the abolition
of offensive arms, Japan sees no reason for its participation
at present in an acreement reached after its vithdrawal from
the Conference; and the London Treaty makes no quantitative
restrictions, is based primarily on a program of exchanging
building infornation, and is therefore of little assistance
to Japan.*
It was reported during the month that Japan agreed with
the contention of the United States that the British Govern-
ment shoula invoke the escalator clause of the ashington
and London Naval Treaties for the retention of excess
destroyer tonnage rather than retain such excess destroyer
tonnege merely through special agreement with other Fowers.**
At a meeting of the prefectural covernors, the Mavy Minister
was reported to have declared that if Japan neglected its
naval preparations for a monent it would lose its position
in East Asia and fail in its work of developing "Manctukuo"
and that therefore it was necessary for Japan to embark upon
a new naval replenishment
rogran unon the expirction of the

0
According to information obtained from the mbassy's

Squadron to the Second Fleet undoubtedly strengthens the

operating in the First Fleet remains unchan ed and their
reorganization into two divisions is probably a step taken
for tactical reasons.

having a civilian as Governor of the island.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). American Oil Interests in Japan.
During the month of July the local representatives
of the Standard Vacuum and Rising Sun Petroleum Companies
6363
continued their negotiations with the authorities of the
Japanese Ministry of Comnerce and Industry and also with
the Mitsui interests with a view to arriving at some sort
of agreement that would enable the Companies to meet the
requirements of the Petroleum Industry Law and at the s ame
time would allow the Companies to operate profitably in
Japan. These negotiations appeared to be making favorable
progress, the representatives of the oil interests reporting
to the Embassy that agreement had been reached in principle
both with the Japanese authorities and with the Mitsui
interests.*

. Japanese Reaction to Rumored Sino-American

515

893.

loan to China had been received and declined to comment on
what Japan would think of the loan if the rumor were true.
Nevertheless, the attitude of the Japanese press reflected
the opinion of the Japanese Govcrnment on loans to China
by third Powers, as exprossed in the Amau statement of

4.
American-Japanese Trade Relations.
It was reported by the JAFAN ADVERTISER of July 4
that leaders of the Japan Economic Federation and officials
of the Foreign Office, including Baron Senosuke Goh, Count
Aisuke Kabayama, and the Japanese Consul General in New
York, had held a meeting on July 2 to discuss measures to
meet American restrictions on Japanese imports and had
agreed upon the following program: in view of the peculiar
nature of the political structure of the United States, Japan
should pay more attention to the attitude of American
financiers than to that of the United States Government;
Japanese financial interests should send representatives
to the United States to discuss the situation with the
Tariff Commission and others directly concerned, rather
than leave the mattor of adjustment in the hands of the
Japanose and American Governments; and an economic mission
should be sent to the United States at the earliest possible
date to negotiate for adjustment of relations covering all
categories of Japanese exports to the United States.
The OSAKA MAINTCHI of July 24 reported that the Foreign
Office had decided upon the necessity of organizing a
Japanese-American Trade Commission to be composed of mer-
chants, Government officials, and officials of the Embassies
and

- 4 -
and Consulates of the two countries. The Foreign Office's
aim is said to be the alleviation of Japan's unfavorable
balance of trade.

on a good will tour of the Philippines sponsored by the

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a).
China.
94
Tue two principal matters of interest in the tangled
Sino-Japanese situation during the month of July were
Japan's complicity in the smuggling in North China and
Japan's apparent apprehension over Chiang Kai-shek's
successful suppression of the rebellion in the Southwest
and his consequent bid for increased control of North China.*
It appeared that Japan had come to the realization that
something should be done to solve the smuggling problem.
The solution considered, however, was one favorable to
Japan, since it was based on Chinese responsibility for
and Japanese innocence in the smuggling operations. Mr.
Kawagoe, the Japanese Ambassador to China, Mr. Amau, the
Foreign Office spokesman, and Mr. Kuwashima, Director of
the East Asia Bureau of the Foreign Office, all reportedly
stated that the solution of the problem could best be
effected by a substantial reduction by the Nanking Govern-
ment of its "excessively high" customs duties.
Press comment on the failure of the Southwest rebellion
was in general to the effect that while Generai Chiang Kai-
shek was to be congratulated on his efforts to unify China,
such unification could not fail to damage Japanese interests
if

(a). "Manchukuo".
The Japan-"Lanchukuo" agreement providing for partial
abolition of Japan's extraterritorial rights in Manchukuo",
which had been signed on June 10, came into force on July 1.
According to a Domei despatch from Hsinking dated July 1,
the "Manchukuo" Minister for Foreign Affairs issued an
official statement on that day in which he clearly indi-
cated that the authorities in Manchuria would grant no
greater privileges to other nations than to Japan.* The
argument used by the "Manchukuo" Minister for Foreign
Affairs was the well-known one, namely, that although
"Manchukuo" had issued a statement and addressed communi-
cations to the Powers to the effect that it would respect
those rights enjoyed by foreign countries by virtue of
their treaties with China, those countries that have ignored
that statement and those communications are "not in a
position to claim rights on the strength of this uni-
lateral declaration of the "Manchukuo" Government.

(f). France.
According to the JAPAN TIMES of August 4, on July 1
a trade agreement was concluded between the Government of
Syria and the Japan-Africa-Near East Export Association
providing that the Japan-Africa-Near East Export Association
shall levy an export control fee of 3% ad valorem on all
shipments from Japan to Syria; that the funds obtained from
this fee shall be used for indemnifying Japanese importers
for any losses that may be incurred in buying Syrian staple
products, such as raw cotton and wool; that the Government
of Syria shall cooperate with the Association in preventing
the importation of Japanese goods other than those identified
by the Association; and that the Government of Syria shall,
as hitherto, impose the minimum tariff on Japanese goods.
(g). Canada.
Sir Herbert Marler, the Canadian Minister, sailed from
Japan on July 3, having been transferred to Washington.
(h). Siam.
The JAPAN ADVERTISER of July 1O reported that Mr.
Itaro Ishii, Japanese Consul General in Shanghai, had been
apnointed Minister to Siam.

(i). Guatemala.
According to the OSAKA LAINICHI of July 1O, on July 8
VD
the Foreign Office received an official report from the
Japanese Chargé d'Affaires in Mexico City, who had been
informed by the Guatemalan Minister for Foreign Affairs in
reply to an inquiry that the Presidential decree issued on
May 18 providing for the exclusion of Asiatics from Guatemala
did not apply to Japanese, since the term Asiatic used in
the decree implied persons born in Continental Asia.

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.98)

WEA
(j). Australia.
On July 8 the Australian Government put in force a
special license system for imports of Japanese goods in
retaliation against the invocation of the Trade Protection
Law against Australian imports, which step had been taken
by Japan on June 25, 1936, as a reprisal against the raising
of the Australian customs tariff against Japanese textiles
in February, 1936.* A few days later the Australian Govern-
ment reportedly offered to suspend provisionally its new
action taken against Japanese goods and to begin fresh
negotiations for the conclusion of a commercial treaty
between the two countries. The Japanese Government flatly
rejected this offer. However, a week later the Japanese
Government offered to resume the trade negotiations with
Australia on a status quo basis and this offer was accepted
by the Australian Government.
Apparently Japan's motive in offering to resume negotia-
tions on a status quo basis, though not on the condition of
temporary withdrawal by Australia of its import license
system, was that this country wished to open the trade
parleys without prejudice to the satisfactory conditions
that prevailed before February of this year. It was probably
felt that Japan's bargaining position would be weakened by
accepting a concession from Australia before the negotiations
commenced.
(k). The Naval Issuc.
NE
It appeared that Japan had no intention of altering
its stand against adherence to the l936 Tripartite London
Treaty. The Japanesc press went so far as to state that
Japanese
Embassy's despatch No. 1918 of June 26, and No. 1919 of
June 26, 1936.

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
Japanese naval authorities had no false hopes that the
United States would abide by the l4-inch gun limit of
the Treaty.*
NE
On July 20 Japan signed the Montreux Treaty for
rearming of the Dardanelles Straits, with the following
reservations: that various provisions of the Treaty
shall not modify the position of Japan as a non-League
Power; and that Japan will reserve freedom of interpret-
ing the closing of the Straits and restriction of the
passage of warships by Turkey in case it is recognized
by the League of Nations that there is danger of war and
also freedom of interpreting the right of passage through
the Straits by warships for the performance of duties under
mutual aid treaties between Turkey and other Powers. Japan
was at first apparently reluctant to sign the Montreux
Treaty. However, this country seemed to feel the pressure
of international alliances to which it is not a party and
to fear that if it shoul d become too isolated from the
other Powers its diplomatic position in Europe might suffer.**
It was probably these considerations that prompted Japan
to sign the Treaty.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
Vory little was done by the Government toward carrying
out the policies announced shortly after the Incident of
8
February 26 and consequently the Cabinet continued to be
critlcized by the Army and the newspapers. However, it was
announced by the press that the Cabinet had decided to
consider the following "truly important" policies:
1.
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1936 of July 9, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1953 of July 22, 1936.

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11
1. Increase of defensive equipment, submitted
by the War and Navy Ministries.
2.
Readjustment of taxation, including a tax
increase, submitted by the Finance Ministry.
3. Nationalization of electric power, submitted
by the Communications Ministry.
4.
Central control of fuel, submitted by the
Commerce and Industry Ministry.
5.
Extension of the term of compulsory education,
submitted by the Education Ministry.
6. Revision of the agricultural village and land
system, submitted by the Agriculture and
Forestry Ministry.
It having been decided that the Cabinet as a whole
would consider the administrative and social reforms
proposed by the various ministries, a meeting to this
effect was held on July 10. After this meeting, however,
it was announced that the policy of consideration of the
national policy plans by the Cabinet as a whole had been
abandoned owing to the large number of such plans, friction
among Cabinet members, and dissatisfaction on the part of
the Wer and Navy Ministries, which wanted priority to be
given to their plans and budgets. Therefore it was decided
that the respective ministries would submit their individual
plans in writing to the Premier and the Finance Minister,
who would later submit to the Cabinet Council those plans
deemed feasible. Apparently Premier Hirota had decided
that it was necessary to allot first the minimum which the
defense services could be induced to accept and later to
consider the plans of the other Ministries in the light
of the money remaining.* It appeared to be fairly obvious
that the defense Ministries, particularly the War Ministry,
were asserting control over the Government. At the same
time the Army continued to express its dissatisfaction
with the Hirota Cabinet.

* Embassy's despatch No. 1952 of July 22, 1936.

0
It was announced by the War Office on July 3 that
Lieutenant Colonel Saburo Aizawa, murderer of Lieutenant
General Tetsuzan Nagata, had on that day been executed.*
(A0
An announcement was made by the Var Office on July 7
to the effect that of 19 officers, 75 non-commissioned
officers, 19 privates, and l0 civilians tried for partici-
pation in the February 26 Incident the following had been
sentenced: 13 officers and 4 civilians to death; 5 officers
to life imprisonment; 1 officer, 17 non-commissioned
officers, and 6 civilians to imprisonment of from 2 to 15
years; 27 non-commissioned officers and 3 privates to
imprisonment of from 18 months to two years, with stay of
execution for three years. In the judgment handed down by
the court martial the reason given for the sentences of
death and life imprisonment was that the officers concerned
had employed the Imperial Army without Imperial sanction.**
The announcement of the War Office stated further that. the
court martial had taken into consideration the purity of
the young officers' motives but had been forced to condemn
them for the reason stated above.***
On July 12 the Ministry of War issued a second announce-
ment to the effect that 15 persons sentenced to death on
July 5 had been executed on July 12.**** Of these 15 men,
13 were former officers of the Army and 2 were civilians.
No mention was made of the other two civilians sentenced
to death on July 5 and it appeared that their execution

Martial law in Tokyo was lifted at midnight on July 17,

under a newly instituted Air Force Headquarters which would
be commanded by a general or a lieutenant-general appointed
directly by the Emperor and which would be comparable in
status to the War Ministry, the General Staff, and the
Inspectorate-General of Military Education.

priations of ¥3,300,000,000 during the next six years for
the replenishment of national defense. It is thought that
a large part of this sum will be used in strengthening the
Army's air force and in increasing the Japanese military
forces in Manchuria to approximately thc same strength as
that of the Sovict Far Eastern Army.**

0
It was reported that the Home Ministry was preparing
γ
an air defense law, the principal provisions of which are:
892
the bill if enacted into law will be applicable to the six
1.0
largest cities of Japan; in Tokyo the 15 wards in the
central part of the city will be set aside as the first
fire zone, where wooden frame buildings will be prohibited
and where, if possible, all houses will be provided with
basements; in the 20 remaining wards, which were formerly
outside the city limits, boulevards 3oo feet wide known
as "velvet zones" will be constructed to afford shelter
to refugees in time of emergency and also to serve as
transport roads over which anti-aircraft guns and other
armaments can be easily moved; all city telephone, tele-
graph, and lighting wires must be laid underground; alleys
and side streets will be widened sufficiently to allow the
easy passage of fire engines; and gas tanks and reservoirs
will be concealed. The OSAKA MAINICHI of July 3O under-
stands that the law is scheduled to go into effect on
Apri1 1, 1938.
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The ASAHI of July 19 stated that the Finance Ministry
94.
had decided to submit at the next session of the Diet, to
be held in December, the Ministry's tax increase plan
but to postpone consideration of all taxation reform
schemes for some time.* It is understood that there will
be
Embassy's despatch No. 1952 of July 22, 1936.

be an increase in nearly all classes of tares as well as

According to press reports, the Finance Ministry's

Government should withdraw its tax increase plans and
obtain new funds only through bond issues.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
EA
(a). Foreign Trade.
Japan's exports for July were ¥222,895,000 and its
imports were ¥203,246,000, which represented a favorable
trade balance of y19,649,000 as compared with unfavorable
trade balances for May and June of ¥21,677,000 and ¥20,723,000.
respectively.*
(b). Financial Trends.
There were very large movements of funds during the
month, due principally to the conversion issue of national
bonds and to the increase in stock payments; the stock
market was more or less stagnant; postal savings increased;
and the average yen rate for the month was $0.29-1/4.*
(c).
* Information obtained from the Commercial Attaché's office.

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.98)
二17
(c). Civil Aviation.
According to the ASAHI of July 27, four new domestic
airlines aggregating 938 miles in length will be opened
for service on October 1 of this year by the Japan Air
Transport Company, partly with the aid of Government funds.
VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.

Ix.
LABOR AND EMIGRATION.
At a general meeting of the Shakai Taishuto (Social
4
Masses Party) at Osaka on July 16, a resolution was adopted
pledging the party to the establishment of parliamentary
government in the interests of the working classes, nationali-
zation of important industries, stabilization of the national
living, and the overthrow of the Hirota Cabinet. It was
reported that a number of Leftist labor organizations not
affiliated with the Shakai Taishuto had decided to offer
their support to this party for the promotion of the
general welfare of the working classes.
Toward the end of the month leaders of the New Japan
Seamen's Union reportedly decided to go on strike through
dissatisfaction over the Shinowners' Association's attitude
toward retirement allowances and minimum wages. However,
on July 29 the Shipowners' Association recognized the
Seamen's right to collective bargaining and the leaders
of the union decided not to strike. It was reported that
the prospects for an amicable settlement of the controversy
were hopeful at the close of the month.
The Japanese press stated that the Overseas Ministry
had

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
- 18-
had drafted a plan for the settlement of five million
Japanese in Manchuria over a period of twelve years at
a cost of ¥2,000,000,000, of which sum the Japanese
Government would furnish y800,000,000.* It was further
reported that 50,O00 emigrants would proceed to Manchuria
in the fiscal year 1937-1938, and that thereafter the
number would gradually increase each year.
X. MISCELLANEOUS.
Nothing to report.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). American Oil Interests in Japan.
Pressed for a definite statement by the Japanese
6363
8941
Minister for Commerce and Industry, the foreign oil com-
panies stated that the construction of the storage tanks
required by the Petroleum Industry Law would be commenced
by the end of September 1936 and that the oil would be
placed in storage by April l937, "subject to alteration
depending upon special arrangement now under discussion".*
However, the foreign oil interests were unable to obtain
a definite statement from the Japanese Government as to
the maximum amount of storage to be required and the amount
of governmental compensation which may be expected for the
carrying of excess sto-ks in the future.
The negotiations vetween the Standard Vacuum Oil Com-
pany and the Risin Sun Petroleum Company on one hand and
the Mitsui interests on the other appeared to be progress-
ing slowly but steadily toward a common agreement. The
Mitsui interests promised the local representatives of
the foreign oil interests that they would make some con-
crete proposal to the foreign oil intercsts within a short
time.**
(b).
Japanese Origin of the Sino-American Loan
Agreement Rumor.
Wit!: reference to the Domei news dospatch from Shanghai
dated July 20, which reported that an agreement had been
concluded between the United States and China providing
for a long-term credit to the latter country to be used
in buying merican airplanes, automobiles, and munitions
of war,*a maiber  the Embassy's staff was informed by
the
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1977 of August 6, 1936.
**

丰**
Embassy's telcgram No. 160 of July 21, 1936, and

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.98)

the Shanghai manager of Domei that this news despatch had
come to his office from the office of the Military Attaché
of the Japanese Embassy in China. The Embassy's informant
added that the story had been sent out when he was absent
from his duties and that he considered the incident a
serious error which had been detrimental to Domei.*
(c). Third America-Japan Student Conference.
The Third America-Japan Student Conference was held
in Tokyo from August 1 to 8.** An address was delivered
at the opening meeting by the Ambassador.*** American
colleges and universities were represented by 43 delegates,
a large part of them from Reed College, Stanford University,
the University of Washington, the University of Southern
California, and Williamette College. There was a week
of round-table discussions on politics, economics, culture,
and religion. Among the specific topics taken up were the
tendency toward dictatorship in Japan, the racial question
in American-Japanese relations, territorial readjustment,
Japanese morals, and Christianity in Japan. Owing to
Government supervision most of the views expressed were
those advocated by the Japanese Government. Despite this
fact, it uppeared probable that the American delegates
would leave Japan with a better understanding of Japanese
students and with a realization that not all of the latter
think politically as the Japanese Government would want.

(a). American-Japanese Trade Relations.
Accordine to the JAFAN TIMES and the CHUGAI SHOGYO
of August 12, Mr. Renzo Sawada, Jarinese Consul General in
New York who is now in Tokyo, and Mr. Royoji Inouye,
Jananese
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2025. of September 3, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1993 of August 20, 1936.
***

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
Japanese Trade Commissioner who returned to Japan
recently, have jointly submitted to the Foreign Orrice
a plan for the readjustment of trade between Japan and
the United States, the object of which is to increase
exports of miscellaneous goods from this country for the
purpose of reducing the unfavorable balance in Japan's
trade with the United States.
The JAPAN TIMES outlined
the principal points of the scheme as follows: to increase
Japan's exports to the United States of all trade articles
excepting raw silk (which is an essential raw material for
American industry) for thc purpose of reducing Japan's
unfavorable balance of trade; to fix the annual maximum
and minimum amounts of the exports involved and to con-
trol such exports in order that the United States may
agree to the above-mentioned proposition; and, in the
event that the United States asks Japan to reduce its
exports of a certain article, to meet such a request on
the condition that a corresponding increase is to be
allowed in exports of another commodity.
With regard to the Japanese economic mission to the
United States about which there has been a certain amount
of talk during the past several months, thc press reported
that it had now been decided that it would be impracticable
to consider the sending of such a mission this fall, prin-
cipally for the reason that the Japanese Government had
not yet formulated its trade policy.
II.
RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
793.97
Sino-Japanese relations, which had been none too
good durin? the four previous months owing to the smuggling
imbroglio in North China, became even more tense during
the month under review. The smuggling in North China
decreased

=== Page 145 === (confidence 1.00)
decreased considerably but this was due not to the efforts
of the Japanese but to the efficient preventive measures
taken by the Chinese authorities and to the over-stocking
of the market by the smugglers.* Japanese authorities
continued to profess the belief that the fundamental
solution of the smuggling situation lay in the lowering
of the Chinese tariff rates. On August 30 the Hopei-
Chahar Political Council, the pseudo-autonomous regime
partially controlled by the Japanese, established its own
customs offices and imposed consumption taxes on goods
imported from East Hopei of about one-eighth of the
customs duties charged by the Nanking Government.
Chiang Kai-shek's success in Southwest China con-
tinued to be viewed with apprehension by the Japanese on
the ground that the General planned to assert the Central
Government's authority over the Hopei-Chahar Political
Council and the Eastern Hopei Autonomous Government, and
to weaken the pro-Japanese regime of General Han Fu-chieh
in Shantung. Several Japanese newspapers commented on
General Chianc Kai-shek's alleged plans in a threatening
vein and asserted that the Japanese Government was firmly
resolved to block any efforts that might be made to restore
Nanking's influence over North China.
Meanwhile, it was reported by the JIJI and the NICHI
NICHI of August 18 that in the opinion of certain Foreign
Office circles interference by the local Japanese military
authorities must be eliminated if the various problems
affecting Japan were to be settled.
At
Embassy's telegram No. 169 of August 17, 1936.

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
At the end of the month it appeared that serious
repercussions might follow an incident which occurred at
Chengtu in Szechwan Province on August 24. According to
Japanese sources, the Chinese authorities attempted to
prevent the reopening of the Japanese Consulate at Chengtu
on the ground that Chengtu was not an open port and also
to impede the journey to Chengtu of the newly appointed
Japanesc Consul, Mr. Eiichi Iwai. As stated in the Japancse
newspaper versions of the matter, Mr. Iwai was held up
at Chungking on August 17 as a result of obstructive
measures taken by the local Chinese authorities, while
his travellin companions proceeded to Chengtu: Koza-
buro Watanabe, Shanghai correspondent of the OSAKA MAINICHI;
Keiji Fukagawa, correspondent of the SHANCHAI MAINICHI;
Takeo Tanaka, an employee of the Shanghai office of the
South Manchuria Railway Company; and Naoshi Seto, a Hankow
merchant. According to Domei despatches from Shanghai, on
August 25 an anti-Japanese mob attacked the hotel in Cheng-
tu in which the Japanese were stayine, killing the two
newspaper correspondents and injuring the other two Japanese.
This action was vigorously condemned by the Japanese
Foreign Office, Army, Navy, and press and the blame for
it was assigned to the Nanking Government. It was reported
by the press that the Japanese Foreign Office was not
satisfied with the statement made on August 26 by a spokes-
man of the Chinese Foreign Office to the effect that the
anti-Japanese act had been plotted by Communists.
While the expected Japanese protest was not filed
with the Chinese Government before the end of the month,
the press reported that the Japanese Army and Navy were
insistent

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
insistent in their demands that the Chengtu incident be
utilized as a means of bringing pressure to bear on China
for the elimination of all anti-Japanese movements in that
country.*
(b).
Great Britain.
WE
There were indications during the month that the
Japanese desired improved relations with Great Britain.
In the early part of the month Japanese newspapers spoke
of a plan for Anglo-Japanese cooperation alleged to be
entertained by Sir Samuel Hoare. Sir Samuel Hoare's plan
was reported by the ASAHI of August 8 to be the following:
Japan will affirm its regard for China's territorial in-
tegrity and the Open Door in China and will undertake not
to encroach on British rights and interests in that country,
in return for which Great Britain will recognize "Manchu-
kuo" and admit that Japan has special rights and interests
in North China; Japan will guarantee to purchase certain
quantities of British goods, in return for which Great
Britain will lower its customs tariffs on Japanese goods;
and Japan will agree informally to the terms of the London
Naval Treaty of 1936 while not necessarily adhering formally
to that pact. On August 18 Mr. Amau told foreign newspaper
correspondents that the Japanese Foreign Office had re-
ceived no report from the Japanese Ambassador in London
regarding the plan for Anglo-Japanese cooperation credited
to Sir Samuel Hoare and denied that any instructions had
been sent to the Ambassador regarding the matter.
Although the initiators of this movement toward
some sort of Anglo-Japanese rapprochement were the Japanese
themselves
*
Embassy's telegram No. 176 of September 2, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1970 of August 6, 1936.

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
themselves, the vernacular press stated that the movement
had originated in England and spoke of the matter as a
"British offer to Japan". In any case, it would appear
that nothing definite was accomplished in the way of
establishing closer relations between the two countries,
whatever may be the prospects for such an arrangement in
the future.*
It appeared that the Japanese military were opposed
to such a political arrangement with Great Britain on the
ground that it would entail too much limitation upon Japan's
freedom of action.**
(c). Australia.
In accordance with an agreement reached in July between
the Japanese and Australian Governments,*** negotiations
were resumed on August 28 between representatives of the
WE
two Governments with a view to reaching a settlement of
the Japanese- Australian trade dispute. The outstanding
questions to be settled appeared to be the following: to
what extent the Australian Government will admit the im-
portation of Japanese cotton and rayon textiles; to what
extent the Australian Government will lower its tariff
rates; and to what extent Japan will admit imports of
Australian raw wool.****

Much publicity was civen the forthcoming visit of

on September 19. It was reported that the purposes of
