(e). Peru.
LA
According to the TOKYO NICIHI NICHI of August 12, the
Japanese Foreign Office was resolved to continue negotia-
tions with the Peruvian Government looking toward a modifi-
cation of the latter's decree of June 26, 1936, which pro-
vides that the number of foreign immigrants of any one
country eligible to live in Peru shall be limited to
16,O00. This newspaper stated that although the decree
theoretically restricted immigrants of all countries, it
was applicable in fact only to the Japanese, of whom
21,000 were living in Peru as against 7,000 Chinese and
6,00o Italians.*
(f). Spain.
WE
On the whole, Japanese public and official opinion
concerning the Spanish civil war has been guardedly neutral
in tone.** As in previous cases, such as the actions of
the League of Nations in general and the international
complications of the Italo-Ethiopian war, the Japanese
press has adopted a superior attitude toward the diffi-
culties in Spain, limiting its comments to the deplorable
aspect of the whole situation. Although the Italian action
in Ethiopia and the present insurrectionary movement against
the leftist government of Spain ought logically to meet
with the sympathy of the Japanese Government and people,
in
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2036 of September 16, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2035 of September 16, 1936.

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.99)
-10
in neither case have the bounds of neutrality been over-
stepped in the expression of opinion.
According to the ASAHI of August 21, the Japanese
Government promptly quashed a move by Japanese radicals
to send monetary aid to the Spanish leftists, citing as
their reason for this step the assertion that assistance
in any form to either the Government forces or to the
rebels in Spain was likely to excite international feeling.
Other plans for an expression of sympathy to the Spanish
Government were uncovered by the police and nipped in
the bud.
An incident which occasioned much comment in diplo-
matic circles and in the Japanese press was the resigna-
tion on August 26 of Mr. Santiago Méndez de Vigo, Spanish
Minister to Japan. On August 26, the Spanish Minister,
the Counselor of the Spanish Legation (Mr. Juan Gomez
Molina) and the Spanish Consul in Kobe (Mr. Francisco
Castillo) issued the following communiqué to the press,
a copy of which was handed to the Ambassador as Dean of
the Diplomatic Corps by the Spanish Minister on the same
day:
"The Spanish Minister, the Counsellor of the
Spanish Legation in Tokio and the Spanish Consul
in Kobe, hereby declare that, although without
more news than those published by the press, based
on innermost conviction, they have from July of
this year broken all relations with the Government
of Madrid; they are absolutely identified with the
movement which is led by the Generals Franco,
Cabanellas and Mola, men of a dignified and
patriotic history who all through their life have
not

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
not maintained other ideal than the welfare and
glory of Spain. It is therefore undeniable that
their idealism which we all good Spanish share
could not possibly stay indifferent against the
regrettable policy developed by the Government
of Madrid which drove Spain into real chaos, owing
to the present struggle between the anarchy backed
by the communism on one hand and civilization,
order and respect towards the most sacred rights
of humanity on the other."
Domei reported on August 29 that despite the resigna-
tion of Mr. Santiago Méndez de Vigo as Spain's Minister
to Japan and his announcement that he had associated
himself with the revolutionary cause, the Japanese Foreign
Office had decided to continue to regard him as the
Spanish Minister for thc reason that Mr. de Vigo's
credentials from the Spanish Government did not lose
their validity through the Minister's private statement.
(g).
Soviet Russia.
There appeared to be a lessening of tension in
Soviet-Japanese relations, owing primarily to the facts
that fewer aggravating incidents occurred and that Japan
was more engrossed with its position in China.*
The
incidents which took place were only minor in character,
the sole affair of any importance being the execution
at Hailar on August 21 of nine members of the "Soviet
Storm Troops". This incident seems to have caused less
than usual anxiety to the Soviet Government.
A statement which received a certain amount of
comment
* Embassy's despatch No. 2002 of August 21, 1936.

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
-12
comment in the Japanese press was that attributed to
Mr. Ota, retiring Japanese Ambassador to the Soviet
Union, to the effect that he strongly advocated the
conclusion of a non-aggression pact with Soviet Russia,
which had been spurned on several occasions by the
Japanese Government. On the other hand Mr. Mamoru Shige-
mitsu, whose appointment as Ambassador to the USSR was
approved by the Cabinet on August 25, was reported to
have stated in an interview with a correspondent of the
ASAHI on August 27 that no alteration would take place
in Japan's diplomatic policy toward Soviet Russia and
that the time was still premature for the conclusion
of a non-aggression treaty between the two countries.
The Army had been opposed to Mr. Shigemitsu for
reasons already reported to the Department.* It was
allegedly this fact which was instrumental in bringing
about Mr. Shigemitsu's resignation from the post of
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs. However, the
insistence of the Foreign Office on Mr. Snigemitsu's
qualifications for the post of Ambassador to the USSR
was reported to have overcome the Army's opposition,
probably because the Army felt that he was sufficiently
in sympathy with the Army's policy toward the USSR.
(h). The Naval Question.
No notification was sent by the Japanese Government
to the British Government concerning Japan's attitude
toward Great Britain's invocation of the escalator
clause of the London Naval Treaty of 1930 for the
retention of 40,000 tons of destroyer tonnage that
would
* Embassy's despatch No. 2006 of Auguet 27, 1936.

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.98)
- 13 -
would otherwise have to be scrapped by the end of the
present year, although reports were to the effect that
Japan was considering the retention of excess submarine
strength in view of the fact that this country had
relatively little excess in destroyer tonnage. Neither
was it clear just what attitude the Japanese Government
would adopt concerning the limitation of guns on capital
ships, i. e. to fourteen-inch or to sixteen-inch.* It
appeared unlikely that Japan had any intention of agree-
ing to any naval limitation treaty in the near future.
(1). International.
Japan's foreign policy came in for a great deal of
discussion during the month of August.** This discussion
was initiated by the Seiyukai, which on July 31 submitted
a statement to Premier Hirota urging the Government to
establish a definite foreign policy for the reason that
there was "hardly an item in the Government policy that
is not connected with foreign relations". On August 4
the Minseito presented a similar statement to the Premier
asserting that the discussion of national defense and
financial plans without deciding upon a definite foreign
policy would only increase the unrest of the people.
On August l0 the Kenyukai (of the House of Peers) also
criticized the Government for not establishing a definite
foreign policy. According to the YOMIURI of August 10,
the Navy Minister, Admiral Nagano, at a conference held
between the Ministers for Foreign Affairs, War, and Navy,
on August 7, announced that he advocated a strong
permanent
本
Embassy's telegram No. 172 of August 27, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1990 of August 20, 1936.

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
permanent foreign policy in order to cope with the
growing tendency to exclude Japanese goods from world
markets. It was reported that the Army, however, was
in disagreement with the political parties and with the
Navy in this matter on the following grounds: that a
national foreign policy has already been established
and therefore no question of its priority over other
national policies exists; that Japan's international
status cannot be improved simply through diplomacy; and
that the nation's diplomatic strength must be increased
by arms expansion. Hence, the Army reportedly held the
belief that the replenishment of national defense should
take precedence over all other national policies.
It was stated by the NICHI NICHI of August 12 that
Mr. Arita, the Minister for Foreign Affairs, had explained
at Cabinet meetings on August 7 and 1l that the Foreign
Office could see no need of taking any steps in response
to the agitation for the establishment of a definite
foreign policy. According to the NICHI NICHI, Mr.
Arita outlined Ja an's present foreign policy as follows:
Japan must increase its Manchurian military forces to
the point where they are equal to those of Soviet Russia
in Siberia and only after this has been effected can the
Foreign Office negotiate with the USSR for the mutual
withdrawal of troops through the conclusion of a non-
aggression pact or the establishment of a demilitarized
zone; Japan must be. carcful to prevent an anti-Japanese
combination between China and Soviet Russia and must
also endeavor to create a buffer zone in North China
between China and "Manchukuo"; and efforts must be
made

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15 -
made to improve Japan's relations with Great Britain
by explaining to that country Japan's objectives on
the continent of Asia and by avoiding friction with
Great Britain over Japan's "Southward Advance" policy.
Mr. Horinouchi, the Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs, stated to a member of the Embassy's staff
that the political parties and other elements in the
Government were afraid to express open opposition to
the excessive budgetary demands of the Army and had
therefore taken the tack of agitating for the establish-
ment of a definite and firm foreign policy as a pre-
requisite to the formation of other policies in order
to attack the Army's military demands indirectly.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
According to Domei of August 21, the following
twelve new national policies finally decided upon by the
Premier and Finance Minister Baba received the approval
of the War and Navy Ministers on August 2l: tax reform;
electric power control; fuel and resources policy; pro-
motion of foreign trade; encouragement of civil aviation;
creation of industrial reserve laboratories; readjustment
of farm debts; creation of a system of mutual insurance
against natural disasters in rural communities; agrarian
land reform; promotion of public health; revision of the
compulsory education system; and inauguration of a
permanent system of grants to local governments from
the central treasury. This news agency also stated that
the Premier and the Finance Minister had acceded to the
demand of the War Minister for approval of the Overaeas
Ministry's plan for encouraging large scale emigration
to

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
to "Manchukuo" and to the Navy Minister's demand for
approval of the shipping control program of the Com-
munications Ministry. It will be observed that most
of these policies are vague and it remains to be seen
whether the method of carrying them out will meet with
the approval of the Army, the political parties, and the
people at large.
Ever since the February 26 Incident the Army has
been clamoring for radical reform in the administrative
organization of the Government. At a Cabinet meeting
held on August 28 Premier Hirota was reported by the
ASAHI of that date to have asked each of the Cabinet
Ministers for suggestions in the matter of the administra-
tive reform of the individual ministries. This newspaper
interpreted the Premier's action as an indication that
he had no intention of bringing about the sort of reform
desired by the Army, such as abolition of some ministries,
creation of new ministries and the appointment of ministers
without portfolio for the formulation of national policies.
The fact that Premier Hirota did not report to
Prince Saionji, the Genro, on the Cabinet's adoption of
a schedule of national policies was seen by the ASAhI of
August 27 as an indication that the Genro had ceased to
be an important influence in Japanese government since
the February 26 Incident. The Premier's omission in this
case can probably be ascribed to the opposition of the
February 26 insurgents and of their moral sympathizers
among the people to the influence of the Genro and other
Elder Statesmen surrounding the Throne.
One of the principal internal political issues during
the month was the question of strengthening state control
over

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17 -
over the electricity supply industry, which was one of
the Government's polioies announced during the month.*
Mr. Tanomogi, the Communications Minister, sponsored a
plan for "private ownership and state operation" of the
electricity supply industry and his plan was supported
by the Army and other elements who favor a kind of state
socialism. It met with the opposition of the electric
power interests and financial and business interests
on the ground that it would not, as claimed, result
in a reduction of rate charges to the general public
unless the Government made up financial losses from some
other sources, which in the long run would be a burden
on the people. Subsequently the Minister of Commerce
and Industry advocated "state ownership and state opera-
tion" in lieu of the private ownership and state opera-
tion sponsored by the Communications Minister. The pro-
posal of the Minister for Commerce and Industry gained
many adherents. However, the Finance Minister stated
in a press interview that he was definitely opposed to
this proposal on the ground that it would necessitate
the issuance of a large amount of national bonds for the
purchasc of private electricity plants. At the end of
the month the controversy showed no signs of settlement
although it appeared that the Premier would make efforts
to work out an acceptable compromise among the opposing
groups. The controversy has political, economic, and
social significance for the reason that it brings up
the question of state socialism, an ideal toward which
there has been a ter.dency since the coming into power of
the Army in 1931.*
(b).
Embassy's despatch No. 2028 of September 4, 1936.

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
- 18 -
(b). The Army.
On Aueust ll the War Minister was reported by Domei
to have issued a warning to the Cabinet that decisions
on national policies must be expedited. It was said
that General Terauchi stated further that there seemed
to be a lack of appreciation of the emergency nature of
the times. After the approval of the Government's poli-
cies mentioned in the preceding section of this report
the press reported that the "national renovation"
desires of the Army were unanpeased, particularly be-
cause of the abstractness of the policies adopted and
also because of the doubtful prospects of radictl ad-
ministrative reform in the Government. The Army's
budgetary demands were reported to exceed ¥3,000,000,000
in additional expenditures during the next twelve years
and there appeared to be little likelihood that the
Premier would be ablc to withstand these demands.
The routine Army shifts which took place on August 1
included about 3,O00 changes and promotions. These
shifts had several significant aspects: the shifts
were acclaimed by the press as contributing to a cor-
rection of lack of discipline in the Army which culminated
in the incident of Fcbruary 26; the list of changes and
promotions was drafted by the War Minister alone and
approved by the Emperor and thus it was the first time
since l913 that the annual list had not been submitted
to the joint approval of the War Minister, the Vice
Minister for War, and the Inspcctor General of Military
Education; the officer appointed as Inspector General
of Education was Licutenant-Gcncral Sugiyama, a man
with

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
- 19 -
with normal ideas and apparently with no connections
with any political groups or factions within or without
the Army; and the list further demonstrates the close
relation between the War Office and the North China
Garrison which has been increasing ever since the
incident of February 26.*
(c). The Navy.
The press reported that the budgetary demands of
the Navy Ministry for the fiscal year 1937-1938 sub-
mitted to the Finance Minister on August 17 amounted to
¥770,000,000 which represents an increase of ¥219,000,000
over the national budget for the current fiscal year,
and that the inauguration of a ¥1,000,000,000 five-year
naval replenishment program was provided for in the Navy's
budget estimates. A part of the increased estimates may
be accounted for by a plan for the training of aviators
and the improvement of airplanes, "in view of the changing
circumstances and trends in other countries".
(d). Political Parties.
While the Minseito were reported to be highly satis-
fied with the Government's policies as decided upon on
August 2l, the Seiyukai criticized those policies, assert-
ing that they were vaguc and omitted to mention a definite
foreign policy.
(e). The Diet.
Premier Hirota and Mr. Baba, the Finance Minister,
were reported to be in favor of the suggestion that the
Diet session should be opened in September instead of
in December in order to avoid the Dict recess at the
end
* Embassy's despatch No. 1976 of August 6, 1936.

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
- 20 -
end of the year. However, the Army declared its opposi-
tion to the plan on the ground that during the holding
of the annual Army maneuvers in the fall the War Minister
and other Cabinet Ministers would be absent from the
capital. It appeared likely that the suggestion would
be dropped because of the Army's attitude.
(f). Reactionaries.
The MIYAkO of August 25 reported that the rightist
movement, which seemed superficially to have been on the
wane since the February 26 insurrection, was regaining
strength and that a group of Army and Navy reservists,
including such outstanding figures as Lieutenant-General
Yoshimi Tatekawa, Vice Admiral Shosaburo Kobayashi, and
Colonel Kingoro Hashimoto were participating in the move-
ment. According to this nuwspaper, the Home Minister
has decided to hold a conference of chiefs of special
high departments of the prefectural police in Tokyo for
three days commencing September 7 in order to cope with
the resurgence of the rightist movement.
(g). The Emperor.
It was reported that during the last week of the
month four prominent women, including a former chief
lady-in-waiting to the Empress, had been arrested on
charges of occult practices which involved disrespect
to the Imperial Household. The women arrested were al-
leged to have called themselves "spiritual descendents
of the gods" and to have disseminated stories which they
claimed were divulged to them by divine sources.
(h)

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.98)
-21 -
(h). Colonies.
As predicted by the press in July*, General Jiro
Minami, former Commander of the Kwantung Army and
Ambassador to "Manchukuo", was appointed Governor General
of Korea to succeed General Ugaki and Admiral Seizo Koba-
yashi, former member of the Supreme War Council, was
appointed Governor General of Formosa to succeed the
civilian, Mr. Kenzo Nakagawa.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
A bill being drafted by the Minister of Agriculture
and Forestry providing for the conversion of tenant farmers
into land owning agriculturists at a cost of ¥60,000,000
over a 25-year period was reported to be meeting with
widespread opposition in farming circles on the ground
that it would entail a drastic increase in the price of
farm land and also with opposition among landowners reluct-
ant to sell their land.
According to the ASAHI of August 29, the Home Office
has started drafting a bill to be presented at the next
regular session of the Diet which provides that shops
in general shall be obliged to close at l0 o'clock in
summer and at 9 o'clock in winter and to give their em-
ployees at least one holiday a month; that the working
hours of department stores shall be limited to ten hours
a day, with four holidays a month for each employee; and
that violators of these provisions shall be required to
pay fines not exceeding ¥500.
V.
* Monthly report for July Section, III (e).

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
- 22 -
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
In a press interview on August 2, the Finance Minister
made the following statements: that in the light of the
prevailing international situation an increase in military
expenditures was unavoidable; that increased Government
expenditures would be met with the issuance of red ink
bonds not in excess of 1,000,000,000 annually and with an
increase in taxes; and that the issuance of red ink bonds
up to about 1,000,000,000 per year would not cause social
unrest.
A tax reform plan tas reportedly been agreed upon by
the Finance and Home Ministers providing for the abolition
or transfer to the national treasury of approximately half
the amount of taxes now being collected by local units
of the Government.
JI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Exports from Japan for July amounted to ¥229,515,000,
while imports into this country amounted to ¥191,148,000,
which represented a favorable trade balance of ¥38,367,000,
as compared with a favorable balance for July of y19,649,000.
The increase in exports during the month was accounted for
by increases in exports of cotton textiles, canned food-
stuffs, and miscellaneous goods.*
(b). Financial Trends.
Money rates were low. The conversion of 5% national
bonds to 3-1/2% national bonds to the amount of ¥654,616, 742
was satisfactorily effected and the bonds were readily
absorbed.
Data and information furnished by the Commercial Attaché's
office.

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.98)
-23 -
absorbed. The Fokyo Wholesale Price Index advanced from
197.4 for July to 200,7! for August. The average yen
rate for the month was 0.29-1/4, i. e., the same as for
the month of July.
(c). Shipping.
A boom was reported in thc shipbuilding industry.
According to the Kobe Shipping Exchange, there were 118
ships under construction in Japan on August 15, totalling
733,400 tons dead weight. Of these ships, six were being
built for foreign countries, freighters numbered 87, passen-
ger boats and combined passenger and freight boats numbered
15, tankers numbered 7, and whalers 3. According to the
JAPAN TIMES of August 10, the Minister for Communications
has drawn up a plan for the construction within the next
four years of 400,000 tons of first class merchant ships,
some with a speed of 25 to 26 knots. It was said that a
Government subsidy of ¥100,000,000 would be granted for
this purpose.
VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
IX.
LABOR.
According to the NICHI NICHI of August 25, a measure
similar to the Nazi law for state control of labor in
Germany was in the process of preparation in the Home Office.
This newspaper states that the bill, which may be submitted
at the next session of the Diet, provides for a final
"settlement" of Japan's labor problems by outlawing unions,
abolishing the right to strike, and establishing Government
"protection" of labor; that the Home Office aims to embody
in

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
in the bill the spirit of Japan's family system, both
economically and ethically; that the Home Office believes
that capitalists must justify their profits by contri-
buting to the general good of society and that employees
owe something more than their work in return for their
wages, namely, duty to the State; and that, in accordance
with the provisions of the bill, the State will act as an
impartial third party to settle questions arising between
capital and labor.
It was stated by the JAPAN TIMES of August 17 and by
the JAPAN ADVERTISER of August 18 that on August 16 the
SHAKAI TAISHUTO, or Social Mass Party, had expressed
opposition to increases in defense expenditures; had re-
iterated its lack of confidence in the Hirota Cabinet;
had pledged itself to work for the overthrow of the present
Cabinet; and had charged that all the working masses could
expect from the Government was increased burdens.
X. MISCELLANEOUS.
It was reported that on August 12 the Home Minister,
the Vice Minister for Home Affairs, the Director of the
Police Affairs Bureau of the Home Office, and the Director
of the Shrines Bureau of that Department had held a meeting
to discuss possible evils in connection with the Olympic
Games in Tokyo in 1940. These officials decided, according
to reports, to start a campaign for a morally pure Tokyo
during the Olympic Games and during the solemn observance
of the 2,600th anniversary of the founding of the Empire,
to prohibit the seeking of autographs of athletes parti-
cipating in the Games (particularly by females), and to
close all dance halls during the 1940 event.

I: RELATIONS WITH TIE UNITED STATES.
(a). Japan's Retention of Excess Submarine Tonnage.
On September 3 the Japanese Ambassador in Washington
delivered a memorandum to the Department of State setting
forth the decision of the Japanese Government to retain an
excess of 11,059 tons of destroyers and 15,593 tons of sub-
marines in view of the British action in invoking Article 21
of the London Naval Treaty of 193o for the retention of
40,000 tons of over-age destroyers.* On September 5 the
American Government replied to the effect that there was
no legal ground on which the Japanese could retain 15,598
tons of submarines without actually invoking the escalator
clause and that the American Government could not follow the
reasoning that because the Japanese did not have sufficient
tonnage in the destroyer category they should compensate by
retaining excess submerine tonnage.
It was reportou by the press that on September 1o the
Japanese Ministries of Forein Affairs and the Navy had
agreed to maintain that the Japanese action was not a
violation of the 193o London Naval Treaty and that on the
contrary the retention of both submarines and destroyers
was more in keeping with the spirit of the Treaty and with
the spirit of disarmament thun the building up of Japan's
destroyer strength to 26,657 tons. On September 14 the
spokesman of the Foreign Office, in an interview with foreign
nowspaper correspondents, set forth arguments in support of
Japan's action which were equally as specious as the views
of the Foreign and Navy offices outlined above.
RP
(b).

to the Lansing Memoirs.
It was stated by the ASAiI of September 24 that there

At the end of the month the Japanese Government was

engage Japanese advisers.
On September 21, the Japanese Navy Ministry issued a
statenent to the effect that since the situation in China
had developed to a point where the Japanese Navy could no

and annarently no action has been taken as a result of it.

There continued to be friction between the Premier

since the February 26 Incident.* On September 22 the
War Minister reportedly presented to the Premier the
following plan of administrative reform: the creation of
a Cabinet Minister without fortfolio who would control
the various existing bureauxfor investigation and coordine-

with the Ministry of Comaerce and Industry; the uniting

of the Ministry of Over-
seas Affnirs.*

controversy between Premier Hirota and General Terauchi
would bring about the resignation of the Cabinet. Neither
did it appear that the War Minister's proposal of Sep-
tembcr 22 had any significance as a project for the exten-

policy on the Asiatic Continent.

(e). Reactionaries.
It was reported that on September 22 the police had
arrested over two dozen right-wing agitators, among them
a number of members of the "God-sent troops", for pre-
ventive detention during the period of the military maneuvers.
At a meeting held on September 26 the Meirinkai, an
organization of retired Army and Navy officers and other
men of nationalistic leanings, passed a resolution to be
presented to the Government calling for the suspension of
diplomatic negotiations with China and a "guarantee occu-
pation" of strategic points in that country.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
When the law providing for the protection and sur-
veillance of persons charged with dangerous thought goes
into effect on November 1,* according to Domei of Septem-
ber 23, the Ministry of Justice will establish "protective
thoughts surveillance stations" in seven cities and sub-
stations in fifteen other cities. These stations and sub-
stations are to be under the control of "thought guidance
officials" and "thought protection officials".
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
Finance Minister Baba's plan for the revision of taxes,
an outline of which has been reported to the Department,**
was published on September 2. In general, the plan pro-
vides for an increase in the income, business profits,
and inheritance taxes. As announced by the Finance Ministry,
the purposes of the proposed revision are the following:
equalization
*
Embassy's despatch No. 20l7 of September 2, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2063 of October 2, 1936.

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.98)
- 15 -
equalization of the tax burdens of the people through a
fundamental reform of the central and local tax systems;
the remedying of the unequal burdens locally, between
rersonal and real property and between individuals and
juridical persons; the consolidation of the national
finances by increased taxation to prevent present expendi-
tures from being left entirely to the succeeding generations
to repay; and the establishment of an elastic taxation sys-
tem both centrally and locelly.
The Finance Minister's tax revision proposal was
opposed in Japanese business circles and by the Japanese
press and appeared to be a potential issue of foremost
importence in the internal political situation.
Accorcing to the JIJI of September 21, the expendi-
tures nrovided for in the budget for the fiscal year 1937-
1938 of an estimated ¥3,260,000,000 wi11 require the
issuance or bonds to the amount of ¥850,000,000, or about
¥150,000,000 more than the issuance for the fiscal year
1936-1937.
Mr. Eigo Fukai, Governor of the Bank of Japan, stated
on September 27 that in his belief the exchange policy of
the Finance Ministry would not be changed as a result of
the situation brought about by the devaluation of the
French franc.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Jepan's September exports were ¥243,712,000, while
imports into this country amounted to ¥189,618,000, which
represented a favorable trade balance of ¥54,094,000 as
compared with a favorable balance for August of ¥38,367,000.
(b).

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.98)

(b). Financial Trends.
a
Although the stock market was slightly unsettled, it
was remarkably steady considering the uncertainty caused
by Finance Minister Baba's tax plan and the difficulties
in China. The Government issued Y6ll,ooo Coo worth or
bonds during the month. For the first time in Japan's
history the national debt exceeded ¥10,000,000,000. The
average yen rate for September was 0.2934 as compared with
$0.2925 for August.
(c).
Aviation.
Domei of September 26 stated that a part of the funds
necessary for the encouragement of civil aviation in Japan
would be obtained by requiring depositors in postal savings
lanks to surrender their regular interest for a period of
threc years. It was also reported by Domei that the Com-
runicetiens Ministry would issue "patriotic postage stamps"
for the development of civil aviation.

IX.
LABOR AID EMIGRATION.
(a)
Labor.
There was some talk during the month of the establish-
ment of a popular front movement in Japan similar to the
movements in Spain and France. However, there appeared to
be no likelihood that e popular front would be established
in this country in the near future.* High officials of
the Home Office declared on September 7 that no popular
front movement would be tolerated in Japan.
The
* Enbassy's despatch No. 204ε of September 18, 1936.

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.98)
- 17 -
The Government's firm control of the labor situation
was demonstrated by the order issued by the War Ministry
on September lo to the effect that all laborers of the
military arsenals at Osaka, Nagoya, and Kokura, numbering
about 5,60o laborers, must sever their connections with
all labor unions on the ground that it was against the
principle and spirit of military discipline for such work-
ers to retain membership in labor unions. According to
the press, every arsenal worker in question took an oath
to withdraw from his union and never to join another one.
Resolutions protestin, against the measure taken by the
military were passed by the Social Mass Party, the Labor
and Farm Council, the Jepan Trade Union Congress, and the
Tokyo Traffic Labor Union. These protcsts appeared to be
ontircly ineffectual.
(b). Emigration.
Accoraing to the MIYAKo of September 20, the Navy end
War Ministries and other Government Departments interested
in Japan's southward expansion have dccided to ask the
Foreign Ofrice to open nogotiations with the Philippine
√9
Commonwealth Government and the Netherland Indies for
relaxation of their restrictions on Japanese emigration.
As already reportcd to tho Department*, Japanese emigration
to the regions south of this country is a part of Japan's
"economic und poaceful southward advance".
X. MISCELLANEOUS.
Accordin to thc JAPAli ADVERTISER of September 26,
the Cabinet Intelligence Commission plans to publish a
wcekly edition of the OFFICIAL GAZETTE concerning current
internal
Embassy's despatch No. 2019 of September 2, 1936.

internal, international, legal, scientiric, and economic
affairs, for the purpose of enlightening the Japanese
public on Government policies.
Considerable editorial comment and epistolary contro-
versy in the colunns of the JAPAN ADVERTISER were caused
by the report that on September 5 the Metropolitan Police
Board had decided to adopt a positive policy for the sur-
veillance of foreigners suspected of espionage and for the
guarding of Japanese womanhood from international scandals.*
This police campaign was said by Domei to have arisen
principally from the desire of the police authorities to
protect both national secrets and Japanese women at the
time of the holding of the Olympic Games in Japan in 1940.

Far Eastern territory. On September 13 Domei reported

opposed to the oft-repeated policy of the USSR.
National energy in Japan was so absorbed during the
month by the tense Sino-Japanese situation that there was
a lull in the border incidents and pretensions which
normally characterize Japanese-Soviet relations.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a).
. American-Japanese Relations in Connection
with the Sino-Japanese Situation.
At the request of Mr. Horinouchi, the Vice Minister
711.90
for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Ballantine, First Secretary of the
Embassy, called on the Vice Minister on October 3.* The
gist of the Vice Minister's conversation was to the effect
that the principal desideratum of the Japanese Government
in China was the suppression of the anti-Japanese activities
and that the other Japanese "desires" were in no sense
"unconditional demands". Mr. Horinouchi specifically denied
that, as had been reported by the press, Japan was demanding
the right to station troops along the Yangtze River or the
establishment of an autonomous regime in North China.
On the same day the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
stated in a conversation with Mr. Dickover, the Chargé
d'Affaires ad interim, that Japan had no intention of
coercing China for the attainment of Japanese aims and
asserted that the only demand upon which Japan was insistent
was the suppression hy the Chinese of anti-Japanese propa-
ganda and acitation.
On October 6, acting under instructions from the
Department, Mr. Ballantine called upon the Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs and read to him a statement to the
effect that the American Government was pleased to have
the benefit of such direct information from the Japanese
Government as had been communicated on October 3.

In reply to a note from the Embassy dated September 21

informed the Embassy that the Japanese Government was unable
for various reasons to give consent to the proposed visits
of the U.S.S. GOLD STAR at Palau, Truk, and Saipan.*
(c). American Petroleum Interests in Japan.
On the basis of assurances given by the Japanese Ministry
of Commerce and Industry the American and British oil interests
reached an agreement with the Mitsui interests on the general
principle that Mitsui would invest approximately ¥17,000,000
for the erection of tanks and for the purchase of petroleum
required to be kept in storage by the oil companies and that
the latter would compensate Mitsui for this service at the
rate of 7-1/2% on the investment.
On October 9 the representatives of the Shell and
Standard interests aadressed a letter to the Japanese Minister
for Commerce and Industry requesting that the latter confirm
oral assurances given by the Vice Minister for Cormerce and
Industry on September 19. No confirmation was received from
the Japanese Ministry of Commerce end Industry before the
close of the month.**

(e). Difficulties Encountered by American Consular
Officers in Procuring Commercial Information
In Japan.
It will be recalled that on July 27, 1936, in com-
pliance with the Department's instructions, the Embassy
made an informal and oral approach to the Japanese Foreign
Office in regard to the difficulties encountered by American
consular officers in Japan in the procuring of information
of a commercial or other non-military nature. On October 27
Mr. Matsushima, the Chief of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs
of the Foreign Office, replied to the approach which had been
made by the Embassy. Mr. Matsushima said that it had been
arranged with the locel Chambers of Commerce in Osaka and
Kobe and with the Japan Economic Federation in Tokyo to
supply any information to Consuls which they found difficulty
in obtaining through direct channels. He appeared to be
of the opinion that if this nrocedure were adopted suspicion
of the Consuls' motives in requesting information would be
avoided.*
(f).
Reception in Japan of Stimson's Book "The Far
Eastern Crisis".
Contrary to expectation, Mr. Henry L. Stimson's book
"The Far Eastern Crisis" was permitted to enter Japan by
the censorship authorities of the Home Office, which had
received
* Embassy's despatch No. 2l03 of October 28, 1936.

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
received a suggestion from the Foreign Ofrice in favor of
the book's admission. Two prominent Japanese monthly
magazines carried incomplete translations of the book and
one of them contained an editorial by Mr. Masanori Ito,
the well-known magazine writer, who recommended that Mr.
Stimson's book be read analytically and without anger in
Japan and at the same time expressed his disagreement with
Mr. Stimson's interpretation of the nature of Japan's
interests in Manchuria and of the exclusively defensive
qualities of China's boycott against Japan. Excepting for
an editorial in the JAPAN ADVERTISER of October 24, no
other critical comment on the "Far Eastern Crisis" came
to the Embassy's attention. In the opinion of the Embassy,
the effect produced on Japan by Mr. Stimson's book will on
the whole be beneficial to the United States.*
II. RELATIONS WIT: OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
7
While no further inoidents occurred to acgravate the
already tense Sino-Jepanese situation which had arisen
largely as a result of the murders of Japanese subjects at
Chengtu, Pakhoi, and Hankow,** the diplomatic negotiations
between the two countries lookine toward an agreement based
on Japanese desires met with serious difficulties during the
month of October.
On October 2 the Japanese Government announced its
decision to send Mr. Kuwashima, Director of the East Asiatic
Bureau of the Foreign Office, to China for conferences with
Ambassador Kawagoe. In commenting upon this decision the
spokesman of the Japanese Foreign Office explained that his
Government, regarding the situation as grave, recognized
the
*
Embassy's despatch No. 21l2 of October 30, 1936.
**
Embassy's Monthly Report for September, Section II (a).

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
the necessity of conveying the "real intentions" of the
Japanese Government to General Chiang Kai-shek and that
telegrams to the Japanese Ambassador in China could not
accomplish this purpose. According to the Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs, the primary purpose of Kuwashima's
trip to China was to bring perspective to the Japanese
negotiators on the spot. However, a possible reason for
Kuwashima's mission was the desire of the Japanese Govern-
ment to emphasize to Ambassador Kawagoe that he must take
his orders from Tokyo and not from his military and naval
advisers and also to make clear to the Japanese public that
the Foreign Office was using every diplomatic means to
solve the problem, so that it could not be accused of
remissness in the event of failure of the negotiations.*
Press reports continued to state that strong demands
had been made of the Nanking Government by Japan. In com-
menting upon a Reuter news despatch from London to the
effect that the Japanese demands included the right to
station troops alon the Yangtze, the right to edit Chinese
school texts, and the autonomy of the five Northern Provinces,
on October 1 the spokesman of the Japanese Foreign Office
denied that any such demands had been made and attributed
the spreading of the Reuter report and similar reports in
countries other than China or Japan to a malicious intent
on the part of those who would profit by alienating China
from Japan.** A move taken by the Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs to counteract the effect of such press reports has
already been mentioned.***

Upon the insistence of the Japanese Government, General
Chiang Kai-shek returned to Nanking for a conference with
Ambassador Kawagoe, which was held on October 8.
official communiqué issued after the conference stated that
General Chiang Kai-shek had expressed profound regret for
the Chengtu and other incidents and had also expressed his
accord with the Japanese proposition to make joint efforts
for the adjustment of relations between the two countries
and "thus contribute to the establishment of Far Eastern
peace". Domei reported on October 14 that Ambassador Kawa-
goe and the Chinese Minister for Foreign Affairs had resumed
their conversations on that day and that, according to
reliable reports, the Chinese Government had accepted Japan's
proposals for the settlement of the Chengtu incident and
also for a eneral adjustment of Sino-Jepanese relatinns.
Although the official communiqués issued in regard to General
Chiang Kai-shek's conference with Ambassador Kawagoe and
the conversations between the Japanese Ambassador and the
Chinesc Minister for Forcign Affairs were vecue and general
in tone, the Japanese news agency Domei and the press did
not hesitate to report the alleged contents of these con-
versations.
It was arparent at the close of the month that the
Japanese Government, for the present at least, had not
altered its policy of seeking its ends in China through
diplomatic means. It appeared that Chinese resistance to
the Japanese demands had definitely stiffened, particularly
to the demands in regard to North China and joint defense
against communism.*
Thus, the impasse reached in the

* Embassy's despatch No. 2107 of October 29, 1936.

- 7 -
negotiations continued without the issuance of an ultimatum
or the employment of other forms of intimidation by Japan.
Presumably Japan had by the close of the month become aware
of a change in the Chinese attitude and had reached the con-
clusion that it would not for the moment risk the consequence
of "independent action", which would probably lead to armed
resistance by the Chinese. However, there remained the
possibility that Japan might be forced to take such action
either as the result of the occurrence of further anti-
Japanese incidents in China or the possible adoption of
such a strong attitude by China as to compel Japan to con-
sider its prestige on the Asiatic Continent.

(b). Great Britain.
There was a feeling of distrust in Japan over Great
Britain's attitude toward and actions in China. The Japanse
Foreign Office appeared to be attempting to convince the
Japanese public that the Nanking Government was being
poisoned against Japan by British inspiration.* According to
Domei, Mr. Kuwashima, Director of the East Asiatic Bureau
of the Foreign Office, stated in an interview on his return
voyage from Nanking that he did not know whether the British
and American authorities in China were engaged in any secret
maneuvers but that he considered it significant that British
journals had published editorials sympathetic with China,
which he thought a reflection of the attitude of the British
Government. On October 1l several Japanese newspapers
reported that shortly after the inauguration of Sino-
Japanese negotiations at Nanking the British Government had
addressed communications to the interested signatories of
the Nine Power Pact suggesting a joint protest to Japan on
grounds
* Embassy's despatch No. 2086 of October 16, 1936.

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
grounds of a violation of that Treaty. According to London
news despatches which reached Japan two days later, the
British Foreign Office denied this report. Great Britain
was severely criticized by the Japanese press for its alleged
extension of large credits to China and was accused of
breaking international good faith by supporting the Nanking
Government in a clandestine manner.
Several other matters complicated Anglo-Japanese relations.
On October 2l Great 3ritain officially notified Japan of
India's abrogation of the Indo-Japanese trade agreement,
to take effect on March 31, 1o37. Subsequently the Indo-
Japanese trade negotiations were reported to have become
deadlocked owing to disagreement in regard to the fixing of
quotas for Japanese cotton textiles and Indian raw cotton.
Authorities of the Keelun~ water police reportedly
accused three officers of the British submarine depot ship
MEDWAY of having taken photographs of strategic points out-
side the port of Keelung, Formosa, on October 6. Although
it was stated by Domei of October 7 that the police intended
to examine the British officers, the affair received no
subsequent oublicity during the month.
Further tension between the two countries was caused
by the mistreatment of thrce British sailors at Keelung in
the early morning of October 8.* An argument having arisen
between a Japanese taxi driver and the three sailors over
the payment of cab fare, the latter were arrested by the
Japanese police and, according to the British version, man-
handled and injured. News despatches from London reported
that Vice-Admiral Sir Charles Little, Commander-in-Chief of
the British naval forces in China, had postponed a visit
to
* Embassy's despatch No. 2115 of October 30, 1936.

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.97)
to Japan in protest against the Keelung incident. On
October 30 the spokesman of the Japanese Foreign Office
told foreign newspaper correspondents that, according to
the police report received from Keelung, the three British
sailors had not been roughly treated by the Japanese police.
An article published in the KOKUMIN of October 29
indicated that the Japanese Government had given up the
idea of entering into a political arrangement with Great
Britain for cooperation. Since the KOKUMIN frequently
expresses the opinions of certain Japanese Army circles,
it is possible that the views attributed to the Japanese
Government came from military sources. In any case, the
chauvinistic elements in Japan hold the view that so long
as the importation of Japanese goods into British territory
is restricted a cooperative arrangement with Great Britain
woula be a one-sided affair.*

III.
INTERNAL FOLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
Although the question of administrative reform con-
914
tinued to occupy the press, no progress was made in
clarifying the details of the reform proposal. Premier
Hirota appeared to be giving his personal attention to
the matter in private discussions but avoiding letting the
plan become an open issue.* Toward the end of the month
the newspapers reported that the Army had come forward
with a suggestion that political reform should include a
restriction of the powers of the Diet whereby Diet members
would not be permitted to become Cabinet ministers and the
political parties would be entirely divorced from the
administration of Government, being allowed only to debate
in the Diet. Opposition to the Army's reported reform
proposal was widespread.**
At a Cabinet meeting held on October 20 a compromise
agreement accepteble to Mr. Tanomogi, Minister for Com-
munications, was approved "in principle" on the much-
discussed
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2087 of October 16, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2ll7 of October 31, 1936.

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15 -
discussed plan for ctate control of electric power industries.
While certain alterations were made in Mr. Tanomogi's original
plan, his thesis of "private ownership and state operation"
remained the basis of the compromise plan, the essential
points of which are as follows: that the various privately
owned electric power industries shall be required to buy
shares in a state institution called the Electric Power
Facilities Company; and that the Electric Power Facilities
Company shall, within a limited period of years, employ funds
derived from loans or surplus in buying back at par the
shares of those privately owned electric power companies
which are willing to sell their shares. It was understood
that the plan would be submitted to the Diet after the New
Year holidays and, according to press indications, it should
meet with opposition in that body.*
(b).
. The Army.
The grand military maneuvers were held in the Hokkaido
from October 2 to October 5. Two outstandine features of
the maneuvers noted by the Embassy's Military Attachó
were
that foreign observers were eiven the opportunity of seeing
lass than in former years and that the maneuvers were not
executed in as up-to-date a fashion as usually.
A War Office statement issued on October 1 reported
the suicide of Captain Wetaru Tanaka of the General Staff
who was involved in the February 26 incident.
(c). Reactionaries.
A new reactionary society, the Dai Nippon Seinento
(Japan Young Man's Party) held its inaugural celebration
on October 17. It is understood that the leader of the
new party is Colonel Kingoro Hashimoto, a colonel on the
reserve list, and that its function will be to spread the
ideal
Embassy's despatches No. 2083 of October 15, and No. 2109
of October 29, 1936.

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
ideal of the Imperial Way and "to construct a moral world".
(d). The Press.
At a conference with foreien newspaper correspondents
on October 6 the Foreign Office spokesman advocated the
€iving by Domei (the semi-official and sole Japanese news
agency) of some indication of the source of its news from
abroad. Apparently the succestion given by Mr. Amau to
Domei was the result of one of that agency's reports from
London to the effect that the British Government had con-
veyed strong observations to Japan with regard to the
Sino-Japanese situation.
(e). The Emperor.
It was again reported* that Admiral Kantaro Suzuki,
Grand Chamberlain, had made up his mind to resign because
of his responsibility over the February 26 incident.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Nothing to report.
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
Finance Minister Baba's tax proposal continued to meet
with opposition from financial circles, the House of Peers,
and the two political parties on the ground that it would
increase the taxation of the masses.** It will be remem-
bered that one of the foremost demands of the February 26
insurgents and consequently one of the principal points
in the Hirota Government's national platform was the
stabilization of the livelihood of the people and as a
corollary a decrease in the existing taxation burden on
the people. However, it appeared likely that Dr. Baba's
plan or a plan similar to it would be eventually adopted
in
Embassy's previous.Monthly Political Reports.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2088 of October 16, 1936.

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17 -
in view of the urgent and insistent demands of the War and
Navy Ministries for considerable increases in their budgets.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS:
(a). Foreign Trade.
Japanese exports for October amounted to ¥245,259,927
while imports into Japan amounted to v187,091,519 which
represented a favorable trade balance of ¥58,168,408 as
compared with a favorable balance for September of ¥54,-
O94,000. The increase in exports for the month was due
EA
to large shipments of cotton and rayon yarn and textiles.
It was reported that Japanese woollen textile manu-
facturers were confronted with two new threats to continue
expansion of their foreign trade: the reported intention of
the Union of South Africa to impose a prohibitive tariff
on imports of Japanese woollen goods and the proposed
establishment of a woollen textile industry in China, which
would absorb the Australian wool now boycotted by Japan and
would create competition for Japanese woollen textiles both
in China and in the world market.
Domei reported that Japan was willing to participate
in the proposed World Economic Conference but that at the
same time official and financial circles in Japan were
worried over a possible attempt by other nations to raise
the level of the yen should such a conference be held.
(b). Financial Trends.
The money market was tight, especially on short term
loans; the stock market was quiet; bond and share prices
weakened slihtly as a result of the Government's announce-
ment of its tax program; and the average yen rate for the
month was 0.2856 as compared with 0.2934 for the month
of September.
SECTIONS VII, VIII, IX and X: Nothing to report.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). Japanese Reaction to the Re-election of
President Roosevelt.
711.94
The Presidential election campaign of November l936
attracted perhaps more widespread attention in Japan than
any previous one.* Editorial comment on the re-election of
President Roosevelt was highly favorable. Regarding the
probable effect of the election on American-Japanese relations
in particular, the YOMIURI predicted that President Roose-
velt's "good neighbor" policy would be continued and the
TOKYO NICHI NICHI hailed President Roosevelt's re-election
in view of the Democratic Party's tradition against inter-
vention in foreign countries, pointing out that the Administra-
tion had consistently maintained the attitude of an "onlooker"
with respect to Far Eastern affairs.
According to Domei, Japanese business and financial
circles also acclaimed President Roosevelt'a re-election,
expressing the opinion that higher American tariffs during
the next four years were unlikely and that a continuance of
the President's relief policies would probably promote a
demand for Japanese goods.
(b). Decision of the United States to Retain 19, 000
Tons of Destroyers.
In accordance with the Department's instructions,** on
November 6 the Embassy informed the Japanese Foreign Office
by a memorandum that the United States Government had decided
to exercise its right under Aticle IVII of the London Naval
Treaty of 1930 to retain 19,000 tons of destroyers.***
WE
(c).
Japanese Reaction to Reportedly Proposed Con-
struction of a Sea-plane Base on Midway Island.
A report from Washin ton to the effect that the American
Navy Department advocated the construction of a sea-plane
base on Midway Island aroused little comment in Japan, which
would
Embassy's despatch No. 2121 of November 10, 1936.
**
Department's telegram o. 225 of November 6, 1936, and
Embassy's despatch No. 2139 of November 13, 1936.
***
Embassy's Monthly Report for September, Section I(a).

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
would indicate that the Japanese fully expected that such a
move would be made by the United States.* The only two
WE
newspaper comments that came to the Embassy's attention were
those in the JAPAN TIMES of November 15 and the TOKYO NICHI
NICHI of November 18. The former newspaper stated that the
Japanese Navy attached a great deal of importance to the
plan of the United States Navy for the reasons that it was
a move to extend the outlying base of the United States
naval air force to a point approximately 1,800 nautical
miles from Tokyo and that the proposed sea-plane base upon
completion would be able to accommodate twenty of the largest
sea-planes. On the other hand, an article in the NICHI NICHI
of November 18 minimized the importance of the United States
Navy's alleged project on the grounds that nothing could be
done to prevent it and that as a result of Japan's refusal
to continue naval negotiations at London in January 1936
this country must be prepared to see the United States in-
crease its armaments for the maintenance of its present naval
superiority.
In the opinion of the Embassy's Naval Attaché, the fact
that Japan has thus far not made an issue of the American
Navy's reported project indicates that Japan considers a
free hand in the Pacific to be of greater benefit than con-
tinuance under the present fortification restrictions. Such
a free hand would allow Japan to strengthen its naval de-
fenses to the southward for the acquisition of a hegemony
over the western Pacific and for the furtherance of Japan's
"peaceful and economic southward advance".
(a).
Assistant Naval Attaché Freed of Suspicion
of Photographic Espionage.
It will be recalled that on October 7 Lieutenant-
Commander
* Information obtained from the Embassy's Naval Attaché.

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Commander Ofstie, Assistant Naval Attaché of the Embassy,
was accused by a member of the Japanese gendarmerie of
having taken photographs from a train near the aviation
station at Kure.* An informal conversation that Lieutenant-
Commander Ofstie had at a social function during the month
under review with the Vice Minister for the Navy and the
Senior Aide to the Navy Ministry would indicate that the
Japanese authorities considered the matter closed and
tacitly admitted that the gendarme had been at fault. Both
these officials expressed to Lieutenant-Commander Ofstie
their regrets that the incident had occurred and implied
that the gendarme in question had obtained false information
from an idle bystander.
II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
7939
No progress whatsoever took place in the negotiations
for a Sino-Japanese accord at Nanking, only one interview,
that held on November 10, taking place between the Japanese
Ambassador to China and the Chinese Minister for Foreign
Affairs.** At the close of the month there appenred to be
little or no hope of a successful conclusion of the negotiation:
The political situation between the two countries was
further complicated by an outbreak of hostilities between
the Suiyuan Provincial forces of the Nanking Government and
the East Suiyuan Mongols. While the Chinese insisted that
the military activities of the East Suiyuan Mongols had
been instigated by Japan, the Japanese claimed that China
was using this allegedly baseless charge as an excuse to
obstruct
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2073 of October 9, 1936, and
Monthly Report for October, Section I (d).
**
Embassy's despatches No. 2127 of November 13, 1936, and
No. 2161 of November 26, 1936, and Embassy's telegram
No. 234 of November 14, 1936.

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.97)
obstruct the Sino-Japanese negotiations in Nanking. Japanese
instigation of the Mongolians to move against the Suiyuan
Provincial forces, if true, might have been one of the steps
prepared by Japan in anticipation of China's failure to agree
to Japan's so-called proposals for the readjustment of rela-
tions between the two countries.*
It became even more apparent during the month that for
various reasons Japanese public opinion was becoming calmer
and less truculent toward China.**
(b). Germany.
762.94
It will be remembered that on September 19 Domei published
a report from Berlin to the effect that upon his return to
Tokyo Ambassador von Dirksen would enter into negotiations
with the Japanese Government for the building up of a German-
Japanese front against Bolshevism; that a military understand-
ing between the two countries had already been reached; and
that if the Japanese Government should agree with Germany's
desire for cooperation against Communism, Germany might find
it convenient to give formal diplomatic recognition to "Manchu-
kuo".***
The Domei report was partially borne out with the announce-
ment by the Japanese Government on November 25 of the con-
clusion of a Japanese-German agreement providing for mutual
defense against the activities of the Communist International,
to remain in force for a period of five years. On November 23
the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs had given Mr. Dickover,
the Chargé d'Affaires ad interim, a summary of the agreement****
and on the afternoon of its release the Vice Minister had
given the Embassy the Japanese text and an "unofficial trans-
lation" in English of the agreement.*****
Although

Although there is no concrete evidence of the conclusion

The existence of some sort of agreement between the two
General Staffs has been the subject of speculation for some

(c). Soviet Russia.
Although aggravating incidents involving Japanese sub-
741.9l
jects or Soviet citizens during the month were few in number
E=
and relatively unimportant , save for an armed clash between

for the worse following the reports of the conclusion of
the German-Japanese agreement against the Communist Inter-
national.****
Mr. Mamoru Shigemitsu, recently appointed Ambassador
to the Soviet Union, left Tokyo for Moscow on November 5
and at a prcss interview held before his departure stated,
among other things, that there was no change in the Japanese
attitude toward a Japanese-Soviet non-aggression treaty,
adding that it was more important to create an atmosphere
favorable to the conclusion of such a treaty than to con-
clude the treaty.
Even before the announcement of the Japanese-German
accord against the Comintern, it appeared most probable
that the U.S.S.R. would continue adamant in its insistence
that the Soviet Union must be represented as one unit and

With regard to the third important question in Soviet-

at Moscow on October l0, it remained to be seen whether
Soviet Russia would in retaliation against the signing of
the Japanese-German accord create any difficulty in the way
of carrying out the concession agreement.
Possibly because of Soviet indignation against Japan
and Soviet pressure on the Outer Mongolian Government, the
preliminary border discussions between the latter Government
and "Manchukuo" were reportedly postponed for a temporary
period, although the only reason given by the Outer Mongolian
Government for such postponement was the illness of its
chief delegate, Mr. Samboa.

(d). Great Britain.
Relations between Great Britain and Japan, which had
become somewhat tense during the previous month, showed no
signs of betterment during November. The problem created
NE
by the mistreatment at the hands of Japanese police of
three British sailors at Keelung, Formosa, on October *
was not solved during the month and British representatives
in Tokyo openly stated that the British Government was not
satisfied with the reaction of the Japanese Government to
the British protest.** Furthermore, Japanese jealousy over
British influence in China was unabated and Japan's con-
clusion of the anti-Comintern accord with Germany was not
calculated to ease any misapprehensions that Great Britain
might have regarding Japan's imperialistic policies.
(e). Australia.
11
The hopeful trend in the negotiations between Japan
and Australia for the conclusion of a compromise agreement
on the importation of Japanese textiles into Australia and
the importation of Australian raw wool into Japan continued
during the month. According to news despatches reaching
Japan from Canberra, Australian political circles predicted
that a settlement in the trade dispute would be reached on
a basis of 40o,Oo0 bales of Australian wool to be admitted
annually into Japan and of 125,000,000 square yards of
Japanese cotton and rayon textiles to be admitted each year
into Australia. Likewise, Japanese opinion seemed more
optimistic with respect to the ultimate settlement of the
long-standing trade dispute.***
LA
(f). Brazil.
Two developments were reported as the outcome of the
recent visit to Japan of the Brazilian Economic Mission.***
According

According to the JAPAN TIMES of November 6, on the previous

of the Tokyo Chamber of Commerce and Industry, the object

(g). Siam.
On November 5 the Siamese Government notified the
Japanese Government of the abrogation of the present Siamese-
Japanese Treaty of Commerce and Navigation, the abrogation
to take cffect one year after the notification. According
to the TOKYO NICHI NICHI of November 6, in handing his
Government's notification to the Japanese Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs the Siamese Minister in Tokyo stated that
his Government desired the abrogation of the present Treaty,
concluded at Bangkok on March 10, 1924, in order that Siam
might recover its "legal and economic autonomy" on an equal
footing. The Siamese Minister was said to have explained
that a similar proposal had been made to all Powers with
which Siam had concluded similar treaties.
(h). Canada.
111
On November 7 the Honorable Robert R. Bruce presented
to the Emperor his credentials as Canadian Minister to Japan,
succeeding Sir Herbert Marler, who departed for Canada on
July 3 en route to Washington to assume there the duties of
Canadian Minister to the United States.*
(1).

(1). "Manchukuo".
Further progress in the process of absorption of
Manchuria by Japan was described by the Japanese press
as continued favorable progress in the relations between
Japan and Manchukuo". According to Domei of November 5,
on that day a complete accord was reached on the general
policies of the "Manchukuo" Government's five-year indus-
trial program at a conference attended by representatives
of the "Manchukuo" Government, the Kwantung Army, the
Japanese Embassy, and the South Manchuria Railway. It
was reported by the TOKYO NICHI NICHI of November 18 that
the Communications Ministers of Japan and "Manchukuo" had
two days previously signed a mutual postal exchange agree-
ment to go into effect on December 1, providing that the
transfer account of each country shall be honored in the
other. Most of the Japanese newspapers reported on November
12 that the Japanese Government, having changed its mind as
a result of recent interviews between Mr. Matsuoka, Presi-
dent of the South Manchuria Railway, and Finance Minister
Baba, had agreed to pay cash for its unpaid shares in the
South Manchuria Railway Company. Finally, Domei thought it
worth while to report from Hsinking on November 25 that
Manchukuoan" Foreign Office circles had expressed the
fullest endorsement of Japan's action in concluding the
anti-Comintern agreement with Germany.
(j). Italy.
No official announcement was made in Tokyo regarding
the agreement reportedly negotiated between Japan and Italy,
although a Domei despatch from Rome dated November 28
stated that the Italian Government had on that day officially
announced the conclusion of a reciprocal agreement providing
for de facto recognition of Manchukuo" by Italy and of the
Italian

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
Italian Empire in Ethiopia by Japan. On November 30 the
spokesman of the Japanese Foreign Office denied that any
agreement had been reached in the negotiations at Rome
between the Japanese Ambassador and the Italian Minister
for Foreign Affairs, despite reports from Rome to that
effect. Mr. Amau added that the negotiations were not con-
nected with the recently concluded German-Japanese Comintern
agreement.* In addition, Japanese officials disclaimed the
wide scope ascribed to the negotiations by the press.
(k). Poland.
EE
During the early part of the month the Polish Chargé:
d'Affaires in Tokyo stated in a conversation with a member
of the Embassy's staff that he had been recently informed by
the Japanese Government of its intention to raise its
Legation in Warsaw to the status of an Embassy and that the
Polish Government was prepared to reciprocate. The Polish
Chargé d'Affaires added that for budgetary reasons of the
Japanese Government the change was not expected to take
place until after April 1, 1937.**
(1). Spain.
When questioned by foreign newspaper correspondents
concerning press reports to the effect that Tokyo would
recognize the Franco Government in Spain "as soon as its
military forces entered Madrid", the spokesman of the
Japanese Foreign Office stated on November 9 that his
Government would wait and see what the attitude of other
principal Powers would be. On the following day, accord-
ing to Domei, the Minister for Foreign Affairs told the
Cabinet that Japan might recognize the Fascist régime in
Spain
*
Embassy's telegram No. 245 of November 30, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2132 of November 13, 1936.
