Spain at an opportune time. The ASAHI of November 10
stated that Mr. Arita had made it clear to the Cabinet
that Japan would not act with unnecessary haste.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
On November 9 the Vice Ministers for War and Navy
894.002
submitted .to a conference of four Cabinet Ministers a
joint.recommendation for administrative reform, the chief
points of which were the following: there should be a
commission of general inquiry, supervision, and coordina-
tion under the jurisdiction of the Prime Minister; the
head of this commission, whether or not a Cabinet member,
should be authorized at times to attend Cabinet meetings
in the capacity of a Cabinet Minister (this point represented
a concession to the opposition to the Army's original plan
of having a Cabinet Minister without Portfolio as the head
of this office); the Foreign Office and the Ministry of
Overseas Affairs should be combined in the interest of a
unified external policy; the Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry and the Ministry of Commerce and Industry should
be combined; foreign trade, fuel policy, electric power,
and other matters should be controlled by a unified office;
aviation, railways, roads, wharves, and all communications
should be under the control of a single Department with
particular stress upon the promotion of civil aviation;
and the Law of the Houses of the Diet, as well as the
Election Law, should be revised for the purpose of renovating
the Diet.*
There was very little discussion in the press of the
Government's plan for state control of electric power
industries
* Embassy's despatch No. 2l41 of November 14, 1936.

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.98)
- 12 -
industries, the matter apparently being considered closed
until the opening of the next Diet Session.* However, a
joint statement urging the Government to leave the manage-
ment of electric power industries in private hands was
issued by executives of the so-called "Big Five" electric
power companies on November 14.
(b). The Diet.
The proposal put forward by undefined military circles
for restriction of the Powers of the Diet** was received with
widespread and intense opposition. Consequently, the War
Minister on November 6 issued a statement in which he denied
that the Army had any intention of reducing the powers of
the Diet. On the same day General Terauchi told Cabinet
members that the Army's policy of giving expression to
political views only through the War Minister would remein
unchanged and he denied knowledge of the source of the pro-
posal for drastic Diet restriction attributed by the press
to the Army. There were evidences of resentment among some
of the younger and more politically-minded officers of the
Army after the issuance of the War Minister's statement.
As already stated in this report, on November 9 the
Vice Ministers for War and Navy presented a joint statement
for administrative reform to a conference of four Cabinet
Ministers which contained a vague proposal for renovation
of the Diet system. This proposal appeared to be a compro-
mise to appease the younger Army element on one hand, and
to agree with the War Minister's statement of November 6
that the Army did not intend to reduce the powers of the
Diet on the other hand.***
Despite
*
Monthly Report for October, Section III (a).
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2ll7 of October 31, 1936.
***
Embassy's despatch No. 2131 of November 14, 1936.

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.98)
13 -
Despite the War Minister's statement of November 6
and the joint statement of the War and Navy Ministers of
November 9, on November 16 the Diet System Investigation
Commission requested that the War Minister appear before
it and explain his ideas on Diet reform. The War Ministry
was strongly opposed to this suggestion, while the political
parties insisted that the War Minister appear before the
Commission. At the close of the month, however, it appeared
that some compromise arrangement would be made to settle
the controversy.*
An Imperial Ordinance was published in the OFFICIAL
GAZETTE of November 9 convoking the 7Oth Session of the
Diet on December 24, 1936.**
(c). The Army.
On November 5 the Army issued one of its regular pam-
***
phlets, the particular purpose of this one being an explana-
tion of the unusually large budget demands of the Army.
Most of the pamphlet is concerned with a recital of the
expenditures on Japanese armaments and a statement explain-
ing the necessity for military preparedness. It reiterates
the  three-fold policy of Japan: (1) the sound development
of Manchukuo"; (2) harmony among China, "Manchukuo", and
Japan; and (3) the consolidation of Japan's position as
the sole stabilizing force in East Asia. The most import-
ant internal political aspect of this Army pamphlet is
that it advocates a totalitarian state as the proper form
of government for Japan.
The Army made a proposal, published in the vernacular
press during the early part of the month, for the enact-
ment of a plan for the mobilization of the nation's
resources
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2163 of November 26, 1936.
Embassy's despatch No. 2129 of November 13, 1936,
***
Embassy's despatch No. 2136 of November 13, 1936.

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
resources to affect all basic industries as well as capital,
labor, transportation, and communication.*
(a). Political Parties.
While individual members of the Minseito and the Sei-
yukai were reported to have endorsed the Japanese-German
anti-Comintern Pact after it had been made public on Novem-
ber 25, the Social Mass Party issued a statement on November 28
opposing the agreement because of the crisis in international
relations confronting Japan.
(e). Reactionaries.
It was reported by the CHUGAI of November 29 that,
stimulated to action by the conclusion of the Japanese-
German anti-Comintern agreement, leaders of several reac-
tionary groups had decided to attempt to combine all rightist
organizations in Japan into one organization, the purpose of
which would be to combat the alleged popular front movement
and the movement of the Minseito and the Seiyukai for the
protection of constitutionalism.
(f). Communists.
Another reported result of the signing of the Japanese-
German anti-Comintern Pact was the announcement on November 29
that the Minister for Justice would instruct chief judges and
chief procurators to exterminate all Communist elements in
Japan. In this connection the JAPAN TIMES of November 30
pointed out that Communism in Japan had already been practi-
cally wiped out and that a further anti-Gommunist drive
would probably lead overzealous judicials and police officials
to mistake liberalism and democracy for Communism.
(g).
* Embassy's despatch No. 2135 of November 13, 1936.

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.99)
- 15 -
(g). The Emperor.
Admiral Saburo Hyakuteke, retired, was formally installed
as Grand Chamberlain to the Emperor, on November 2l, suc-
ceeding Admiral Kantaro Suzuki. It will be recalled that
Admiral Suzuki was seriously wounded by the February 26 in-
surgents and that for some time he had been contemplating
resigring on account of his self-assumed responsibility for
the February 26 Incident.
IV.
COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Nothing to report.
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
According to press reports, on November 27 the Cabinet
approved for presentation at the next session of the Diet a
national budget for the fiscal year 1937-1938 of ¥3,041,000,000,
a record budget in the history of Japan. According to news-
paper estimates, the budget for the next fiscal year, if
approved by the Diet, will be ¥731,000,000 higher than the
budget of ¥2,310,000,000 for the current fiscal year. It
was also estimated by the press that the Army and Navy share
of the budget as approved by the Cabinet is 46% of the total
budget and is ¥350,000,000 higher than the military share for
the fiscal year 1936-1937.
VI. GENERAL ECONCMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Japan's exports for the month were ¥236,877,000 and
imports into this country were ¥2l8,015,000, which represented
a favorable balance of trade of ¥18,862,000 as compared with
a favorable balance for October of ¥58,168,408.*
Various measures were reported to be under consideration
by the Japanese Government for the enhancement of Japanese
foreign
* Figures obtained from the Commercial Attaché's office.

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.99)
- 16 -
foreign trade. The principal measure in this program was
said to be a plan for the establishment of a double-tariff
system by which additional duties of lo0% would be levied
on goods coming from countries with which Japan has nót a
favored-nation agreement or from countries with which Japan
has no trade treaty. It was reported that the purpose of
this project was the prevention of the abrogation of trade
treaties by countries with which Japan has such treaties
and a warning to non-treaty countries that negotiations must
be engaged in with Japan for the conclusion of commercial
treaties.
According to press reports, the Ministry of Commerce
and Industry has decided to submit a foreign trade control
bill and a foreign trade guild bill to the Diet by which a
stricter Government control of Japanese imports and exports
would be established, including the establishment of a com-
mission to harmonize the interests of manufacturers and traders.
It was also reported that there would be presented to the Diet
a bill for the establishment of an export tariff to "encourage
and control the nation's foreign Trade" which would yield
about ¥4,000,000 annually and another bill for the imposition
of a statistics tax which would bring in about ¥5,000,0o0 a
year to be used for the compilation of reliable and extensive
figures on export trade.
(b). Financial Trends.
The money market was active as compared with a sluggish
money market for October; the stock market was quiet, princi-
pally on account of the unsatisfactory progress of the Sino-
Japanese negotiations and the Government tax increase program;
the average yen rate for the month was 0.2850 as compared
with $0.2856 during the month of October.*
(c).
* Information obtained from the Commercial Attaché's Office.

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.99)
- 17 -
(c). Shipping.
The TOKYO NICHI NICHI of November 6 reported that the
number of men employed in shipbuilding had doubled since
1934, that Japanese shipbuilding activity during the current
year would be the greatest since l9l9, and that Japan now
stood third among the maritime nations of the world from
the point of view of ship tonnage.
VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) American and British Oil Interests in Japan.
No satisfactory written confirmation was received
from the Japanese Government of the oral assurances
894.6363
given to the representatives of the American and British
oil interests on September 19, 1936, by Mr. Yoshino, then
Vice Minister for Commerce and Industry.* Furthermore,
the Ministry of Commerce and Industry having adopted an
antagonistic attitude in November, certain other indi-
cations of opposition to and discrimination against the
foreign oil companies became manifest in the first half
of December. Therefore the representatives of the Ameri-
can and British companies came to the conclusion that
little or nothing could be expected from the oral assur-
ances which had been given in good faith by Mr. Yoshino
before his resignation as Vice Minister for Commerce and
Industry.**
Consequently, after consultation between the Ameri-
can and British oil representatives and their respective
Embassies and under instructions received from the Gov-
ernments of the United States and reat Britain, the Ameri-
can and British Embassies on December 24 made substantial-
ly identicel (not joint) representations in writing to the
Japanese Foreign Office in which each Government expressed
its anxiety with regard to the future of its oil interests
in Japan, requested a definite indication from the Japan-
ese Government that the latter does not desire to force
those oil interests out of Japan, and expressly requested
that the Japanese Government give the written assurances
sought
*Embassy's despatch No. 2076 of October 14, 1936.
**Embassy's despatch No. 2194 of December 28, 1936.

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
sought by its oil interests for the continuation of op-
erations in Japan.*
On December 25, the day after the making of repre-
sentations, a report was published by Domei to the effect
that the American Ambassador had notified the Minister
for Foreign Affairs of the intention of the United States
Government to retain 19,000 tons of destroyers in excess
of the London Treaty limits because of Great Britain's
retention of five "B" class cruisers under the authority
of Article 17 of the London Naval Treaty. Since the
American Government's notification of the retention of
destroyers had been made on November 6, there is a
possibility that the Japanese Foreign Office gave out
this version of the Ambassador's conversation with Mr.
Arita in an effort to conceal the fact that representa-
tions had been made by the American and British Govern-
ments on the oil question.
(b) Rctention of Excess Submarine Tonnage by the
Japanese and American Governments.
On December 29 the Japanese Ambassador in Washington
informed the American Government of the formal invoca-
tion by his Government of Article 2l of the London Naval
Treaty for the retention by Japan of 15, 598 tons of excess
submarines. In reply the American Government stat by
note that it would exercise its right to retain a pro-
portionate amount of submarine tonnage and on December 31,
1936, would have 15,230 tons of excess submarines.**

(c) Forthcoming Visit of American Textile Mission.
On December 18 Domei conveyed to the Japanese people,
particularly the cotton textile interests, a message from
Dr. Claudius T. Murchison, head of the unofficial Ameri-
can Textile Mission scheduled to sail for Japan on Decem-
ber 24. The gist of this message was that the Mission,
recognizing the difficulty of solving problems of inter-
national trade by unilateral government action, strongly
desired to achieve mutual understanding between the Ameri-
can and Japanese textile interests, which would contribute
toward a continuance of mutually profitable trade relations
and a strengthening of good will between the two countries.
According to the Japanese press of Kobe and Osaka,
industrial circles in Osaka seemcd to feel that the coming
Mission was only a tourist party and that few if any tan-
gible benefits could be derived from the visit of the
Mission.*

() Soviet Russia.
761.94
The Soviet-Japanese situation remained greatly ag-
gravated until the latter part of the month in consequence
of the refusal by the USSR on November 20 to sign the
new Fisheries Treaty, an act taken in retaliation against
the conclusion of the Japanese-German anti-Comintern agree-
ment.** On December 2l, however, representatives of the
two Governments agreed to the extension for one year or
until December 31, 1937, of the 1928 Fisheries Treaty.***
This agreement, which was finally signed in Moscow on
December 28, relieved the immediate tension between the
two countries. Nevertheless, there remained unsettled
the question of a new and more lasting fisheries treaty
and the problem of establisning mutually acceptable border
commissions. Furthermore, the Sovict Union remained con-
vinced that the pact between Japan and Germany against the
Communist Internationale was designed as a direct threat
against the security of Soviet Russia, despite repeated
Japanese denials. And prominent Soviet officials pro-
fessed the belief that Japan had instigated both the
Suiyuan hostilities and the Sian uprising in China.

to raise their respective Legations to Embassies and
of Poland's geographical position in consideration of
the Japanese-German anti-Comintern agreement.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). American Oil Interests.
Early in January the Standard and Shell gasoline quotas
for Korea for the first half of 1937 were considerably
894.6363
reduced.* and on January 9 Mr. Dickover, First Secretary
of the Embassy, made an oral and informal approach to Mr.
Matsushima, Chief of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs of
the Foreign Office, on this matter, calling to his atten-
tion the general representations made on December 4 with
regard to American oil interests in Japan,** reciting briefly
the facts of the Korean situation, and stating that these
developments were very disheartening to the American oil
interests. Sir George Sansom, the Commercial Counselor of
the British Embassy,also approached Mr. Matsushima informally
with regard to the Korean quotas.***
Replying to the Embassy's formal representations of
December 24, the Japanese Foreign Office on January 15
delivered a note verbale to the Embassy in which it was
stated that the Japanese Government had no intention of
discriminating between the business operations of the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company and those of any other oil
companies, that the Japanese Government had no thought
whatever of driving the American oil interests out of
Japan, and that the Japanese Ministry of Commerce and
Industry had intended a certain letter to be a confirmation
of the four-point oral assurances given to the representa-
tives of the American and British oil interests on Sep-
tember 19, 1936, by Mr. Yoshino, then Vice Minister for
Commerce and Industry.**** At about the same time the
British Embassy received a similar reply to its representa-
tions of December 24. These replies were considered by
both
Embassy's telegram No. 3, January 8, 1937.
**
Embassy's telegrams No. 264 of December 19, 1936, and
No. 268 of December 24, 1936.
***
Embassy's despatch No. 2233 of January 29, 1937..
****
Embassy's telegram No. 6 of January 15, 1937.

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
both the American and British Embassies to be satisfactory.
On the other hand, the Japanese Government's replies to the
points regarding kerosene quotas and the proposed revised
tariff were not so satisfactory as the general assurances
mentioned above.
(b). Visit to Japan of the American Textile Mission.
An unofficial American Textile Mission, headed by Dr.
Claudius T. Murchison, President of the Cotton Textile
Institute, visited Japan from January 8 to January 28. On
January 22 a mutually satisfactory agreement was signed by
the Mission and by Japanese cotton textile interests.*
This agreement establishes a quota for l937 for cotton piece
goods or piece goods of which the principal material is
cotton of 155 million square yards for shipment to the
United States or else of the volume of contracts for such
shipments on hand on January 21, 1937, depending on which
amount is smaller, and a quota for 1938 of 100 million.
square yards with the proviso that 25% of this amount may
be added to the 1937 quota and deducted from the 1938 quota.
The 1937 quota is based on the approximate amount of un-
filled orders for shipment to the United States in the hands
of Japanese mills on January 2l. A decidedly favorable im-
pression was created by the visit of the American Textile
Mission, which was due in large measure to the charactor
and personality of the American business men who composed
the Mission.**
(c). Difficulties Encountered by American Consular
Officers in Procuring Commercial Information
In Japan.
In an interview with Mr. Matsushima, Chief of the
Bureau of Commercial Affairs of the Foreign Office, on
January 15, Mr. Dickover, First Secretary of the Embassy,
explained
Imbassy's telegram No. 27 of January 25, 1937.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2235 of January 29, 1937.

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.95)
- 3 -
explained the Department's views concerning the difriculties
sometimes experienced by American consular ofricers in
Japan in obtaining ordinary commercial information.* In
brief, Mr. Dickover stated that the American Government must
reserve the right for its consular officers in Japan to
endeavor to obtain through all lawful and appropriate
sources information distinctly not of a military character,
even though the Embassy might find it desirable from time
to time to avail itself of Mr. Matsushima's offer of the
special services of one of his Bureau's secretaries for
the collection and preparation. of commercial information.**
(d). Proposed Japanese Economic Mission to the
United States.
It was reported during the month that the Japan Economic
Federation had decided to send an economic mission to the
United States in the spring of 1937 to return the visit of
the unofficial American Economic Mission which came to Japan
in April, 1935, under the chairmanship of Mr. W. Cameron
Forbes.***
II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
77
According to press reports, the Japanese Government has
decided to resume the negotiations with the Chinese Govern-
ment which had been broken off during the early part of
December.****
Apparently, little was known concerning the
basis on which the negotiations would be resumed. However,
there was some discussion of a "re-orientation" of Japan's
policy toward China based on economic lines. For example,
Mr. Toyotaro Yuki, then newly appointed President of the
Tokyo Chamber of Commerce and Industry, reportedly stated on
January
*
Department's instruction No. 1147 of November 25, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2223 of January 21, 1937.
*丰*
Embassy's despatch No. 1277 of May 3, 1935.
Embassy's despatch No. 2190 of December 17, 1936.

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
January 17 that a readjustment of Sino-Japanese relations
must be made from the angle of economic relations. In
this connection it was reported that plans were being made
to despatch an economic mission to China to return the visit
to Japan of the Chinese Economic Mission in the autumn of
1935. Mr. Kenji Kodama, former President of the Yokohama
Specie Bank, was mentioned as the probable head of this
Mission and also as Japan's "special economic ambassador"
to China.
(b). Soviet Russia.
EE
Criticism of Mr. Arita's policy toward Soviet Russia
761.94
continued during the month and was not abated by Mr. Arita's
public statements in explanation.* In a New Year's message
he stated that Japan's position as the stabilizing factor
in the Far East had been enhanced as the result of the
anti-Comintern agreement with Germany and that Japan desired
to pursue friendly relations with the Soviet Union. Mr.
Arita made two similar pronouncements, the first in a radio
broadcast on January 8 and the second in his speech before
the Diet on January 2l. None of these statements satisfied
the popular demand for a clarification of Japan's Soviet
policy.**
It was reported that Mr. Yureneff, the Soviet Ambassador
to Japan, would probably resign on account of his failure
to learn of the anti-Comintern agreement sooner than he did.
Mutual suspicion was increased by two developments
during the month, one being the detention at Vladivostok
of the Japanese steamship SIBERIA MARU on a suspicion of
espionage, the other being the accusation brought out in
the trial in Moscow of Radek and sixteen other alleged
conspirators against the Soviet Government to the effect
that Japan had organized train wrecks in Soviet Russia and
was connected with the "Trotsky Center".

Embassy's despatch No. 2219 of January 16, 1937.
Embassy's despatch No. 2237 of February 1, 1937.

(c). Great Britain.
There was no amelioration in the strained relations
between Japan and Great Britain.*
Apprehension was expressed by Japanese newspapers over
the reported movement in Great Britain, Australia, New
Zealand, and other British territories toward the banning
of Japanese shipping from British inter-port routes.
WE
Newspaper reports stated that, in addition to the
economic missions to be sent to the United States and
China, the Japan Economic Federation planned to send an
economic mission to Great Britain to return the visit of
the Barnby Mission to this country in September and Oct-
ober 1934.**
(a). Chile.
LA
The newspapers reported that the Chilean Government
had decided to send an economic mission to Japan which
would leave Chile for this country in April of this year
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Diet.
The much-heralded campaign of opposition against the
Government on the part of the political parties*** took
shape with the re-convening of the Diet on January 2l. In
an interpellation in the House of Representatives, Mr.
Kunimatsu Hamada of the Seiyukai, a former Speaker of the
Lower House, violently attacked the Hirota Cabinet and also
the military for their interference in politics.****
General Terauchi, the Minister for War, replied to Mr.
Hamada
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2239 of February 1, 1937;
Department's telegram No. 9 of January 16; Embassy's
telegram No. 8 of January 18; Embassy's telegram No.
14 of January 22; Embassy's telegram No. 26 of January 25.
Embassy's despatch No. l040 of November 2, 1934.
Embassy's Monthly Report for December, Section III (c).
Embassy's telegrmm No. 13 of January 22, 1937.

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.97)
Hamada in an inept and clumsy fashion, the session ending
in confusion and disorder.* Owing to the antagonism between
the Government and the political parties (especially between
the Army and the parties) the Diet was prorogued for two
days and on January 23 the Cabinet resigned,** teportedly
because of the Army's demand that either the Diet be dis-
solved or the Cabinet resign. Upon the Cabinet's deciding
against dissolution, the Minister for War tendered his
resignation, thus causing the fall of the Cabinet.
(b). The Cabinet.
After the resignation of the Hirota Cabinet, the
Emperor on January 24 requested General Kazunari Ugaki,
the well-known liberal, to form a Cabinet.*** Ostensibly
on the ground that the Army would not be capable of en-
forcing discipline with an Ugaki Cabinet in power, the
Army refused to furnish a War Minister for the proposed
Cabinet and therefore on January 29 General Ugaki reported
to the Emperor that he was unable to form a Government.
There was a strong undercurrent of criticism among the
people of the Army's obstruction of General Ugaki, who
undoubtedly was the nation's choice as Premier. According
to the press, Baron Hiranuma, reported to have fascist ten-
dencies, was asked to form a Cabinet but stated that he
was unable to accept the responsibility of the Premiership.
On January 29 General Senjuro Hayashi, retired, War Minister
in the Okada Cabinet until his resignation on September 4,
1935, was selected to form a Cabinet, which task it was
believed would not be difficult in view of the Army's support
of the Premier-designate.****
(c).
*
Embassy's despatch No. 224l of February 1, 1937.
Embassy's telegram No. 21 of January 23, 1937.
丰丰拿
Embassy's telegram No. 22 of January 24, 1937.
Embassy's telegram No. 34 of January 30, 1937.

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.99)

(c). The Army.
Although the Army met with several rebuffs during
894.20
January, there was no indication at the end of the month
that the Army's predominant position in the political
affairs of the country had been lessened thereby.* In
fact, the Army seemed to have gained a victory in the seleo-
tion by the Emperor of General Hayashi for Premier .** On
the other hand, it appeared that Hayashi had been chosen to
form a Government because it was thought that he would be
able to control the Army and through moderate policies to
minimize friction between the Army and the Government.
Immediately after tendering his resignation as War
Minister on January 23, General Terauchi told the press that
he had taken this step because the views of the political
parties were found to be fundamentally at variance with those
held by the Army, denying the allegation that Mr. Hamada's
speech in the House of Representatives had been the cause for
his resigning. General Terauchi reportedly added that the
domestic and international situation confronting Japan was
too grave to be tided over by any sort of temporizing com-
promise between the political parties and the Army.
The Army issued one of its regular pamphlets on January 27
entitled "The Armies of the Empire and Other Powers". After
the usual preamble in support of the continuing necessity
for increased military expenditures, the pamphlet goes on
to state that the Soviet Union is manifesting a provocative
attitude toward Japan, that anti-Japanese and anti-Manchu-
kuo" policies and the policy of playing off one foreign
Power against another remain as China's guiding principles,
that Great Britain is extending its influence over China
and interfering in Japan's policy in East Asia, and that the
United States
* Embassy's despatch No. 2241 of February 1, 1937.
** Embassy's telegram No. 34 of January 30, 1937.

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
United States is desirous of "controlling the Pacific",
advanoing on the China market, strengthening its naval
power and its Pacific fortifications, and expanding its
overseas market to keep pace with its economic revival.
(a). The Navy.
Admiral Nagano, the Navy Minister, was reported to have
attempted to mediate between the Army and the political
parties after their clash in the Diet on January 2l. His
efforts met with no success and his visits to the presidents
of the Seiyukai and the Minseito were said to have caused
indignation in the Army.
(e). Political Parties.
Toward the close of the month a movement was reported
to be under way for the formation of a new political party
of a reactionary character, the object of which would be a
recognition of the Army's existing strength in politics and
a uniting of the Army with political bodies. According to
newspaper reports, this movement had its incentive in the
desire of General Tatekawa, retired, to bring about a
grouping of military and other patriotic socicties and was
supported by several prominent men, including Prince Konoe,
Yonezo Maeda and Chikuhei Nakajima of the Seiyukai, Keikichi
Tanomogi of the Minseito, Tetsunosuke Yamazaki of the Showakai,
and Count Hideo Kodama, a member of the House of Peers.
(f). Reactionaries.
According to Domei, on January 23 executive members
of the Current Situation Council, an organization composed
of representatives of the Japan Production Party and other
rightist groups, addressed a communication to General Tera-
uchi expressing gratitude for his endeavors in the political
crisis and also a communication to Admiral Nagano suggest-
ing that he resign from the position of Navy Minister because
cf his failure to cooperate with the Army.
(g).

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.99)
(g). Communists.
Lifting a press ban, the Government announced on
January ll that twenty-five members of a communist-anarchist
organization had been indicted for having plotted terrorist
uprisings in agricultural districts of Japan during the
summer of 1935.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Nothing to report.
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
EA
The financial situation in Japan during January was
894.51
less favorable than it had been for a number of years.*
However, conditions have not as yet become serious and the
prevailing opinion seemed to be that the Government would
be able to avoid a dangerous crisis for some months and
perhaps through the year l937.** There were the following
favorable factors; the national debt, although on the rise,
did not appear to be excessive fornation of the size and
power of Japan; money rates were easy; there was little
or no currency inflation; and the unfavorable visible
foreign trade balance for l936 was partly offset by a
favorable invisible trade balance.
On the other hand, there were unfavorable factors,
principally the unbalanced budget and the consequent large
issues of "deficit bonds" necessary in 1937. The weakness
of the yen and apprehension of the further pressure which
would result from large speculative imports expected in
anticipation of the proposed increased tariff rates were
the causes of the foreign exchange control regulations
announced by the Government and put into force on January 8.
The adverse factors in Japan's financial position were due
in great measure to the unusually heavy governmental ex-
penditures, principally military.
Strong
Embassy's telegram No. 5 of January 13, 1937.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2209 of January 1, 1937.

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.98)
- 10 -
Strong opposition to the Cabinet's financial polioy,
principally to the proposed tax reform, continued to be
expressed. In fact, the widespread dissatisfaction over
the Government's financial policy formed an important part
of the strong opposition to the Government which brought
about the Hirota Cabinet's downfall toward the close of the
month.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Exports from Japan in January amounted to ¥200,304,000
and imports into this country were ¥304,126,000, as compared
with December exports of ¥296,817,000 and imports of ¥284,
515,O00. This represented an unfavorable balance of trade
of ¥l03,822,000, as compared with an unfavorable balance for
December of only ¥12,302,000.
(b). Financial Trends.
The outstanding feature of the month was the sharp rise
Amace
in commodity prices, the Bank of Japan's wholesale price
index number for January being 233.3 as against 214.9 for
December l936. .Bond and stock prices appreciated. In the
latter part of the month the activity of the stock market
decreased owing to the Cabinet crisis. The average yen rate
of the Yokohama Specie Bank was 0.28625 as compared with
0.28552 for December. It should be noted that the main-
tenance of the yen at a slightly higher level during January
was due to the fact that the exchange was artifioinlly
bolstered by the Yokohama Specie Bank and that strict
exchange control was enforced.
(c). Industries.
The JAPAN TIMES of January 9 reported that for the year
1936 Japan had led the world in the production of rayon yarn,
with an output of 275,500,000 pounds as compared with the
United States output of 270,000,000 pounds.
(a).

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.97)
-11 -
(a). Shipping.
It was reported on January 5 that an agreement had been
reached on December 31, l936, between the Osaka Shosen Kaisha
(Osaka Mercantile Steamship Company) and the Kokusai Kisen
Kaisha (International Steamship Company) by virtue of which
the Osaka Shosen Kaisha will have the complete management
Cmmern
of the other steamship company. The amalgamation of the
two companies was expected to cause serious competition
with the Nippon Yusen Kaisha (Japan Mail Steamship Company).
VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.

IX. LABOR.
The JAPAN CHRONICLE of January 20 reported that 30,O00
employees of the Yawata Iron Works, of the Nippon Iron Com-
pany, had demanded that their employers grant them special
allowances or else an increase in wages in view of the rise
of commodity prices.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). Premier Hayashi's Reference to American-
Japanese Relations in Diet Speech.
In the section of his speech before the House of Peers
711.9
on February 15 devoted to foreign affairs, Premier Hayashi
stated: "Japan's policy of promoting friendship with Great
Britain and the United States is immutable. Between the
former country and ours there exist questions of various
sorts requirine adjustment". The Premier made no mention
of questions pending between the United States and Japan.
Several days prior to the delivery of this speech it had been
intimated to the Embassy that General Hayashi would purposely
not mention pending questions between the two countries since
he was desirous of conveying the impression that Japanese-
American relations are satisfactory.*
(b).
Reaction in Japan to the American Naval Building
Program.
The reaction in Japan to the recent announcement of
America's navel building procram was a quiet one. The an-
nouncement brought forth no official reply from the Japanese
Navy and received little attention in the Diet interpellations.
However, in answer to an interpellation in the Diet the Navy
Minister asserted that Japan had no intention of building up
to parity with "the strongest of naval powers". There was
very little editorial comment on America's new building plans.
The JAPAN TIMES of February 2O and the OSAKA MAINICHI of
February 18 were in agreement that Japan need not attempt to
build its navy up to parity with that of the United States
but
*
Erbassy's telegram No. 57 of February 15, 1937.

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
but must only maintain a navy strong enough to remain supreme
in the Western Pacific. The latter newspaper added that if
Great Britain's new naval building program should stimulate
the Uuited States to fresh building, the Japanese Navy might
finally be obliged to build more ships to meet the situation
proportionately.
In this connection a previous editorial in the JAPAN
TIMES (February 13) is of interest. It described the present
foreign policy of the President of the United States as one
of non-interference with Japan in the Far East under any
circumstances.*
(c). Final Ratification by Japanese and American
EA
Interests of the Cotton Textile Agreement.
On February 7 the Association of Exporters of Cotton
Cloth to the United States ratified the agreement concluded
at Osaka on January 22 by the unofficial American Textile
Mission and Japanese cotton textile interests.** The four
other organizations that took part in the negotiations held
from January 15 to January 22 had already approved the agree-
ment.***
Approval was given to the agreement on February 17
by the American Cotton Textile Institute, which had sponsored
the American Textile Mission.
(a). Forthcoming Visit to the United States of an
Unofficial Economic Mission.
It will be remembered that the press reported in January
that the Japan Economic Federation had decided to send an
economic mission to the United States in the spring of 1937
the visit of
to return the unofficial American Economic Mission which came
to Japan in April 1935 under the chairmanship of Mr. W.
Cameron Forbes.**** This report was confirmed on February
12

would visit the United States in April.** On February 22
Mr. Shokyuro Kadono, Vice President of the Okura interests,
accepted the chairmanship of the Mission which, it is under-
stood, will visit Great Britain in addition to the United
States. According to the OSAKA MAINICHI of February 23, the
Japanese Economic Mission will endeavor to readjust Japanese-
American trade relations in general without dealing with any
individual item of trade and will also stress the importance
of redistributing world resources.
(e).
American Government's Attitude toward Smuggling
in North China.
On February 8 the Ambassador took up orally and informally
with the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs the question of
smuggling into North China, stating that the situation still
adversely affected American trade in general and that the
situation continued to be of concern to the American Govern-
ment.*** The Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs made his
usual comment in reply to the effect that an improvement in
the situation mi, ht be expected when Chinese customs tariffs
were reduced.

Having arrived in Japan on January 3l, President Quezon,
accompanied by General MacArthur and twenty-five others,
sailed for the United States on February 2. During his
brief stay in this country President Quezon was presented
to the Emperor by the Ambassador and attended a luncheon
given by the Emperor in his honor.**** At an official dinner
given by lir. Arita the gracious and laudatory remarks con-

(g). American and Jananese Interest in the Open Door
In the Congo Basin.
In accordance with the Department's instructions,** the
Embassy inquired of the Foreien Office on February 8 con-
cerning the attitude of the Japanese Government toward the
continuance of the Open door in the Congo Basin. On Feb-
ruary 16 the Chief of the Treaty Bureau of the Foreign Office
told a member of the Embassy staff that he was in a position
informally to state the opinion of the Japanese Government
that it was interested in the maintenance of the open door
in the Congo Basin and would like to see the St. Germain
Convention perpetuated and similar conventions adopted else-
where in Africa.*** On Februery 24 the Embassy informed the
Foreign Office informally that the American Government's
interests in the maintenance of the provisions of the St.
Germain Convention providing for equal commercial opportunity
and the open door was based solely on the fact that those
provisions were in direct harmony with the broad commercial
policy pursued by the American Government, and that the
American Government did not intend to initiate any teps
for a revision of the Convention.****

II. RELATIONS WITI OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a).
China.
7931941
There was further evidence during the month that Japan
was contemplating an alteration of i ts former aggressive
policy toward China and the adontion of conciliatory tactics
with a view to arriving at some sort of an economic rapproche-
ment.* A report in the ASAHI of February 6 stated that the
Army hoped that Japan's diplomacy toward China would tend
toward a strengthening of economic cooperation based on
racial harmony.** In his speech before the Diet on February
15 Premier Hayashi said that it was urgent ly necessary to
cultivate cordial feelins between the two countries, to
foster mutual appreciation, to brin about closer contact
not only between the two Governments but also between the
two peoples, and to achieve in more concrete form "cooperation
and mutual assistance". Many of the interpellations made
by members of the Diet urged the adontion by the Government
of a more sympathetic attitude toward China and Premier
Hayashi's replies lent weight to the conciliatory expres-

Japanese
Monthly report for January, Section II (a).
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2266 of February 16, 1937.

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.99)

Japanese press reported that the Government would abandon
its political desiderata with regard to China and would in
future pursue two aims, both economic in character: the
reduction of the Chinese tariff and the establishment of
an air service between Japan and China.
(b). Soviet Russia.
EF
Although the Soviet Government continued to obstruct
761.94
the Japanese-Soviet shipping service and traffic on the
international railway, it denied having any intention of
interference in these matters and contended that the measures
already taken had been largely in retaliation for similar
acts on the part of the Japanese.* It seemed probable,
however, that the USSR had chosen the present time to take
such steps for the purpose of indirectly protesting against
the Japenese-German anti-Comintern agreement.
In cortrast to his conciliatory remarks about China,
Premier Hayashi's statements concerning the Soviet Union in
his speech before the Diet on February 15 were uncompro-
mising.** He asserted that the USSR must "correctly compre-
hend Japan's position in East Asia" and urged that that
country take a broad view and cooperate with Japan.
On the other hand, several interpellations were made
in the Diet advocating a more friendly policy toward Soviet
Russia, one interpellator going so far as to suggest that
Japan join hands with that country to present a strong front
against Great Britain.***
The most significant interpellation
regarding Russia was that of Mr. Ozaki, who questioned the
wisdom of the German-Japanese anti-Comintern agreement and
suggested as an alternative a re-examination of the Soviet
proposal

Great Britain.
WE
The Keelung incident remained unsettled.** Another
outstandin issue was settled, however, with the acceptance
by the Japanese Foreign Office of the British proposal for
a solution of the perpetual leasehold question in Japan.***
A report that Sir Robert Clive, British Ambassador to
Japan, had been transferred as Ambassador to Belgium was
confirmed on February 26. On his departure this spring
Sir Robert Clive will have served as Ambassador in Tokyo
for nearly three years.
(a). Italy.
The Italo-Jenanese Institute, a cultural organization,
held its inaugural meeting in Tokyo on February 1l at which
Baron Kishichiro Okura was elected president and the Italian
Ambassador to Japan was elected honorary president. It is
reported thet a plan is progressing for the establishment
of a Japanese-Italian Institute in Rome and that the
activities of the two institutes will be the exchange of
professors and students between Italy and Japan, the holding
of lecture meetings, the promotion of scientific study,
et cetera.

(e). The Netherlands.
Accordins, to a news item in the OSAKA MAIIICHI of
February 21, the Government has decided to send Mr. Kazue
Kuwashima, recently appointed Japanese Linister to The
Hague, on a tour or investigation in the Hetherland East
Indies with a view to alleviating the tense trade situation
existing between Japan and thos: colonies. Mr. Kuwashima
left Tokyo en route to the Netherland East Indies on
February 28.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
A Cabinet under the Premiership of General Senjuro
894.002
Hayashi was installed in office on February 2, consisting
of the following members:
Minister for Education, General Hayashi (concurrently)
Minister for Overseas Affairs, General Hayashi
(concurrently)

Navy Minister, Admiral Mitsumesa Yonai

Minister for Communications, Count Hideo Kodama
Minister for Agriculture and Forestry, Mr. Tatsunosuke
Yamasaki.

Minister for Railways, Admiral Godo (concurrently)
General Nakamura, who was appointed only as a stop-gap War
Minister, was succeeded on February 1o by General Gen
Sugiyama.*
The
* Embassy's despatch No. 2268 of February 18, 1937.

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
The Hayashi Cabinet's first statement of policy,
issued on February 8, was nebulous and ambiguous.* A more
significant statement of policy was contained in the
Premier's speech before the Diet of February 15.** The
part of this speech which dealt with internal political
affairs was conciliatory in tone and indicated a desire
to cooperate with the Diet as far as possible. Those
sections of the speech which dealt with foreign affairs
and Government finance are discussed in other parts of
the present report.
(b). The Army.
General Ugaki's efforts to form a Cebinet during the
latter part of January having failed because of the Army's
opposition, particularly the opposition of the faction led
by General Itagaki, Chief of Staff of the Kwantung Army,
Premier-designate Hayashi was faced with the problem of
finding a War Minister suitable to the Army and also to
the political parties. At first he chose General Itagaki,
who was acceptabie neither to the political parties nor to
the so-called moderate "Big Three" of the Army (Generals
Sugiyama, Terauchi, Umezu). Therefore General Nakamura was
chosen to tide over the situation and General Sugiyama was
appointed War Minister on February 10, with General Umezu
as Vice Minister for Var.*** The fact that the chauvinist
Itagaki groun was excluded from membership in the Cabinet
cannot be interpreted to mean that the influence of this
group has been materially lessened. In the first place,
Itagaki was Hayashi's first choice for War Minister. Further-
more
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2256 of February 15, 1937.
**
Embassy's telegram No. 57 of February 15, 1937.
***Embassy's despatch No. 2268 of February 18, 1937.

more, it is likely that the assignment of several of the
Itagaki group to high Division commands, as announced in
the regular periodic shifts of Army officers on February 24,*
reflected Premier Hayashi's respect for this faction.
No untoward incidents were reported to have taken place
on the anniversary of the February 26 Insurrection. Accord-
ing to Domei of February 24, the police authorities issued
orders prohibiting all public meetines or functions of any
sort in commemoration of the event with the exception of
memorial services to be held by the families of the victims
of the revolt. Domei also stated that the police had ob-
served a tendency toward public approval of the rebels'
action and had even heard praise of the insurrectionaries.
On February 24 memorial services were held for Mr. Takahashi,
Viscount Saito, and Ceneral Watanabe, who were assassinated
by the insurgents. The HOCHI, the YOMIURI, and THE JAPAN
TIMES published editorials on February 26 deprecating the
Incident.
(c).
The Diet.
Having been prorogued on January 21, the Diet was
reconvened by Premier Hayashi on February 15. In a series
of frank interpellations the Government was assailed on a
number of issues.** The most outstanding interpellation of
the liberal Diet element was that of Mr. Yukio Ozaki on
February 17, while the most chauvinistic interpellation was
that of Mr. Seico Nakano on the following day.*** Desirous
of having its budget passed and of avoiding the contingency
of Diet dissolution, the Government maintained a conciliatory
attitude throughout the month.****
While attacking the
Government

Government vigorously, the political parties showed no

(d). Political Parties.
It became apparent during the month that General Ita-
gaki and his ultra-chauvinistic croup in the Army were
backing the movement for the formation of a new reactionary
political party.* Having learned through the circumstances
which surrounded the downfall of the Hirota Cabinet that
the political parties can at times be troublesome, the Army
has apparently come to the conclusion that its best policy
will be to develop a single nationalist party for control
of the Diet.** There are indications, however, that the
leaders of the new party movement do not look for ultimate
success for a number of years to come.
(e). The Navy.
The part played by the Navy in thwarting General Hayashi's
plan to include General Itagaki and Admiral Suetsugu, both
chauvinists, in his Cabinet was discussed in the Embassy's
despatch No. 2268 of February 18. A further evidence of the
moderating influence of the Navy may be found in the follow-
ing conditions laid down by Admiral Yonai for his acceptance
of the Navy portfolio, as reported by the JAPAN CHRONICLE of
February 2: all persons, military and civilian, must con-
scientiously refrain from interfering in political matters
of no concern to them; sudden changes in political and
economic organization must be avoided and all reforms must
be progressive; foreign policy must emanate fror one source;
and Japan's policy toward China must be re-examined in order

(f). Reactionaries.
According to Domei, members of the Japan Production
Party, a reactionary organization without Diet representa-
tion, called on Mr. Yukio Ozaki on February 22 and recom-
mended that he take responsibility for the "anti-Army"
statements conteined in his interpellation of February 17
and resien his position as a member of the House of Repre-
sentatives.
IV.
COURSE OF IEGISLATICN.
On account of the time taken up by interpellations no
legislative projects were considered by the Diet other than
the Government's budget bill, discussions on which started
on February 17.
Of the bills reported to have been presented
to the Diet thus far the following may be mentioned: bills
for the revision of the Military Secrets Preservation Law,
the Conscription Law, the Patent Law, and the Law Governing
the Organization of the Courts of Justice; and a bill pro-
viding for an increase in taxation. Accordine to the JAPAN
CHRONICLE of February 24, the bill for the revision of the
Military Secrets Preservation Law (enacted in 1899) contains
clearer definitions of military secrets, stricter provisions
for the prevention of organized espionage and of the acti-
vities of spies, new provisions for the prohibition of the
entry

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.98)
-13-
entry of persons into certain specified zones to preserve
secrets relative to exercises, experiments, and researches,
and more stringent regulations against the entry of foreign
vessels into naval ports and forbidden ports and bays. It
will be recalled that a more drastic bill along similar lines
failed of passage in the May 1-May 27 Diet session of 1936.*
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
On February 2, Mr. Toyotaro Yuki was installed as
Finance Minister in the Hayashi Cabinet and has generally
been considered as the strong man of the Cabinet. The
appointment of Mr. Yuki, in whom the nation apparently has
confidence as a conservative financier in contrast to his
predecessor, Dr. Baba, soon began to make itself felt in
an easing of monetary tension.** Mr. Eigo Fukai formally
resigned as Governor of the Bank of Japan on February 8 and
was succeeded by Mr. Seihin Ikeda,*** formerly managing
director of the Mitsui organization. This appointment was
interpreted by a Diet interpellator as a step toward finan-
cial fascism.****
A budget for the fiscal year 1937-1938 amounting to
approximately ¥2,770,000,000 was approved by the Cabinet on
February 12 and on February 17 was presented to the Diet for
its consideration. Although this ficure represents a decrease
of ¥270,000,000, or approximately 9%, as compared with the
budget approved by the Hirota Cabinet, it is still l9% bigher
than the actual budget for 1936-37. Furthermore, the military
appropristions in the Hirota Cabinet's proposed budget have
not been reduced in the present budget estimates. It is

Largely because of the necessity of obtaining the Diet's

by the Army and the Navy, Premier Hayashi has had to approach
the Diet in a friendly and conciliatory attitude.
The
political parties criticized the Government in the Diet on
the grounds that the proposed military appropriations were
too large and that there was too great a disproportion
between these expenditures and those set aside for stabiliza-

Cabinet's budgetary plans.* At the close of the month it
was generally conceded by competent observers that the
budget presented by the Hayashi Cabinet would be passed by
the Diet.
VI.
GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL COIDITIONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Japan's exports for February amounted to ¥225,654,000
and imports into this country: amounted to ¥302,434,000,
as compared with January exports of ¥200,304,000 and imports
of ¥304,126,000. Thus there was
an unfavorable balance of
trade of ¥96,780,000 as compared with an unfavorable balance
for January of ¥103,822,000.
(b).
* Embassy's despatch No. 2293 of March 1, 1937.

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
(b). Financial Trends.
The stock market was active and the price of shares
appreciated considerably; few Government bonds and corporation
debentures were issued owing to uncertainty concerning the
political situetion; commodity prices declined slightly as
compared with the previoua month. The Bank of Japan's whole-
sale price index number was 230.4, which represented a
decrease of 2.9 as compared with January; the money market
was active; and the average yen rate of the Yokohama Specie
Bank was $0.28521, as compared with $0.28625 for January.
(c). Shipping.
WE
According to the OSAKA MAINICHI of February 7, the report
EA
of the Imperial British Shipping Committee is expected to
contain a recommendation for the closing of the Indian coastal
shipping trade to Japanese ships and officials of the Japanese
Ministry of Communications have stated that the adoption of
such a measure would be tantamount to an economic blockade
of Japan.
The ASAHI of February 13 reported that the Kochu Koshi,
an organization founded by the South Manchuria Railway Company
for the development of North China, had decided to establish
the Tangku Transport Company with a capitalization of
¥3,000,o00 for the importation from North China of salt,
iron ore, coal, and raw cotton. It is understood that the
motive behind the Kochi Koshi's plan is a desire to acquire
the resources necessary for helping to put Japan on a semi-
wartime footing.

DX. LABOR.
The OSAKA MAINICHI of February 4 reported that the
Japanese delegation to the International Labor Conference
to be held at Geneva in May would oppose the 40-hour working
week, one of the priicipal efenda of the conference. In
addition Japanese employers were stated by the JAPAN CHRONICLE
of February 17 to have decided against the raising of the
minimum age for factory workers.
Four crews of the Tosa Steamship Company went on strike
on February 9 allegedly because the Company had urged its
seamen to renounce their membership in the Japan Seamen's
Union. The Union presented demands to the Tosa Steamship
Company for the adoption of a minimum wage system, the
establishment of a retirement allowance system, and an in-
crease of wages twice a year. Holding himself responsible
for these labor difriculties, Mr. Tamikichi Kobayashi,
Managing Director of the Company, committed suicide on
February 16. According to the JAPAII CHRONICLE of February 19,
Mr. Kobayashi had been in disagreement with the President
of the Company over the latter's insistence that he take a
strong attitude toward the strikers.
A strike of a distinctly different character was started
on February 28 by another scamen's union, the ieirokai
(Enlightened Serene Society), which charged the Nippon Yusen
Kaisha (Japan Mail Steamship Company) with "lèse majesté"
for not having hoisted flags on its vessels at the time of
the naval review held off
Osaka last fall. According to
Domei, the striking union demanded that the Nippon Yusen
Kaisha apologize through the newspapers for its "lèse majesté"
and immediately renovate the Company's policy on the basis
of the guiding spirit of "Japanism".
X. MISCELLANEOUS. Nothing to report.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). Settlement of the Perpetual Lease Question.
The perpetual lease question was settled between the
Japanese and American Governments on March 25 with the
signature and exchange of notes by the Japanese Minister
for Foreign Affairs and the Anerican Ambassador.* According
to the terms of the agreement, the system of perpetual
leases as regards American leaseholders will terminate
on April 1, 1942, when the leaseholds shall, without com-
pensation, be converted into the rights of ownership under
Japanese laws and ordinances, the status quo regarding
taxation of leaseholders being preserved until March 31,
1942.**
(b).
Japanese Salmon Fishing in American and
Canadian Waters.
On March 2O the CIUGAI SHOGYO SHIMBUN published the
text of a pamphlet issued by the Kaiyo Gyogyo Shinko Kyokai
(The Open Seas Fishery Development Association), a society
founded in May, l936, which is reputed to have the backing
of the Japanese Navy.*** The pamphlet upholds the alleged
riht of the Jananese to take salmon in the northeast Pacific
outside territorial waters and requests the Japanese Govern-
ment to investigate and deliberate on the whole question of
salmon fishing.****
(c). Unofficial Japanese Economic Mission to the
United States.
On March 18 Baron Seinosuke Goh issued a statement
concerning the unofficial Japanese Economic Mission scheduled
to denart for the United States on April 28 ***** in which

(f).
American-Japanese Relations in Connection with
Sino-Japanese Relations.
In a talk with the Ambassador on March 5, Mr. Sato,
the new Minister for Foreign Affairs, stated that in his
opinion Japanese-American relations as well as the relations
between Japan and other countries must be largely influenced
by Japan's relations with China. The Minister for Foreign
Affairs explained that he wanted to improve Sino-Japanese
relations and thereby improve Japan's relations with the
rest of the world.***
II. RELATIONS VITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
Although Japan's recently announced intention of adopting
a more conciliatory and more equitable policy toward China
has relieved the tension between the two countries, nothing
of a concrete nature has been effected toward a rapprochement
and nothing hes been done for the settlement of such out-
standing issues as the East Hopei autonomous regime and the
smuggling in North China.****
The Chinese have insisted
that political questions must be settled before an economic
rapprochement can be reached and consequently the Japanese
have failed to further their economic aims with regard to
North China, the principal of which are the lowering of the
Chinese customs tariffs, the establishment of a Japan-North
China
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2316 of March 18, 1937.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2325 of March 20, 1937.
***
Embassy's telegram No. 77 of March 5, 1937.
****
Embassy's despatches No. 2317 of March 18, and.
2345 of Apri1 2, 1937.

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
China air service, the building of a Sino-Japanese railway,
and the development of an iron mine in Chahar with Japanese
capital. The visit of the Japanese economic mission to
China appears to have been more of a failure than a success
despite the optimistic remarks made by its chairman, Mr.
Kenji Kodama, upon his return to Japan.
On the other hand, Mr. Sato, the newly appointed Minister
for Foreign Affairs, stated on several occasions during the
month that Japan must deal with China on an equal footing
and in a more conciliatory spirit. The military took
exception to some of Mr. Sato's remarks on the ground that
they were too conciliatory. As a consequence the Minister
for Foreign Affairs was forced to make a qualifying state-
ment in the House of Peers to the effeat that in advocating
a policy of conciliation and patience toward China he did
not mean that Japan should be submissive if China trampled
upon international justice or damaged the prestige of Japan.*
Subsequent remarks were made by Mr. Sato which also showed
the influence of the military.** For example, he said that
Central and South China must be dealt with in a different
way from North China since there were special and peculiar
circumstances in the latter region; that the Sust Hopei
and Hopei-Chahar administrations were not a matter for
discussion with Japan; and that the question of Japan's
"special trade"(smuggling) in the East Hopei region was a
concern of East Hopei and should not be discussed with Japan,
but that Japan would consider it in connection with Chinese
tariffs.
In conclusion, it appeared at the end of the month that
Japan still desired to adopt a less aggressive policy toward
China
*
Embassy's telegram No. 89 of March 13, 1937.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2345 of April 2, 1937.

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.97)
China but the lines on which the policy ould be worked out
remained undefined owing to certain fundamental differences
between the two Governments.
/EE
(b). Soviet Russia.
There was evidence in the Diet pronouncements of Mr.
741.94
Sato that he realized the necessity of doing something
definite to improve the relations between Japan and the
Soviet Union.* However, the recently concluded German-
Japanese anti-Comintern agreement continued to be the
principal obstacle to the improvement of relations between
the two countries and Mr. Sato's efforts to explain away
the pact met with no success and were dismissed as illogical
both by Diet interpellators and by the press. Despite his
apparent desire to bring about an improvement of the situa-
tion it is obvious that the Minister for Foreign Affairs
cannot abandon the agreement, since the Premier has already
stated that he would support it. According to news despatches
from the USSR, Soviet leaders were skeptical concerning Mr.
Sato's conciliatory remarks in the Diet. Furthermore, the
situation was complicated by reports of the conclusion of
a Soviet-Chinese agreement. The dangerous possibilities of
such an agreement are patent, as it would indicate an inten-
tion on the part of China and Soviet Russia to isolate and
actively oppose Japan.*
WE
(c). Great Britain.
-11.91
On March 27 the Japanese Government informed the British
Government by an aide-mémoire that it was not able to accede
to the desire of the British Government for acceptance by
Japan of 14-inch guns as a maximum calibre for guns on
capital ships.**
The reasons given in the aide-mémoire for
Japan's
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2351 of April 2, 1937.
**
Embassy's telegram No. 104 of April 1, 1937.

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.99)

Japan's rejection of the l4-inch gun calibre restriction
for capital ships were the same as those given by the
Japanese delegates to the London Naval Conference in
December 1935 and January 1936, namely, thet a disarmament
agreement acceptable to Jepan must be based upon a maximum
common upper limit and upon the principle of "non-menace
and non-aggression", and that qualitative limitation un-
accompanied by quantitative limitation is contrary to the
spirit of disarmament.*
It was announced during the month that Sir Robert L.
Craigie, Assistant Foreign Under Secretary and the Foreign
Office's expert on naval affairs, had been appointed British
Ambassador to Japan to succeed Sir Robert Clive.**
Certain
observers in Tokyo interpreted this appointment as evidence
of Great Britain's interest in Japanese naval policy and
in the conclusion of en eventual naval disarmament agreement.***
On March 25 the Japenese Foreign Office announced the
followinf details of the new trade agreement sicned pro-
visionally between Japan and Burma: Japan is accorded an
annual cotton textile import cuota of 42 million square
yards provided that Japan purchases 65% of Burma's exportable
raw cotton, with the additional proviso that such purchases
need not exceed 70,o00 bales of raw cotton annually.****

between Japan and the Netherland Indies. This is the first

of that country before taking up residence at The Hague.
In a newspaper interview at Singapore on March 27 Mr. Kuwa-

negotiations then in progress between the Governments of
Japan and Netherland India. In addition, Mr. Kuwashima
expressed satisfaction with the results of his inspection
tour and with the conversations which he had had with

(e). Australia.
A group of Australian rotarians composed of sixty or
more persons and described by the Japanese press as a "good
will mission" arrived at Yokohama on March 17.
The newspapers
interpreted the visit of this party as a further demonstration
of the improving relations between Japan and Australia.
Upon his arrival in Japan on March 27 Mr. Kurumatsu
Murai, former Japanese Consul General in Sydney, declared
that Japanese-Australian trade relations were satisfactory
and thet he expected the successful conclusion of a commercial
treaty between the two countries in the near future.
LA
(f). Brazil.
A Brazilian student "good will mission" arrived in Japan
on March 1 8 and its members were extensively entertained
while in this country. In contradiction of reports emanating
from Brazil, Mr. Ruy Mauricio de Lima e Silva, head of
the student mission, stated that the "useful and honest"
Japanese immigrants who were coming to Brazil were welcome in
and of assistance to his country.

(g). Spain.
The Spanish Government at Valencia formally notified
the Japanese Foreign Office on March 29 that Mr. José Luis
Alvarez, professor of Spanish at the Osaka School of Foreign
Languages, had been appointed Chargé d'Affaires ad interim
of the Valencia Government in Japan. In this connection
reference is made to the Embassy's despatch No. 2035 of
September 16, 1936, which dealt with the resignation of
Mr. Santiago Mendez de Vigo, Spanish Minister to Jepan,
Mr. Juan Gomez de Molina, Counselor of the Spanish Legation,
and Mr. Francisco Castillio, Spanish Consul at Kobe, sym-
pathiziers with the insurgent cause in Spain.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet and the Diet.
Throughout the month interpellators assailed the Govern-
ment on the grounds that the Army had interfered too much
in politics, that the Government had adopted no real measures
for the stabilization of the people'e livelihood, and that
the Government had no well-defined foreign policy.* Because
of its desire for the passage of the budget bill, the Govern-
ment's replies to these interpellations were conciliatory
but did not commit the Government to any definite line of
action.
On March 8 the Government's budget bill for the fiscal
year 1937-38 amountin to ¥2,815,000,000 was approved by
the House of Representatives and on March 29 by the House
of Peers.**
Once havine had its budget bill passed, the Government
felt itself in a stronger position and ordered the dissolution
of the Diet on the ostensible grounds that the Minseito and
the
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2316 of March 18, 1937.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2339 of April 1, 1937.

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
the Seiyukai were obstructing passage of certain measures
(minor in character and of no vital interest to the Govern-
ment) in retaliation against the Government's refusal to
urge the Upper House to pass the election revision bills
sponsored by the two principal political parties.* The
Imperial Decree ordering the dissolution of the Diet specified
April 30 as the date for the election of members of the
House of Representatives.
It seemed that the Cabinet's determination to dissolve
the Diet was a result of its opposition to party or consti-
tutional government and not of active opposition by the
parties, which had talked a great deal yet had not seen fit
to reject the Government's budget with its huge military
expenditures. The Government charges the political parties
with a lack of appreciation of the prevailing situation,
both at home and abroad, and of "insincerity" in dealing
with governmental business.
While the Government can look
for little in the way of support from the coming general
election, at the same time it has nothing to lose, Further-
more, it apparently has taken a definite step toward the
encouragement of the formation of a new nationalist or
fascist party.
(b).
The Army and the Navy.
The Army's opposition to Mr. Sato's conciliatory diplomatic
policy has already been discussed in this report. Undoubtedly
the Army was a determining factor in the Cabinet's decision
to dissolve the Diet. It should be noted, however, that it
was the Navy Minister who acted as the Government's spokes-
man in favor of dissolution. Admiral Yonai implied in his
statement
* Embassy'a despatch No. 2350 of April 2, 1937.

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
statement that the dissolution was a disciplinary measure
against the political parties, which had lacked "sincerity".
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
At the session which ended on March 3l, eighty-three
Government bills were presented to the Diet and forty-eight
of these were passed by both Houses. As already mentioned,
the Cabinet's budget bill for the fiscal year 1937-38
amounting to ¥2,815,000,000 was approved by both Houses.
Some of the laws enacted which are worthy of mention are the
following: a law providing funds for the relief of widows
and orphans; a law regulating the price of raw silk; a law
revising the law concerning special accounts for cultural
enterprises in China; a law requiring manufacturers and
sellers of gasoline to mix alcohol with gasoline in a pro-
portion to be determined later by decree; and a law providing
certain regulations concerning air defense.
V.
GOVERNIINT FINANCE.
Thirteen thousand nine hundred kilograms of gold,
worth about ¥50,000,000, were sent to the United States by
the Japanese Government in three shipments during the . month.
The cold was taken from the specie reserve of the Bank of
Japan and consisted of local purchases made by the Government
three years ago after the promulgation of the Gold Purchase
Act. According to Domei, the proceeds of the sale of the
gold will be credited to the New York office of the Yokohama
Specie Bank, presumably to support the exchange value of
the yen.*
VI.
GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDI TI ONS.
(a). Foreign Trade.
Fxports from Japan for March amounted to ¥276,006,000
and
Embassy's telegram No. 67 of March 3, 1937,

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
and imports into this country amounted to ¥400,568,000, as
compared with February exports of ¥225,654,000 and imports
of ¥302,434,000. Thus there was an unfavorable balance of
trade of y124,562,000, as compared with an unfavorable
balance for February of ¥96,780,000.*
(b). Financial Trends.
Financial trends in Japan during the month of March
were satisfactory, being marked by industrial expansion,
laree capital payments, increased prices of stocks and
bonds, and continued appreciation of commodity prices. The
average yen rate of the Yokonama Specie Bank was $0.285, as
compared with 0.28521 for February.*
(c). Aviation.
The Japanese press reported that an airplane manufacturing
company would be established with an initial capitalization
of ¥30,000,000 under joint investment by the principal
Japanese industrial companies and that the Mitsui interests
would constitute the heaviest investor.
(d). Shipping.
According to the Japanese press, the Government and the
894.9
Shipbuilding Association have agreed to establish Japan's
shipbuilding industry on a semi-wartime basis and under the
management of a Shipbuilding Control Commission which will
consist of representatives of the Army and the Navy, the
Communications Ministry, and the various shipbuilding companius.
As reported by the Consulate at Kobe,**the Navy is
considering the purchase of the Kawasaki Dockyards at Kobe
for the sum of ¥50,000,000 and in connection with this step
1t is said that the Nippon Yusen Kaisha will absorb the
Kawasaki Kisen Keisha.

Information furnished by Commercial Attache.
**Despatch from the Consulate ct Kobe, No. 98, dated
March 27, 1937.

VII & VIII - Nothing to report.
IX. LABOR.
The strike which was started on February 28 by the
seamen's union called the Merokai (Enlightened Serene Society),
894.504
which charged the Nippon Yusen Kaisha with "lèse majesté"
for not having hoisted flags on its vessels at the time of
the naval review held off Osaka last fall, came to an end
on March 15 when the Nippon Yusen Kaisha admitted itself
at fault for not having flown flags at the time and the
Merokai apologized for having caused inconvenience.*
The Commander of the Osaka gendarmerie is reported to
have informed an official of the new seamen's union of two
conditions which the Army wishes to be carried out in con-
nection with the proposed merger of the new and old seamen's
unions: (1) the single union should, from the standpoint
of naval defense in the broad sense, sever relations with
the Social Mass Party and the Japan Labor Unions Conference,
"which stand for social democracy", and (2) the single union
should dismiss Mr. Manryo Yonekubo, Vice President of the
Japan Seamen's Union, who is alleged to have declared once
that the Manchurian incident was an "Imperialistic invasion".
X. MISCELLANEOUS.
Nothing to report.

DEPARTMENT OF

RTAMENT OF STATE
June 9, 1937
There are a few items in Tokyo's
political report for April that are
worthy of noting.
It is reported that agreement has
been reached between the Japanese Hosiery
Exporters Association and the American
Federation of Hosiery Manufacturers Associa-
tions to restrict Japanese exports to the
United States of stockings and socks to a
total of 1,500,000 dozen, the agreement to
remain in force until December 31, 1939.
Mr. Suemasa Okamoto, former Chief of
the American Bureau and one-time Secretary
at the Embassy in Washington, has been
assigned as Consul General at Shanghai.
Mr. Amau, the Foreign Office spokes-
man, has been appointed Minister to
Switzerland; Mr. Kuriyama, Chief of the
Treaty Bureau, has been appointed Minister
to Sweden.
There was a considerable amount of
labor unrest in Japan during April owing
to the increase in the cost of living.
A number of companies have raised wages
voluntarily to ward off expected disputes.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). Kadono Economic Mission to the United States.
An unofficial Japanese Economic Mission sailed for the
71.9J
United States on April 28, apparently with no specific
program to offer in the trade discussions to take place in
Washington. The primary objects of the Mission are to return
the visit to Japan of the Forbes unofficial mission in April
1935 and to discuss general problems of trade between the
two countries, as well as to promote "good will".* Mr.
Chokyuro Kadono, chairman of the Mission and recently
elected President of the Tokyo Chamber of Commerce and
Industry, seemed reluctant to reveal in a conversation with
a member of the Embassy staff just what the aims of his
Mission were. There were rumors, not substantiated by any
of the statements made by Japanese officials or business
Ieaders, that the Kadono Economic Mission intended to
initiate conversations which might result in negotiations
between Japan and the United States for the conclusion of a
reciprocal trade agreement. The Mission will also visit
England and Germany.
(b). Difficulties Eperienced by American Automobile
EA
Assembling Plants in Japan.
American automobile assemblin plants in Japan con-
tinued to have difficulties with the Japanese Government.**
It came to light during the month that the Japanese Govern-
ment had refused to grant permission for one American com-
pany to construct a new plant to operate within the scope
of the Automobile Industry Control Law, that the Company
had not been allowed to make certain desired improvements
in its property, and that it would not be allowed under any
circumstances
* Embassy's despatch No. 2386 of April 29, 1937.
**Embassy's despatches No. 1659 of February 5, 1936,
and No. 2394 of April 30, 1937.

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
circumstances to exceed its quota of 12,360 cars per annum,
even if the Japanese producers could not supply the demand.
(c). Provisional Agreement between Japanese and
American Hosiery Interests.
The JAPAN TIlES of April 9 reported that after pro-
longed negotiations between representatives of the Japanese
Hosiery Exporters Association and the American Federation of
Hosiery Menufacturers Associations a provisional agreement
had been reached restricting Jupanese exports to the United
States of stockings and socks (including all silk, rayon,
mixed rayon and silk, and high grade cotton stockings and
socks) to 1,500,000 dozen, the agreement to be applied
retroactively from January 1, 1937, and to remain in force
until December 31, 1939.
(d). Yoshizawa's Appointment as Chief of the American
Affairs Bureau of the Foreign Office.
On April 3 the Cabinet approved the appointment of
Mr. Seijiro Yoshizawa, former Counselor of the Japanese
Embassy at Washington, as Chief of the American Affairs
Bureau of the Foreign Office and also approved the ap-
pointment of Mr. Suemasa Okamoto, former Chief of this
Bureau, as Consul General at Shanghai.

(b). China.
At the close of the month it appeared that Sino-Japanese
193.9
negotiations were at a standstill, owing principally to
China's realization of its increased strength and of Japan's
weaknesses, and to Japan's understanding of this feeling on
the part of the Chinese.** Divergent points of view are
held by the two countries regarding the improvement of
relations: Japan wants to attain certain economic objectives
in China while the latter country insists that economic
matters cannot be discussed before political issues are
disposed of. The two principal political issues are the
continued existence of the East Hopei Autonomous Government
and the "special trade" or smuggling in North China, for
both of which the Japanese are responsible. It is obvious
that the Japanese authorities, including the military, see
the necessity of adopting a less aggressive attitude toward
China as the result of the strides made by that country
toward national unification. However, it remains vague
