(c). Soviet Russia.
761.94
Mr. Yureneff, the Soviet Ambassador to Japan, arrived
in Tokyo on April 13 after an absence on leave of two
months. There has been a great deal of discussion in the
press concerning several interviews between him and Mr.
Sato, Minister for Foreign Affairs, which were held
reportedly for the purpose of improving relations between
the two countries. The Embassy has been unable to ascertain
the substance of these interviews.
Upon his arrival in Japan, Mr. Yureneff was asked in
a newspaper interview if in his belief there was a way
open for finding smooth relations between the two countries
and his reply was: "I believe there is. Everything depends
on the attitude of the Japanese Government." There would
seem to be a movement on foot at present toward an attempted
readuustment of Soviet-Japanese relations, the motive power
behind this movement being on the Japanese side.*

WE
agreement between Japan and the Netherland East Indies.
According to Domei, the principal provision of the agreement
is that 25% of the imports into the Netherland Indies from
Japan may be handled by Japanese importers and merchants on
the condition that Japan shall purchase Java sugar on a
preferential basis.

(f). France.
On April 15 the Japanese Foreign Office exchanged
notes with the French Ambassador for the relinquishment of
the perpetual leaseholds of French nationals in Japan. The
notes were of the same purport as those exchanged with the
British Embassy and with the American Embassy on March 25,

The Swiss Minister to Japan exchanged notes on April 15
with the Japanese Foreign Office, similar to those exchanged
by the French Ambassador and the Foreign Office, settling
the perpetual leasehold question between the Swiss and
Japanese Governments.
(h). Argentina.
Japanese newspapers reported that at the request of
the Foreign Office the Japan Chamber of Commerce and Industry
had decided to send an economic mission to Argentina for
the promotion of trade between the two countrics.

A Chilean Economic Mission to Japan, under the chairman-

the visit having been proposed two years before by the
Japanese Foreign Office and the Japan Chamber of Commerce
and Industry.** Two days after its arrival the Mission was
handed a list of twelve proposals for improving and ex-
pediting trade between the two countries. These proposals

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet' and the Diet Elections.
Following the dissolution of the Diet on March 3l, the
Cabinet on April l0 issued an eight-point statement of policy,
general in tone to the point of vagueness, urging improve-
ment in education end culture, administration, diplomacy,
armaments, livine, industry, rural conditions, and taxes.*
Although the Minseito, the Seiyukai, and the Shakai Taishuto
vigorously attacked the Government in the election campaign,**
they failed to agree upon a concrete platform of opposition
likely to injure the Government, whose position was not
greatly weakened as a result of the election.***
The
election, held on April 30, broucht a decrease in the Min-
seito and the Seiyukai seats and an increase in the repre-
sentation of the Shakai Taishuto, of the independents, and
of the small parties. This election left no doubt that the
electorate disapproved of the non-party Cabinet, yet it gave
no indication that this Cabinet or one similar to it could
not continue to function in contravention of parliamentary
politics, which have been in desuetude since l932.
It is significant to note that a phonograph record made
hy Mr. Yukio Ozaki, veteran upholder of parliamentary politics,
for use in the election campaign was banned on April 19
allegedly because a passage in it (undisclosed to the press)
was judged by the Government to be harmful to the public mind.

According

Embassy's telegram No. 107 of April 10, 1937.

**
Embassy's despatch No. 2367 of April 16, 1937,

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.99)

According to present indication s, the special session
of the Diet will be convened in August.
(b). The Army.
In a speech made before 60o Army officers on April 8
General Sugiyama, the War Minister, said that the Army had
become the pilot and not simply the "propulsive force" of
the nation's efforts to advance its fortunes. The War
Minister went on to state that the Japanese people lacked
proper appreciation of the gravity of the present inter-
national situation and also lacked national spirit.
(c). Political Parties.
Little or no progress was made in the Cabinet's efforts
to form a pro-Government political party. Prince Konoye's
apparent reluctance to head a new political party constituted
an obstacle to the Government's plan. Finance Minister Yuki
and Mr. Hirota, recent Premier, were mentioned by the press
as possible choices for the presidency of the new party.
In any case, little can be expected in the immediate future
from any pro-Government party which may .be formed.
(d). Proposed Government Planning Board.
The Government was reported to have approved the
establishment of a Planning Board (Kikakucho), which is to
incorporate the Cabinet Inquiry Bureau created in May. 1935,
and to assume more comprehensive functions. It appeared
that the proposed Board would be designed for a planned
economy and that in a way it would be a manifestation of
Premier'Hayashi's conviction that the now existing political
institutions of Japan are inadequate for the governmental
leadership necessary to the nation at the present time.

Nothing to report.

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.99)
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
At a bankers' luncheon on April 20 Mr. Yuki, the
Finance Minister, delivered an address in which he said
that he was in favor of additional legal restriction of
importation of certain unnamed commodities outside the
essential raw materials class, that bank investing should
be in accord with Government planning, that bankers should
consider the importance of making investments in China, and
that Manchuria was increasingly depedent on Japanese financing.
He added that for cooperation with foreign countries and for
arriving at a more complete understanding in harmony with
Japan's financial and economic policy, Japan should parti-
cipate il: any international conference which might be held.*
The Japanese Government decided to resume shipments of
gold to the United States and to ship 15,0oo kilograms in
the immediate future. The first shipment, amounting to
4,500 kilograms, left Kobe on April 21.**
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a) Foreign Trade.
Japanese exports during April amounted to ¥263,028,000
and imports into Japan amounted to ¥336,681,000 as compared
with March exports of ¥276,006,000 and imports of ¥400,568,000.
There was thus an unfavorable balance of trade of ¥73,653,000
as compared with an unfavorable balance for March of y124,562,000
An Imperial Ordinance was promulgated on April 15 pro-
EA
viding for the suspension of import duties on iron and steel
to be effective from that date and to remain in force until
March 31, 1938. It is understood that the reason for this
measure is the present shortage of iron and steel in Japan
and the alleged necessity of replenishing military and naval
armaments.
(b).
*
Embassy's telegram No. 116 of April 21, 1937.
**
Embassy's telegram No. 114 of April 20, 1937.

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
(b). Financial Trends.
The stock market was active in spite of the political
uncertainty over the Diet election and the prices of shares
appreciated considerably during the month. Commodity prices
continued to increase. The Bank of Japan's wholesale price
index for the month was 248, the highest for any month since
l925. Due partly to the export of gold above mentioned, the
yen showed a slight appreciation, the average yen rate of
the Yokohama Specie Bank being $0.28608 as compared with
$0.285 for March.
VII: ACTIVTTTES OF AMERICAN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
IX.
LABOR.
The ASAiI of April 1 reported that the Home Ministry
was preparing a bill to be introduced in the next Diet
session providing for the restriction of working hours
uniformly to twelve lours a day, the primary object of the
measure beinf the improvement of national health.
It was stated by the press that the Home Ministry had
decided to ban parades or any sort of demonstrations by
labor unions on May Day.
There was a considerable anount of labor unrest during
April owing to the increase in the cost of living. Labor's
claims for higher wages were recognized in several instances,
a number of disputes being settled on the basis of a raise
of wages ranging from 8% to 15%. At the same time a number
of companies raised wages voluntarily to ward off expected
disputes.
X. MISCELLANEOUS.
Nothing to report.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). Statements of the Minister for Foreign Affaira
EU
Concerning American-Japanese Relations.
It was reported by the NICHI NICHI of May 4 that Mr.
Sato, the Minister for Foreign Affairs intended, if possible,
to conclude a Pacific non-aggression pact, either between the
United States and Japan or between the United States, Great
Britain and Japan, the purposes of which would be to give
concrete expression to Japan's peaceful intentions and to
check naval competition.* In a statement issued to the
foreign press correspondents on May 6, Mr. Sato admitted that
he had discussed casually with a reporter of the NICHI NICHI
the question of a non-aggression pact with the United States
but added that he had as yet no concrete plan, had made no
approach to the United States, and did not intend to put
forward the idea.** In the same prepared statement Mr. Sato
stated that there was no vexing issue, political or otherwise,
between the United States and Japan, and that the friendship
between the two nations was destined to grow with the years.
At a conference of prefectural governors on May 17 the
Minister for Foreign Affairs said that relations with the
United States were satisfactory on the whole; that the ex-
pansion of Japanese foreign trade had brought about diffi-
culties with the United States which had been gradually
settled by mutual understanding; and that he hoped that Japan
and the United States, whose economic relations were comple-
mentary, would continue to collaborate in the task of pre-
serving peace in the Pacific.***
(b). American Oil Interests.
On May 29 the foreign oil companies (the Standard Vacuum
Oil Company and the Rising Sun Petroleum Company) received
from
*
Embassy's telegram No. 124 of May 4, 1937.
**
Embassy's telegram No. 126 of May 7, and despatch No. 2410
of May 11, 1937.
*卓*
Embassy.'s telegram No. 131 of May 7, and despatch No. 2443
ofMav281932

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
from the Foreign Office a fairly satisfactory aide memoire
regarding the Korean oil quota problem and referred the
matter to their head offices in New York and London, res-
pectively.* This development, together with the recent
appointment as Minister of Commerce and Industry of Mr.
Yoshino, whos: attitude as Vice Minister toward the diffi-
culties being experienced by the foreign oil companies was
helpful, increases prospects of a practical adjustment of
the problem being found.
II. RELATIONS WITH OTIER COUNTRIES.
(a). China.
793.9
There was observed during the month a stiffening in
Japan's attitude toward China which had apparently become
more conciliatory during previous months.** It will be
recalled that the reorientation of policy toward China
reportedly decided upon by the Japanese Government embraces
a desire to maintain a strong position on the Asiatic main-
land through friendship and economic cooperation with China
insteed of by actual political control of that country.
However, this conciliatory program net with setbacks during
May and the Minister for Foreign Affairs, the chief pro-
ponent of the policy. found it necessary to issue statements
showing a firmer stand on the part of Japan. Other Japanese
officials made public statements of like tenor.
Sino-Japanese relations, which had appeared to be
improving during recent months, were marred by a number of
developments:. There was a recurrence of anti-Japanese
agitation in North China and there occurred several incidents
which were said to have caused serious concern to the
Japanese authorities. At Swatow on liay 22 Chinese police
arrested
*

*

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.99)

which was urging internal harmony for the particular purpose
of resisting foreign aggression; that it must be made clear
that Japan's designs in China are not aggressive; and that
the Japanese Government was seeking greater harmony through
personal contact and through cultural and economic cooperation.
In the second speech the Minister for Foreign Affairs
made the statement, apropos of Japan's foreign policy in
general, that war is never profitable but that once the
honor and prestige of Japan are injured the Japanese must
be willing to fight. He added that some people might be of
the opinion that he would yield to a foreign country even
if the honor and prestige of Japan were injured but that
he only wished to follow "the normal path of diplomacy until
the last moment, if the worst is to come." *
I1 a speech before the prefectural governors on May 19
General Sugiyama, the War Minister, expressed apprehension
over Chinese policies toward Japan in the following state-
ment: "As for China, we find that it is concentrating on
centralization and replenishment of armaments and other
defensive undertakings based on anti-Japanese slogans. As
a result, it has come to overestimate its national strength,
and this in turn has served to encourege the anti-Japanese
sentiments of its people. We fcar that China may ultimately
resort to sundry steps to obstruct Japan's peaceful advance
at its very foundation."
On May 18 Admiral Rukuzo Suiyama, Chief of Staff of the
Third Fleet, told newspaper correspondents that although the
open movement against Japan had been held in check in Shanghai
and Nanking, underground anti-Japanese activities had been
vigorously carried on in South China. Also,he stated that
China
* Embassy's despatch No. 2443 of May 28, 1937.

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
China had adopted the policy of making approaches to
powerful countries in order to check Japan and expressed
regret that there were "no signs of Sino-Japanese relations
turning for the better".
Aside from the developments and statements outlined
above, certain definite stumbling blocks continued to com-
plicate Sino-Japanese relations. After the return to Japan
of Mr. Kawagoe, Japanese Ambassador to China, a meeting
was held of representatives of the Foreign Office and the
War and Navy Ministries to discuss the proposed "new deal
for China". According to newspaper versions of the results
of the discussion, the "new deal" will provide for Japan's
recognition of China's increasing strength and unity but
will contemplate neither the relinquishment by Japan of its
special position in North China nor the immediate abolition
of the East Hopei Autonomous Régime. Since the abolition of
the East Hopei Autonomous Régime and the extension of the
Nanking Government's control over North China appear to be
the principal desiderata of the Chinese, it would not seem
that the proposed "new deal for China" will be acceptable to
that country as a basis for further negotiations.*
(b). Soviet Russia.
E'1
Little of interest occurred in the relations between
761.94
Japan and Soviet Russia.** The conversations held by the
Minister for Foreign Affairs and Mr. Yureneff, the Soviet
Ambassador to Japan, apparently resulted in no scttlement of
pending issues. It is possible that the firm stand taken by
the Soviet Union against the German-Japanese anti-Comintern
agreement was the snag which impeded the informal negotia-
tions between Mr. Sato and Mr. Yureneff. Before his departure
on
*
Embassy's despatch No. 24l9 of May 14, 1937.
**
Embessy's despatch No. 2426 of May 14, 1937.

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
on May 15 from Tokyo to Moscow en route to his new post at
Berlin, Mr. Yureneff issued a statement to the press in which
he characterized relations between the two countries as
"unstable" but not bad, but he added that there was no
question whicn could not be solved amicably. While regretting
that his conversations with Mr. Sato had not brought about a
solution of pending problems, Mr. Yureneff expressed belief
that a solution could be found by thorough discussion of the
existing issues, the only requirement being that each nation
demonstrate "sincerity".*
Shortly after the issuance of the Soviet Ambassador's
statement the Government of the U.S.S.R. announced that it
intended to abrogate the agreement of September 4, 1934,
between Soviet Russia and "Manchukuo" governing the frontiers
of the two states formed by the Amur and Ussuri rivers.
Several border incidents occurred, all of them similar
in character to the incidents which have been reported during
the last few years and none of them of serious proportions.
In his speech before the prefectural governors on May 17,
Mr. Sato stressed Japan's efforts to eliminate all unnecessary
friction and to solve pending issues with Soviet Russia;
stated that Soviet officials had maltreated Japanese residents
and officials and obstructed Japanese shipping, admitting
that complaints of similar action by Japanese officials had
also been made; and asked the prefectural governors to be
careful to prevent such occurrences in the future, inasmuch
as he (Sato) had asked the Soviet Government to do so.

(c). Great Britain.
In his statement on foreign policy read to foreign news-
741.94
paper correspondents on May 6, the Minister for Foreign
Affairs
* Embassy's despatch No. 2445 of May 29, 1937.

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
Affairs discussed Anglo-Japanese relations first, indicating
that the Japanese Foreign Office now considers those relations
of primary importance. Mr. Sato stated that Anglo-Japanese
relations had improved owing to the recent settlement of
certain contentious questions and that he hoped to strengthen
the traditional Anglo-Japanese friendship.* In reply to a
question put by a correspondent, Mr. Sato said that the
Anglo-Japanese conversations then proceeding had so far
consisted only of a very general exchange of views and that
it was premature to believe that anything like an arrange-
ment had been reached. He denied that spheres of influence
in China were being discussed in these conversations but
admitted that the relations of Great Britain and Japan with
China were being discussed.
The Minister for Foreign Affairs made similar remarks
concerning Anglo-Japanese relations in his speech to the
prefectural governors in Tokyo on May 17. He stated that
friendship with Great Britain was highly desirable; that a
number of problems with regard to China and with regard to
trade required solution; that the settlement of the Keelung
Incident and of the perpetual lease question had eased the
atmosphere; and that the visit of Prince and Princess Chichibu
to London was adding to Anglo-Japanese friendship.**
Sir Robert Clive, retiring British Ambassador, delivered
a speech at a banquet given him by the Japan-British Society
in Tokyo on the evening of May 6 in which he said that the
last three years represented a period: of marking time in
Anglo-Japanese relations, that there was a desire on both
sides to see a closer understanding between the two countries,
and that with patience this was sure to come. The reference
to
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2410 of May 11, 1937.

to "patience" may have been intended as an indication that

Sir Robert Clive left Japan for his new post at Brussels

Fll
(a). International.
The statement made by Prime Minister Joseph Lyons of
700.0011
Australia at the opening meeting of the British Empire
Conference in London on May 14, that Australia would welcome
a non-aggression pact among thc countries of the Pacific
conceived in the spirit of the League of Nations Covenant,
received considerable publicity in Japan. The reaction to
the proposal was one of reticence on the part of Government
officials and of suspicion on the part of the press.** It
appeared that although Japan was not fundamentally opposed
to the idea, this country felt that the time was not ripe for
consideration of such a pact. Furthermore, it was stated by
the vernacular press that Japan would be most reluctant to
discuss certain issues of a controversial nature which would
undoubtedly have to be discussed in any conference that might
be held for the conclusion of a Pacific non-aggression treaty.
The Japanese press was unanimous in criticizing Prime Minister
Lyon's suggestion adversely.

(e). The Naval Question.
Mr. Hugh Byas, Tokyo correspondent of the NEw YORK
TIMES, had an interview in the latter part of the month
with Admiral Yonai, the Navy Minister, who definitely denied
current press reports that Japan:was building or would build
super battleships carrying guns larger than sixteen inches
in caliber.*
(f). Germany.
According to a Domei despatch from Hsinking dated
May 2l, a new agreement was signed in Berlin on that day
extending by three years the "Manchukuo"-German Trade Agree-
ment, which was signed on April 30, 1936, and which was due
to expire at the end of May 1937.
(g). "Manchukuo".
On May 8 the "Manchukuo" Government announced the follow-
ing reform of its administrative machinery: the Legislative
and Supervisory Councils are to be done away with; the six
Ministries - Foreign Affairs, War, Civil Affairs, Finance,
Industry, and Mongol Affairs - are to be abolished. In the
future the Premier will be the only State Minister, the
present Ministries being directly under his supervision,
and in place of the six Ministries to be abolished, Ministries
for Public Peace, Civil Welfare, General Industry, and
Economic Affairs will be established.
9A

It will be remembered that in February of this year the
Japanese were reluctant to make further exports of steel

meeting of the Japan-Brazilian Enterprise Association

At a final conference between the members of the

(j). Mexico.
The vernacular press reported that the Japan Marine
Products Company had decided to establish a fishery company
under joint investment by and management of Japanese and
Mexican capitalists and that negotiations to this end
would be started upon the forthcoming arrival in Japan of
Mexicans interested in the enterprise.
(k). Latin America.
In reply to an inquiry of the Department of Commerce
and Industry regarding the desirability of making a survey
of overseas markets in the South Seas, the Near East, Africa,
and Central and South America, the Tokyo Chamber of Commerce
and Industry recommended that the Government confine its
investigations to Central and South America in view of the
limited appropriation for this project.*

-11-
(1). Siam.
Upon his arrival in Japan on May 9 Mr. Ishii, Japanese
Minister to Siam, stated in a press interview that the
Siamese had great admiration for Japan but he warned his
countrymen against attempts to force friendship on the
Siamese people without giving sympathetic consideration
to Siam's national aspirations.
(m). Poland.
According to a Domei despatch from Seoul, an official
of the Polish Consulate General in Shanghai reported to the
Seoul police on May 15 that while on a train en route to
Tokyo, where he was proceeding to confer with the authorities
of the Polish Legation, he had been robbed of important
documents.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet and the Political Parties.
The Japanese press in general interpreted the results
of the Diet election of April 30 as a defeat for the Hayashi
Cabinet, a victory for the political parties.* Since there
were no clear-cut issues offered by either of the major
political parties, the Seiyukai and the Minseito, the voters
elected much the same class of men to the Diet as those who
had been members when the House was dissolved on March 31,
1937.**
On May 3 the Cabinet issued a statement to the effect
that the Lower House had been dissolved on March 31 because
regeneration of parliamentary government was the cornerstone
of governmental reform; that the Government wanted the newly-
elected Diet members to be guided by a spirit of self-sacrifice
in
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2428 of May 15, and Embassy's
Monthly Political Report for April, Section III (a).
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2423 of May 14, 1937.

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
- 12 -
in developing the constitutional system peculiar to Japan;
that the people shared the Government's desire in this respect;
and that the Government would continue on its way "confident
of the nation's support of the Government's earnest desire
for perfect cooperation with the people."*
Premier Hayashi issued a statement to the effect that
the election returns were not & surprise to the Government
and that the Government had had no intention of attempting
to win the election.
Opposition to the Government by the Seiyukai, the Min-
seito, and the Shakai Taishuto increased during the month,
leaders of these parties issuing statements which agaailed
the policies of the Hayashi Cabinet and particularly its
high-handed attitude toward the parties. The political
parties were particularly irate over General Hayashi's
failure to include in his Cabinet a few Ministers taken
from the parties. Finally on May 3l the Hayashi Cabinet
resigned en bloc. The decision of the Cabinet to resign
came soon after the issuance of a vigorous public statement
by Prince Konoe that Premier Hayashi should have chosen some
Ministers from the political parties immediately after the
election of April 30.**
Little or no progress seened to have been made during
the month toward the establishment of the proposed new
political party, reactionary in character and designed to
support thc Hayashi Cabinet or similar non-party cabinets
that might be formed in the future.
(b), The Army.
According to the CHUGAI of May 17, the new policy of
War Minister Sugiyama and Vice War Minister Umezu is "action
before
Embassy's telegram No. 122 of May 3, 1937.
Embassy's telegram No. 14l of May 31, 1937.

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
- 13 -
before words" and General Sugiyama recently stated: "No
matter how clamorously we talk, the talking will not only
be ineffective but will do harm instead of good. We will
just go ahead and work things out while the public is
unaware". This newspaper estimated that the Army's
budgetary demands for the fiscal year 1938-39 would amount
to Yen 950,000,000 as compared with the Army's appropriations
of Yen 720,000,000 for the fiscal year 1937-38.
(c). The Navy.
A pamphlet entitled the "Non-Treaty Era and Our Navy"
was issued by the Navy Ministry on May 27, the anniversary
of Admiral Togo's victory over the Russian Fleet in the
Battle of the Japan Sea. The purpose of this pamphlet, as
of previous Navy pamphlets, would seem to be to impress upon
the people of Japan the necessity for continued and strenuous
efforts to keep the Navy up to the requirements of national
defense. Germany and Italy are pointed to in the pamphlet
as perfect examples of the grim determination and military
preparedness necessary to Japan for the maintenance of its
power as the "stabilizing influence" in East Asia. It is even
stated that "Italy, foreseeing the inability of the League
and the insufficient power of France and Great Britain,
carried out her long cherished desire to annex Ethiopia."
The pamphlet goes on to remark that various European
countries and the United States have been carrying out an
aggressive economic advance in East Asia, especially in the
development and extension of air routes. In addition, the
document refers to the strengthening by the United States
of its defense works in the Pacific, especially in Hawaii,
and asserts that the conclusion to be drawn from these
activities is that they "must be considered as preparations
for operations in the Pacific." It is also stated that the
Japanese

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
Japanese fleet must be able to repulse any attempt to
disturb the tranquillity of East Asia and able also to
defeat any fleet which might invade the Western Pacific.*
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Nothing to report.
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
Observers of the financial situation believed that the
foreign exchange control law might be revised so as to
establish more rigid control over remittances for other
than essential import trade requirements, for the munitions
industry and allied industries, and for the acquisition.
and withdrawal of Japanese securities issued abroad. The
Finance Ministry was reportedly concerned over the adverse
visible trade balance and it was stated that this Department
was preparing other measures for the further restriction of
imports to offset the unfavorable trade balance which is
inevitable, despite heavy exports of gold.*
VI.
GENERAL EC ONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a).
Foreign Trade.
Japan's exports for May amounted to Yen 262,248,000
while imports into Japan amounted to Yen 420,074,000, as
compared with April exports of Yen 263,028,000 and imports
of Yen 336,681,000. Hence there was an unusually large
unfavorable balance of trade of Yen 157,826,000, as compared
with an unfavorable balance for March of only Yen 73,653,000.
Thus far this year imports are 31.25 per cent greater
than for the corresponding period of l936, and exports have
increased by 23.47 per cent.**
(b).
Embassy's telegram No. 138 of May 28, 1937.
**
Data furnished by the Commercial Attaché.
●

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.98)
- 15-
(b). Financial Trends.
The stock and bond markets were quiet throughout the
month, reflecting uncertainty over the internal political
situation and also over Sino-Japanese relations. The money
market was easy. Commodity prices, which had been steadily
advancing since November 1936, showed a decline of 2.8% from
the month of April. The prices of rice, tea, fertilizera
and soda increased owing partly to seasonal faotors but the
prices of copper, lead, raw rubber, iron, imported lumber,
textiles, and cereals decreased as the result of low prices
abroad and uncertainty in the political situation.*
The average yen rate of the Yokohama Specie Bank was
$0.2875 as compared with 0.28608 for April.
VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FCREIGN COMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
Ix.
LABOR.
A number of labor disputes arose during the month and
there appeared to be a tendency on the part of both employers
and the Government to listen sympathetically to the demands
of the strikers. In one case, at least, wages were increased
as a result of labor's demands: the Oji Electric Railway
Company granted a second 5% wage increase to conductors and
motormen employed by the Company.
It was reported that the Government would give official
recognition to the demands by Japanese workers for increased
pay on account of higher living costs but that vigorous
measures would be taken against any political manipulations
that might appear in labor disputes.
X. MISCELLANEOUS. Nothing to report.
* Information furnished by the Commercial Attaché.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a). Negative Reply of the Japanese Government to
the American Government's Ingairy Concerning
Japan's Willingness to Adopt the 14-Inch Gun
Caliber as a Maximum.
Acting under instructions received from the Department,*
711.9
on June 7 the Ambassador left an aide mémoire with Mr. Hirota,
the Minister for Foreign Affairs, inquiring whether the
Japanese Government would be willing to accept the provision
of the London Naval Treaty of l936 for a reduction in the
future caliber of guns on battleships from 16 inches to 14
inches. On June 18 the Minister for Foreign Affairs handed
the Ambassador an aide mémoire stating that the Japanese
Government is not at this juncture in a position to adopt,
apart from quantitative restriction, a mere limitation of
the gun caliber for capital ships. The aide mémoire explains
that the Japanese Government has not altered its position
as elucidated at the last London Naval Conference, namely,
that it cannot subscribe to qualitative limitation of arma-
ment if not accompanied by a simultaneous restriction of
quantity.**
Mr. Hirota observed to the Ambassador that Japan's
inability to give a favorable answer to the American Government'
inquiry was no indication of any lack of friendliness on the
part of Japen for the United States. He expressed the hope
that in future, as the general international atmosphere
improves, some favorable solution of the naval problem will
come "from somewhere".***

(b). Statement of the Minister for Foreign Affairs
Concerning Japan's Policy Toward the United States.
In the Ambassador's initial interview with the new
Minister for Foreign Affairs on June 7, Mr. Hirota requested
that the President and the Secretary of State be informed
that his guiding policy in conducting Japan's relations with
the United States would be based on the Hull-Hirota exchange
of messages in l934.*
(c), Alaska Salmon Fishing Situation.
It was reported in the United States that a large number
of Japanese fishing vessels were preparing to invade the
Bristol Bay salmon fisheries and that some of these vessels
were already there engaged in fishing. Pursuant to instructions
received from the Department,** the Embassy asked the Foreign
Office whether the Japanese Government had changed its policy
of refusing to permit Japanese vessels to fish for salmon in
waters adjacent to Alaska.*** On June 1l the Foreign Office
informed the Embassy that no license had been issued for salmon
fishing in the Bering Sea, stating that the Japanese vessels
reported to be operating near Alaska were presumably engaged
in crab fishing. The Foreign Office added that it was
approaching the Department of Agriculture and Forestry with a
view to giving the American Government further assurance that
no licenses for salmon fishing will be granted, not only until
after the three-year investigations are completed but also
until there has been opportunity for discussion with the American
Government of the salmon fishing problem after the completion
of such investigations.****

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
EU
(a). Great Britain.
(1).
Anglo-Japanese Negotiations Looking Toward
A Political and Commercial Agreement.
In reply to a question from Mr. Dooman, Counselor of the
Embassy, on June 23 Mr. Yoshizawa, Director of the American
Bureau of the Foreign Office, stated that the Japanese
Ambassador to Great Britain had been authorized to enter
into formal negotiations with the British Government with a
view to the conclusion of a political agreement regarding
China and also regarding commercial problems which affect
Great Britain and Japan.**
According to a Domei despatch from London which appeared
in the Japanese newspapers on June 22, the forthcoming nego-
tiations at London will be along the following lines: (a)
the Japanese Government will emphasize adjustment of trade
relations (presumably this refers to Japan's desire for the
removal of quotas on Japanese imports into British colonies
and for an assurance that special restrictions will not be
imposed upon Japanese goods imported into British markets);
(b)
Embassy's despatch No. 2494 of July 7, 1937.
Embassy's telegram No. 165 of June 23 and despatch No. 2482
of June 24, 1937.

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.99)
(b) in return Japan will discuss the possibility of issuing a
declaration that "Japan has no territorial ambitions in China
and is ready to respect British vested interests in that
country". Mr. Yoshizawa told Mr. Dooman that the Domei
despatch was a substantially accurate and intelligent estimate
of the subjects which might be discussed in the negotiations.
The Director of the American Bureau of the Foreign Office said
that he could even add that British and Japanese economic
interests in China and commercial problems arising out of
Japanese imports into British markets would be discussed.
According to news despatches to Japan from London, Foreign
Secretary Anthony Eden stated in the House of Commons on June 25
that the talks which had been proceeding between Japan and Great
Britain for the improvement of their relations would "very
shortly lead to an examination of concrete proposals".
In this connection it is interesting to note that, according
to a Domei despatch from Shanghai dated June 27, on that day
the Nanking Government issued the following statement through
the official Central News Agency: (1) All matters relating to
China which are taken up in the Anglo-Japanese negotiations must
be arranged with full respect for the sovereignty and territorial
integrity of the Chinese nation; (2) Any agreement on matters
pertaining to China must be preceded by an exchange of views
with the Chinese and also must be endorsed by the latter.
i (2). Boycott Against the Purchase of Japanese Rayon
Piece Goods Adopted by The Indian Merchants
Association of Yokohama.
On June 5 the Indian Merchants Association of Yokohama
adopted a resolution forbidding the purchase by members of the
Association of Japanese rayon piece goods. It was said that
this step had been taken in retaliation against the recent
20 per cent reduction by the Japanese Rayon Textile Dyers
Amalgamated Company, Limited, of the respective quotas held
by individual dying companies which have heretofore dyed rayon
material to be exported to British India by the Indian morchants
of Yokohama.

On the evenin of June 4 Prince Konoye stated in a radio
address that it would be his policy in foreign relations to
seek international justice, and in domestic relations to seek
social justice. (It was subsequently reported that the Premier
was studying measures for the realization of "peace based on
justice and not on the status quo". If such is the Premier's
plan it would indicate that he intends to further Japan's de-
sire for a redistribution of world markets). Prince Konoye's
radio address did not constitute a political platform. In
fact, his statement that he could not yet announce concrete
points of his platform gave the impression that the policy
of the Cabinet would be to meet questions as they arise rather
than to try to carry out any formulated platform.*
Before accepting the task of formine a Cabinet Prince
Konoye obtained the consent of the irmy and the Navy to the
continuing in office of General Sugiyama and dmiral Yonai as
Army and Navy Ministers, respectively. This consent was ap-
parently given without conditions as to the composition of the
Cabinet. Therefore, it represented an expression of confidence
in Prince Konoye as Premier rather than an approval of the in-
dividuals to be chosen for each post in the Cabinet.

(e). House of Peers.
Prince Konoye has been kcown for some time to be in
favor of reforming the House of Peers. He was said to have
told newspaper reporters on June 20 that in his opinion a
reform of the Upper House should first of all include a re-
duction of the proportion of Peers to the total membership
of the Upper House; that the Lower House represented public
opinion and could thus be called the House of common sense;
and that the House of Peers should have more men of expert
knowledge and experience and be reorganized so that it would
stand for "expert opinions and not for the Peers". It was
reported by the press that even the progressive members of the
House of Peers were "astounded" at Premier Konoye's views.

V. GOVERNNMENT FINANCE.
In an interview with the press immediately after his
acceptance of the Finance portfolio on June 3, as reported
by the JAPAN TIMES of June 4, the new Finance Minister, Mir.
Kaya, emphasized that he would reject any excessive economic
and monetary control measures although he admitted the ne-
cessity of a broad and fundamental economic planning for
harmonizing the expanding state expenditures and the actual
economic strength of the nation. Mr. Kaya, while asserting
that he would follow the essentials of the financial and
economic policies of his predecessor, lir. Yuki, admitted
that he might adopt "good parts" of the policies of Dr. Baba,
Finance

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.99)
-17-
Finance Minister under the Hirota Cabinet.
Despite the assurances of Mr. Kaya, financial and busi-
ness ciroles in general were reported by Domei of June 13 to
be apprehensive lest the Finance Ministry "must eventually
enforce" a system of drastic financial control and managed
economy to meet the budget problem resulting from the increase
in military expenditures and to meet also the necessity of
raising a large amount of molcy for increased industrial pro-
duction.*
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AID FINAICIAL CONDITIONS.
(a) Foreien Trade.
Exports from Japan for the month of June were
Yen 281,195,000 as compared with imports into Japan of
Yen 382,318,000, while May exports amounted to Yen 262,248,000
and imports to Yen 420,074,000. This represented an unfavor-
able balance of trade of Yen 101,123,000 as compared with an
unfavorable balance for May of Yen 157,826,000.** The excess
of imports for the six months' period, January 1 to June 30,
inclusive, reached the large figure of Yen 618,301,000.
It was reported that the Foreign Office had decided to
send economic missions to Africa and Latin America in AuE-
ust of this year with a view to readjusting Japan's foreign
trade and that these missions would be composed of Government
officials and representatives of Chambers of Commerce and of
export guilds.***
The press reported that the Konoye Cabinet's bills for

During the early part of the yeer it will be recalled

of wool importers was organized for the purpose of regulating

reports to the effect that a movement was on foot to set up a

in view of the complaints on the part of the cotton mills of a
lack of coordination among thc importers which occasionally
leads to scarcity of stocks on hand of raw cotton and to un-
toward fluctuations in price. Whilc the Embassy cannot at
this juncture attempt to comment on this project, it is appar-
ent that such an association, if established, would virtually
perfect the facilities which Jepan now has for regulating its
purchases in the various raw cotton markets.

IX. LBOR.
According to figures publishcd by the Social Bureau of
the Ministry for Home iffairs, there were 1,229 cases of
labor disputes during thc first five months of l937 as com-
pared with about 7oo cases for the corresponding period of
1936.

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
1936. The Home Orficc also reported that over 80 percent of
the current year's labor disputes represented demands for in-
creases in wages or discharge allowances because of the rise
in the cost of livine; that the wage increases demended had
been from 10 to 30 pcrcent; that employers had in general
agreed to wage incrcases ranging from 8 to 10 percent; and
that the employers concerned had been more willing than here-
tofore to concede the demands of their employees, while on
the other hand the cmployees involved had rarely resorted to
violence or to other extreme means for the attainment of satis-
faction.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) American Government's Attitude toward the North China
Situation.
S
On July 13 the Ambassador reported to the Department by tele-
T graph that he saw no reason why the American Government should take
R action along the lines suggested by Mr. Eden to the Amorican Am-
I bassador in London, namely, a combined offer by the American and
C British Governments of help in settling the North China diffi-
T culties.*
L
At a press conference on July l6 the Secretary of State made
Y a statement expressing the deep concern of the United States over
the disturbed situations in various parts of the world. The Jap-
C anese pross published an extensive summary of the Secretary's state-
 ment based on a Domei report. No comment by Japanese officials or
N by the Japanese press came to the Eabassy's attention excepting
F that contained in an editorial in the NICHI NICHI of July 18, which
I incidentally cited the Secretary's statement and asserted that
D Japanese rights in the present North China incident rested solely
E on the Boxer Protocol and that Japan was persistently endeavoring
 to prevent aggravation of the situation.**
Acting under telegraphic instructions received from the Dopart-
 ment, the Ambassador called on the Minister for Foreign Affairs at
4. 5:30 on July 22 and read to him the statements in the Department's
: telegram covering the interview which the Socretary of State had
bad with the Japanese Ambassador in Washington on the morning of
July 21.*** Among these statements were the following: that the
Secretary of State had told the Japanese Ambassador that the Am-
erican government would be glad at any time to say or do anything
short of miediation (which of course would require agreement of
both parties in advance) that might in any way whatever contribute
toward oomposing the present matters of controversy between Japan
and China, and that the Secretary had made it clear that he was in-
viting voluntary suggestions on the part of the Japanese or Chinese
Governments

(a) China.
7939
On the night of July 7 and in the early morning of July 8 an
armed clash took place at Lukouchiao (Marco Polc Bridge, 10 miles
二
southwest of Peiping) between troops of the Japanese North China
Garrison, who were reportedly executing maneuvers, and troops of the
29th Chinese Army. According to a Japanese newspaper extra published
o the morning of July 9, news despatches from Peiping received at
ta: Jananese War Office stated that mediation by tho Mayor of Tientsin
inc tusulted in an oral agreement providing for tho withdrawal of
tie apanese forces north of the Yungting River and of the Chinese
forces south of the River. However, fighting broke out again on the
vening of July 10 and continued during the early morning of July 11.
The Japanese authorities in Tokyo took a serious view of the
irhting which had occurred during the evening of July 10 and the
a1ly morning of July 1l, and on the afternoon of July 1l the Cabinot
in dc public a resolution the substance of which was that the fighting
ulsequent to the oral plan of settlement was a matter of deliberate
rinese military planning against Japan and that although the Japan-
 Government had not abandoned hope that negotiations might yet
ri vent aggravation of the situation, the Cabinet had decided to take
-i nicessary measures for despatching military forces to North China.

A written agreement providing for the withdrawal of Chinesc and
Japanese troops from the Yungting River was reaohed on the evening
of July ll by Japanese North China Garrison authorities and repre-
sentatives of the Hopei-Chahar Political Council. According to in-
formation furnished to the Embassy by the Japanese Foreign Orrice, th.
agreement further proided for replacement of the 29th Army troops by
troops of the Chinese Peace Preservation Corps, apology for the inci-
dent by General Sung Che-yuan, Commander of the 29th Army, punishment
of the Chinese officers responsible for the clashes, and measures to
be taken by the North China authorities against the anti-Japanese
activities of the Blue Shirts and communists.
On the following morning, however, further fighting reportedly
occurred near Peiping. The Japanese military authorities in North
China took the position that this fighting had been entirely due to
the actions of the 29th Army troops and that the latter had broken the
written agreement of July ll.
On July 13 there were indications of a strong and unanimous
determination on the part of the Japanese Government to resist,
?ven at tho cost of extensive hostilities, any movement which
might tend to weaken Japan's position in North China.*
An official of the Japanese Foreign Office stated to a member
of the Embassy's staff on July 14 that future developments in North
China would dopend on (1) whether the Blue Shirts would incite the
9th Army to further anti-Japanese activities, and (2) whether the
anking Government would send troops north of Paoting, Hopei Province,
in contravention of the Ho-Umezu agreement of 1935. This official
stated cmphatically that Japan would not permit Nanking Government
troops to proceed north of this point. **

Although little fighting was reported from July 1l to July 17,
the situation remained tense. On the latter date Mr. Hidaka,
Counselor of the Japanese Embassy at Nanking, called on the Chinese
Minister for Foreign Affairs and handed him a memorandum, the terms
of which, according to Mr. Hidaka's subsequent statements, were the
following: (1) the Chinese Government should cease provocative acts
(which, Mr. Hidaka, explained, meant cease sending troops into Hopei
Province); and (2) the Japanese Government should cease impeding the
local negotiations in the north.*
The Embassy was informed by the Foreign Office that on July 18
General Sung Che-yuan had tendered an apology to General Kiyoshi
Kazuki, Commander of the Japanese North China Garrison, for the oc-
currence of the Lukouchiao incidents, in accordance with the terms of
the July ll agreement.**
On July 19, in reply to the Japanese memorandum of July 17, an
official of the Chinese Foreign Office delivered a note to the
Counselor of the Japanese Embassy at Nanking containing the following
four points: (1) the two countries should agree on a date when move-
ments of their military forces would cease, and these forces would
be recalled to their original positions; (2) diplomatic negotiations
should be opened for sottlement of the dispute; (3) the authorizstion
of the Nanking Government is essential for any agreement concluded
on the spot; and (4) China is willing to accept any means of settle-
rent recognized by international law and treaties. In the evening
of the same day the Japanese Foreign Office issued a statement appro-
oos of the Chinese memorandum charging the Chinese Government with
ittempting to befog the issue and asserting that the only issue was
hether or not the Nanking Governmont would continue to obstruct
'nnlementation of the July ll local agreement. ***

terminate all troops movements in the affected area if similar action
were taken by the Japanese Government and that as a preliminary
neasure the Chinese Minister for Foreign Affairs was considering pro-
posing that on July 17 all troop movements on both sides should stop.**
Mr. Eden stated in the House of Commons on July 22 that as long
is the present situation in North China persisted it did not seem
nportune to open the conversations with Japan with regard to the
nclusion of a political agreement to which the British Government
ins looking forward.

(r) Notherlands India.
EA
Aocording to information furnished the Embassy by the Nether-
lands Chargé d'Affaires, the Netherlands India-Japan trade nego-
tiations in Tokyo came to an end during the month without agreement,
since the delegates had been unable to reach an understanding on the
apportionment of quotas for Japanese exports to the Netherlands
Indies between Japanese exporters and Netherlands Indies exporters
in Japan.
(g) Italy.
A Domei report from Rome dated July 27 stated that the letters
of credence of Mr. Hotta, the new Japanese Ambassador to Italy,
which were addressed to "The King-Emperor", were generally con-
sidered in Rome as official recognition by the Japanese Government
of Italy's conquest of Abyssinia and of the creation of an African
Empire.
(n) Canada.
The JAPAN TIMES of July 9 reported that owing to the recent
increase in prices of Canadian wheat and strengthening of the
Foreign Exchange Control Law imports of wheat from Canada would be
nuch smaller this year than formerly.
ITI. INTERNAL POLITICAL CONDITIONS.
(a) Cabinet.
Several Cabinet plans, such as those for the creation of a
7942
Vinistry of Health and for reform of the House of Peers, appeared to
nave been shelved by the Government as a result of the latter's pre-
occupation in the North China situation. In fact, interest in the
internal political situation appeared to be overshadowed by the
concern

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
concern felt by the Government, the press, and the people in general
over the North China dirficulties.
(b) The Diet.
844.32
Until the close of the month the proceedings of the special Diet :
Ic, which was convened on July 23, were conoerned largely with
the North China situation.* The Government's supplementary budgets
for financing Japanese military operations in China were quickly
passed by both Houses.**
In an address delivered before the Diet on July 27 Premier
Konoe stated that in sending troops to North China the Japanese
Government had no other purpose than to "preserve the peace of
East Asia" and that he hoped most fervently that reconsideration
and self-discipline on the part of the Government and people of
China would make speedily possible a fundamental adjustment of
Sino-Japanese relations. Subsequently, in reply to an interpellation,
the Premier said that what Jepan wanted of China was not territory
but cooperation.
Mr. Hirota, the Minister for Foreign Affairs, delivered an
cddress to the Diet in which he said that he believed that foreign
governments understood fully Japan's attitude of "patience and self-
restraint" with regard to North China. General Sugiyama, the War
inister, told the Diet that the origin of the present North China
ji ficulties was the general anti-Japanese movement in China which
"as an outcome of the Nanking Goverrment's thorough anti-Japanese
folicy and of China's over-estimation of its national strength.
On July 28 Premier Konoe stated in reply to an interpellation
that in his opinion it was premature to talk at the present time of
rarthrowing the National Government since there were many persons
i.1 that Government who understood Japan, including General Chiang
Kai-shek.

At the conference of provincial governors on July 15 the

VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
EA
(a) Foreign Trade.
Exports from Japan for July amounted to ¥294,267,000 while im-
ports into Japan amounted to ¥373,633,000 as compared with June
exports of ¥281,195,000 and imports of ¥382,318,000. This repre-
sented an unfavorable balance of trade of ¥79,366,000 as compared
with an unfavorable balance for June of ¥101,123,000.
(b) Industries.
It was stated by the press that the Nippon Seitetsu Kaisha, a
semi-official company embracing the Yawata Iron Works and several
private iron foundries, was preparing a "mobilization of iron and
steel" in connection with the North China emergency and had already
notified wholesale dealers in iron and steel that they might in the
future be asked to restrict their sales.
(c) Petroleum.
Domei reported that the Government had decided to open coal
fields in the Japanese half of Saghalien in preparation for launch-
ing a large scale coal liquefaction enterprise for the production of
petroleum.
VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.
VIII. ACTIVITIES OF FOREIGN COMMERCIAL INTERESTS.
Nothing to report.

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
IX. LABOR AND EMIGRATION.
(a) Labor.
A survey by the Home Orrice published during the month revealed
894.504
that there had been 1,332 labor disputes during the first five months
of 1937, almost double the number for the same period of 1936, and
that l69,797 persons had participated in these disputes or fivo times
as many as during the same period last year.
About five hundred members of the Kobe Municipal Streetcar
Employees' Union started a strike for higher wages on July 6.
According to most newspaper reports, the Hyogo prefectural authori-
ties issued a warning to the employees that the strike must end im-
modiately in view of the North China situation and the strikers re-
turned to work on July 19. The JAPAN CHRONICLE of July 20 reported,
however, that the strikers had "voluntarily" decided to return to
work.
(b) Emigration.
According to Domei, on July 2 the Cabinet approved plans for
the creation of a new "Manchukuo" Development Company for the pur-
pose of sending five million Japanese to "Manchukuo" within the
next twenty years. It was said that the new company would be cap-
italized at ¥50,000,000 and would absorb the existing "Manchukuo"
Development Company which was established with a capitalization of
only ¥ 15,000,000.
X. MISCKLLANEOUS. Nothing to report.

IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION:
A number of importent Government bills were passed by the

convoked for two rensors: (1) to provide funds to meet the ex-
penses of military oneretions in North Chine, and (2) to cnact
legislation which would eneble the Government more effectively
to conduct milit& ry op rctions against the Chirese. The supple-
me'tary budgcts for financi & militsry onerations in China ere
teken up in Section V of the present report.

the Diet, the Government's objectives in drafting these emer-
gency bills were to mobilize Japanese industry on a wartime

VI.
GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS:
(a) Foreign Trade.
Exports from Japan for Aufust amounted to ¥250,34l,000,
while imports into this country were ¥302,954,000, as compared
with July exports of ¥294,267,000 and imports of ¥373,633,000.
This represented an unfavorable balance of trade of only
Y52,613,000, as comnared with an unfavorable balance for July
of Y79,366,000. However, at this season of the year exports
usually exceed imports and thcrefore the unfavorable balence
of trade for the month is a reflection of the Sino-Japanese
hostilities and the necessity of importing large amounts of raw
materials for the munitions industries.
The Ministry of Commerce and Industries reportedly completed
its dreft of en emergency bill, to be presented to the Diet in
September, granting the Commerce and Industry Minister wide
authority in the control of goods involved in foreign trade.
(b) Shipping.
The Ministry of Communications was said to be considering
a bill to be presented at the September session of the Diet
providing broad control of shipping and of the shinbuilding in-
dustry.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Representations ade by the Embassy in Connection
with the Sino-Japanese Conflict.
In accordance with the Department's instructions, on Septem-
ber 1 the Ambassador read aloud and handed to the Minister for
Toreign Affairs an aide-mémoire protestirg against the bombing
of Nanking, asking that Japan discontinue activities which re-
sulted in the indiscriminate destruction of non-military property
and in the wounding and painful death of civilians, and requesting
in general that the Japanese Government reirain from attacks upon
defenceless cities, hospitals, trains, and motor cars throughout
China.* In addition, the Ambassador appealed orally to Mr. Hirota
on the basis of humanity as well as practical wisdom to take steps
to avoid the serious risk of injuring Japanese-Americen relations,
which vould inevitably occur if American citizens should be in-
jured by the apparently indiscriminate bombing operations being
carried on by Japanese forces in various parts of China. Mr.
Grew stressed Mr. Hirota's responsibility for guiding the political
relations of Japan, which might be considcred as paramount to mili-
tary considerations. The Minister for Foreign Affairs rerlied to
the Ambassador that it was the intention of the Japanese forces in
China to attack only military establishments but that mistakes
vore unfortunately inevitablo and that he would bring the American
mhassador's representations to the attention of the War and Navy
Ministries.
In reply to its representations of September 1, on September 16
tl. Embassy received from the Foreign Office an aide-mémoire dated
'ember 15, the substance of which was that the Japanese must con-
tinue
Department's telegrams No. 182 and No. 183, August 30, 1937;
Embassy's telegram No. 335, September 1, 1937.

tinue bombing operations against Chinese military organs and es-
tablishments in Nanking; that the Japanese Government was prepared
to do whatever lay in its power to facilitate the withdrawal of
nationals of third countries from China in general and to afford
protection to their property; and that nothing was further from the
thought of the Japanese forces than to make attacks in China upon
defenceless cities, hospitals, trains and motor cars not used by
the Chinese for military purposes.*
Pursuant to the Department's instructions, the Ambassador
called on the Minister for Foreign Affairs on September 20 and
nade to him emphatic representations concerning the announced

emnhasized the serious effect on American public opinion which

States and in the world at large; stated that by her policy and

of the world a liability of suspicion, distrust, popular antipathy,

ievolved upon Mr. Hirota to guide Japan's foreign relations and

eastern part of Shansi Province on the Peipine-Suiyuan Railway,
was captured by the Japarese on September 13. After a battle
said by Domei to have been the largest engagement in which the
Japanese Army had taken part since the Battle of Mukden against
the Russians in 1905, the Japanese occupied Chochow on the
Peiping-Hankow Railway on September 17. A week later they cap-
tured Paoting, the point at which there had been a large concen-
tration of Chinese Central Army troops since the early stages of
the conflict in North China. It was reported by Domei that by

south of Paoting. On September 24, the day of the occupation of
Paoting, Tsangchow, reportedly an important strategic point on
the Tientsin-Bukow Railway, was taken possession of by the Japanese
forces operating along that line.
Meanwhile, Japanese air raids continued both against coastal
and interior cities in Central and South China. Nanking and Canton
were repeatedly bombed from the air. The Japanese authorities re-
ported heavy damage to military establishments in those cities while
the Chinese authoritics, as well as certain foreign observers, re-
ported slight damages to military objectives but large losses of
civilian life and serious damages to non-military establishments.
Japanese airplanes also bombed the following places: Hankow,
Swatow, the Boca Tigris forts on the estuary of the Canton River,
fortifications on Bias Bey, and Shekwan, on the southwestern coast
of Kwantung Province.

III. INTERIJAL POLITICAL CONDITIONS.
(a) The Diet.
794.32
The 72nd Session of the Diet was formally convened on Sep-
tember 4 and was adjourned on September 8, the Government's emer-
gency budget and all the Government's bills having been passed.
The lerislation enacted is taken up in Section Iv of the present
report.
On September 5 addresses were delivered by the Minister
for Foreign Affairs, the Premier, the Minister for Finance, the
War Minister, and thc Navy Minister. After outlining the Japancse
Government's version of the present hostilities in China, with
particular reference to the "provocetive and anti-Japanese attitude"
of the Chinese, Mr. Hirota stressed the following points:
that
the Nanking Government and the local militarist régimes in China
had for many years been inciting Chinese public opinion aainst
Japan as a means of strengthening their own political influence;
that in collusion with communist clements they had still further
impaired Sino-Japanese relations; that obviously the basis policy
of

=== Page 471 === (confidence 0.99)
of the Jepanese Government aimed at the stabilization of Eest Asia
throuch conciliation and cooperation emong Jepen, "Manchukuo", and
Chine for their common prosperity cnd well-bcing; that China, ig-
noring Jepan's true motive, had mobilized her "vest armies" against
Japan, with the result that Jepan hcd hcd to resort to force of arms;
that Japan hać no other objective than to see a "happy and tranquil
North China"; and that Japan wanted all China to be freed from the
danger of a recurrence of such calamitous hostilities as the present.
The Minister for Foreicn Affairs ended his speech by expressing the
hope that the statesmen of China would turn over a new leaf and
would act in unison with the "high aims and aspirations of Japan,"*
PremierHirota asserted that since the outbreak of the
"affair in North China" on Juiy 7 the fundamental policy of the
Japanese Government toward China had been simply and purely to seek
the reconsideration of the Chinese Government and the abandonment of
its erroneous anti-Japanese policies, with a view toward making a
basis readjustment in the relations between the two countries;
that because of the acgravation of the situation by the Chinese, Japan
had been forced to deal a "firm and decisive blow" agaiist the
Chinese Governnent; that the adoption by one country as a nation-
al policy of a movement such as that edopted by China against Japan,
includine the underlying principle of national education by im-
planting such a policy in the minds of the young, was "unprecedented
in the history of the world"; that Japan was firmly convinced that
it was in accordance with "the right of self-defense and with the
cause of righteousness and humanity" that Japan had armed to give
a decisive blow to such a country, so that it might reflect upon
the erroFs of its ways; and that at the present moment the sole
measure for the Japanese Empire to adopt was to administer a
thoroughgoing blow to the Chinese Army so that it might lose
completely its will to fight.**

(d) The Emperor.
It will be recalled that Dr. Tatsukichi Minobe, former
university professor and author of the theory that the Emperor
of Japan is merely an institution, has been persecuted for some
time. In February 1936 Dr. Minobe was shot and wounded by Juse
Oda, a member of the Daito Kogyo Jyuku, a reactionary organization.
On September 2s the Tokyo District Criminal Court sentenced Oda
to six years of penal servitude for his attempt to assassinate
Dr. Minobe.

IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
The September session of the Diet was convoked for the same
Ieasons as the emergency session of the Diet which was convened on
Tuly 28 and ended on August 8, namely, to provide funds to meet the
:xpenses of military operations in Chine and to enact legislation
which would enable the Government more effectively to conduct mili-
tary operations against China.* The Diet performed its duty in
these lines in a war atmosphere to tne full satisfaction of the
Covernment.** The followine war measures were enected: a law
providine for a public loan for military expenditures; e law
cstablishing e military expenditure eccount; a law revising foreign
exchanre control; e law providing for the mobilization of the muni-
tions industry; a law providing for the transfer of horses for
military use; laws esteblishing temportery grain control and tem-
norory fertilizer control; laws providinf for temporary import
"nd export control and temporery shippine control;
. a lew stipu-
luting that soldiers at the front shall be relieved of taxes and a
law providing for a temporary adjustment of funds.
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
As already stated, an energency military budget of over two
billion yen was approved by the Diet on September 9. According
to reliable informetion reccived by the Embassy, this emergency
budeet wus divided into the following items when presented to
the Dict: Army - ¥ 1,422, 712,777; Navy - ¥ 349,958,381; and
Reserve - ¥ 250,000,000.
The totel budget upproved by the end
of Septenber amountcd to ¥ 5,474,566,294, a tremendous increase
over the budcet for tie fiscal year 1931-32 of ¥ 1,476,000,000,
over the budret for 1936-37 of ¥ 2,307,000,000 and also over the
basic budget for 1937-38 approved at the 70th session of the Diet
in March of ¥ 2,813,937,971.***

VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONLITIONS.
(a) Foreign Trade.*
Japan's exports for September were ¥ 292,300,000 and im-
ports into this country were ¥ 263,081,000, as compared with ex-
ports for August of ¥ 250,341,000 and imports of ¥ 302,954,000.
This represented a favorable belance of trede of ¥ 29,219,000 in
contrast to an unfavorable trade balance for Au ust of ¥ 52,613,000.
The increase of exports was due to seasonal factors, while the
decline in i ports was accounted for by the various covernmental
measures restrictinc imports.
Mr. Shinji Yoshino, the Minister for Commerce and Industry,
stated in & newspaper interviev on September 25 that economic op-
eraticns in North China must be started as soon as peace and order
were restored and that it would be necessary to purchase cotton
and wool from North China in oraer to increase the purchasing
power of the peo le in that territory.
On September 20 t.e Finance Ministry informed representatives
of ten lcadin distributors of forein movine picture films that,
with the exception of news reels, no more foreien moving picture
films would be allowed to enter Japan during the rest of the
present calendar year, in view of the fact that foreien exchange
must be rcserved for essentiels, and that the products of
"Hollywood and other foreirn movie centers" were not regarded as
nccessary for the successful tcrmination of the campain in China.

(c) Industries.
According to the NICHT NICHI of September 24, two of Japen's
largest industries, spinning and foodstuff menufacturing, have
been classified under the Emergcncy Capital Readjustment Law, which
has been onerative since September 9, as non-urgent industries
which do not require expansion or improvement for the time being.
The NICHI NICHI also stated that almost all Japanese industries,
exceptine the munitions industries, would be forbidden to expand
equipment, incrcase capitalization, or issue debentures without
Government permission.
The CIIUGAI of September 28 reported that in accordance with

disputes should be settled by peeceful und rational means,
and strikes in all branches of industry should be abolished.

Cooperation Committec shell bo orgnized joi tly by the
1
ropresontetives of labor, capitrl, and the Government.

as a nation-l labor policy."

1.
RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Representations made by the Embassy in Conection with the
Sino-Japanese Conflict.
711.94
Following a telegraphic request received from the Consulate
General at Shanghai, the Embassy made oral representations to the
Japanese Foreign Cffice on November 1 urging that since all Japan-
ese forces had withdrawn from the proxinity of the Hongkew and
Yenepoo areas of the International Settlement of Shanghai, those
ristricts should now be thrown open without delay cr restrictions

On November 27 Sir Robert Craigie informed Mr. Grew that it was
upon instructions issued to them by General Matsui and not from the
Japanese Foreign Office that Japanese civil officials at Shanghai
refused to discuss the customs question with British and other
third party representatives. In a telegram to the Department
dated November 27 the Ambassador stated that he shared the British
Ambassador's concern over the customs issue in view of the possi-
bility that the Japanese military might force an objectionable ar-
rangement upon the local Chinese customs officials before something
could be done to stop it.
Therefore, the Ambassador recommended to
the Department thet action should be taken most urgently to pro-
tect and preserve tangible American interests in China, namely,
the integrity of the Shanghai customs ana the disposition of its
revenues. The Ambessador recommended parallel, not joint, action
and a formal signed note to the Minister for Foreign Affairs. Con-
curring with the Ambassador's recommendations, the Lepartment in-
structed him to address to the Minister for Foreien Affairs a formal
signed note to the following effect: th United States Govern-

 mcan Far Eastern policy.
It will be recalled that adverse criticism of the American Far
Eastern policy abated toward the end of October. Notwithstanding
the participation of the United States in the Nine Power Treaty
Conference at Brussels and rumors thet the United States was more
responsible for the holding of the Conference than the other Powers
concerned, surprisingly little invective against the United States
appeared in the Japanese press. As the sessinns of the Conference
proceeded and as it became more and more apparent that no action
would be taken by the Conference in an effort to coerce Japan,
criticism of American policy became less and less manifest.

The Japanese Army forces in China were not particularly active

Suiyuan fronts, but they made a rapid advance against the Chinese
forces which had been driven out of the Shanhai area and were at-

Nanking. According to announcements issued by the Japenese Army,

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Sinking of the U.S.S. Gunboat PANAY by Japanese
Airplanes.
On December 1 the Embassy brought to the attention of
the Japanese Foreign Office a report received from the
Embassy at Nanking that the U.S.S. PANAY was at Nanking
and requested that the appropriate Japanese military and
naval authorities be promptly notified of this report.
a
On the basis of telegrams received fron. the Embassies
in Nanking and Hankow with regard to the jeopardizing by
こ94.
the Japanese military forces of the U.S.S. Gunboat PANAY
and of American refugees on Standard Oil Company ships, the
Ambassador called on the Minister for Foreign Affairs on
the morning of December 13 and requested him to take furth-
er steps to restrain Japanese military forces from continuing
the sericus risks to American lives and property involved
in the dropping of shells in the vicinity of these ships.*
Later in the day it became known that the U.S.S.
PAvAY and two Standard Oil Company ships had been sunk by
Japanese naval airplanes above Nanking on the Yangtze River
on the afternoon o1 December 12. Mr. Hirota called per-
sonally on Mr. Grew at the Chancery on the afternoon of
December 13 and expressed the profound apologies of the
Japanese Government for the sinking of the PANAY.
A high official of the Foreign Office called on the
Ambassador on the afternoon of Decenber 14 and delivered
a note from the Ministor for Foreicn Affairs which stated
that
* Embassy's despatch No. 2710, Decemher 23, 1937.

=== Page 562 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
that the sinking of the PANAY had been due to a mistake aris-
ing from low visibility; that the Japanese Government re-
gretted the incident most profoundly, desired to make sincere
apologies, would make indemnification and would appropriate-
ly deal with those responitl: ror the incident; and that the
Japanese Government had already issued strict orders to the
authorities on the spot with a view to preventing a recurrence
of a similar incident.
Pursuant to the Department's instructions, the Ambassa-
dor called on the Minister for Foreign Affairs on the evening
of December 14 and read and delivered to him a signed note
of protest against the incident. The note outlined the cir-
cumstances ettendant upon the sinking of the PANAY; referred
to previous acts of Japanese armed forces in China which had
violated the riehts of the United States and had seriously
endangered the lives of American nationals;
and stated that
the American Government requested and expected of the Jap-
anese Government a formally recorded expression of regret,
an undertaking to make complete and comprehensive indemni-
fications, and an assurance that definite end specific steps
had been teken which would ensure that hereafter American
naticnals, interests, and properties in China would not be
subject to attack by Japanese armed forces or unlawful inter-
ference by any Japanese authorities or forces whatsoever.*
At first the authorities of the Japanese Foreign, Navy,
and Wer Offices denied reports that after the bombing of
the PANAY the gunboat and its survivors had been machine
gunned by Jepanese surface craft.

II. RELATIONS WITH CTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
(1) Capture of Nanking and Other Cities in the
Shanghai-Nanking Area.
The Japanese forces continued their rapid advance against
Nanking. After several days of fighting ardund the city, in
which the Chinese resistance was described as "stubborn" by
the Japanese news agency, Domei, Japanese troops took possess-
ion of the capital on December 14. Meanwhile, according to
an International News Service despatch from Nanking dated
December 7, General Chiang Kai-shek had on that day fled by
airplane from Nanking to Hankow, accompanied by his wife and
Mr. Donald, his adviser, eluding the Jupanese planes which
pursued him. The Japanese Embassy in Nenking was re-opened
on December 18 with the return from Shenghai of the Counselor,
Mr.
Department's telegram No. 319, November 28, and Embassy's

No. 2689, December 9, 1937.

=== Page 573 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
Mr. Shinrokuro Hidaka. According to Domei, a "Peace Mainte-
nance Commission" was inaugurated in Nanking on December 23
with a platform based on opposition to communism, severance
of relations with General Chiang Kai-shek's National Govern-
ment, and a policy of friendship toward Japan.
On December 2l Japanese forces operating on the north-
ern bank of the Yangtze River captured Chuhsien, on the
Tientsin-Pukow Railway. Three days later Japanese units
occupied the city of Hangchow, capital of Chekiang Province,
apparently without resistance on the part of the Chinese.
The Japanese took possession of Tienchang on December 26.
(2) Japanese Military Operations in Shantung
Province.
Having secured control of Shantung Province north of the
Yellow River, Japanese units proceeded south of the River and,
meeting with little Chinese opposition, on December 26 took
possession of Tsinan, capital of the Province and headquarters
of General Han Fu-chu's forces.
Following the destruction by Chinese of Japanese-owned
cotton mills in Tsingtao, on December 23 the Japanese military
hoadquarters in Tientsin issued a comnuniqué to the effect
that the Japarese forces were prepared to iflict thorough-
going chastisement on the Chinese for their breach of faith
and intended to demand appropriate compensation for Japanese
property destroyed. Vice Admiral Hasegawa, Commander-in-Chief
of the Japanese Fleet in China waters, made an announcement
on December 26 that Tsingtao would be included in the zone of
Japanese "naval interruption of maritime traffic established
on September 5.

(3) Activities of the Japanese Air Forces.
Japanese Navy and Army airplanes conducted raids through-

and South China, including Canton. Domei reported that in

Japanese naval planes had bombed and destroyed the airdrome

(4) Official Japanese Statements.
I a statement setting forth the views of the Japanese
Government on the situation created by the occupation of
Nankine, Premier Kenoye declared on December 14 that there
was no longer any substance behind the claim of the Nanking
Government to be the central régime of China; that the régime
was swiftly being reduced to rothing more than a war machine;
that if a new régime should arise, Japan would have no choice
but to consider concrete measures for co-existence and co-
prosperity with it; that no régime in China could succeed
excepting on & pro-Japanese foundation; that only through
the emergence of such a pro-Japanese China could the interests
of European Powers and of the United States in the Far East
be guaranteed; and that in order to prevent a recurrence of
such a tragedy as the present China incident, Japan must not
hesitate

=== Page 575 === (confidence 0.99)
