hesitate "to perform a major operation". Also apropos of
the capture of Nanking, both the War Minister and the military
section of the Imperial Headquarters announced that Japan
must make redoubled efforts for the achievement of its ob-
jecties. in China and thet the termination of hostilities
must be regerded as a matter for the remote future. In a
statement on December 17 regarding the formal entry of Jap-
anese troops into Nanking, Vice Admiral Havegawa, Commander
in-Chief of the Japanese Naval Forces in China waters, asserted
that the Japanese troops, not contented with the capture of
Nanking, were determined to continue the fight until the "aims
of the campaign" had been completely attained. None of the
officials mentioned gave any definite inuication of what
"the aims of the campaign" were.
(5) Autonomous Régimes in China Sponsored
by the Japanese.
Additional evidence came to light during the month under
review concerning the manner in which Japan evidently pro-

China, while refraining ostensibly from infringing on China's
territorial integrity.
This evidence
consisted in the forma-
tion under Japanese pressure of several pro-Japanese régimes
in portions of China which had been subjugated by the invad-
ing forces of the Japanese. The inauguration of a "Peace
Maintenance Commission" in Nanking has been mentioned.
was reported that in Januery 1938 an autonomous régime would
be founded in Nanking. The so-called "Provisional Government
of China" was organized in Peiping on Decembef 14 with such
Chinese as the Japanese military were able to persuade to
become officials. It was stated that the principles of the
new

(b) Great Britain.
(1) Japanese Attacks on British Naval Vessels
LADYBIRD AND BEE
Reports were received by the British Embassy that the
British naval vessels LADYBIRD and BEE, as well as British
merchant vessels, all in the vicinity of Wuhu on the Yangtze
River, had on December 12 been attacked by Japanese machine
guns and field pieces; that the LADYBIRD had suffered four
direct hits with one seaman killed, another seaman seriously
wounded, and several men receiving minor injuries, including
the flag captain, and that the BEE had been fired upon but
with no damage resulting. On December 13 the British Ambassa-
dor protested personally in the most emphatic manner to the
Minister for Foreign Affairs against these attacks, stating
that "British public orinion was bound tc take a most serious
view of this incident" and urging that the Japanese Govern-
ment make a suitable apology as soon as possible. Mr. Hirota
called in person on ir Robert Craigie on the afternoon of
December 14 and expressed apology and regrets on behalf of
the Japanese Government.
On December 14 the British Ambassador addressed to the
Minister for Foreign Affairs a letter to the effect that the
Japanese order to fire on all ships moving on the Yangtze
appeared still to be in force and that furthermore the guns
of Japanese batteries were still trained pointblank on the
LADYBIRD and the BEE.
Sir Robert Craigie stated with regard
to the first point that it appeared to him to disclose on the
part of the responsible Japanese military authorities in
China a regrettable disregard for the consequences of their
action of December 12, and with regard to the second point
that

=== Page 579 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
that he emphatically protested and requested that immediate
instructions be sent to the Japanese commander at Wuhu to
desist from "this threatening and wholly unnecessary action."
Acting under instructions received from his Government,
on December 16 the British Ambessador addressed to the Minister
for Foreign Affairs a note protesting against the attacks made
by Japanese aircraft and land forces on the British warships
LADYBIRD and BEE and on British merchant vessels at Wuhu and
near Nanking on December 12. The note expressed the satis-
faction of the British Government at having received the uote
of the Minister for Foreign Affairs dated December 14, which
offered the profound apology of the Japanese Government for
the attacks on the British vessels, stated that measures had
been immediately taken to prevent the reourrence of such in-
would
cidents, and added that the Japanese Government/deal suitably
with those responsible for the incidents and would pay the
necessary compensation. In addition, the British note re-
quested that an assurance be given that all that had been
said in the Japenese note applied equally to Japanese attacks
on British merchant vessels. In conclusion, the British
Government asked to be informed what measures had actually
been taken of a character which would put a definite stop to
these incidents.*
On December 18 the British Embassy re-
ceived the Foreign Office's provisional reply, which stated
that the assurances given in the Foreign Office's note of
December 14 with regard to British warships were equally ap-
plicable to the British merchant vessels which were attacked
in similar circumstances on December 12.

Embassy's telegram No. 642, December 16, 1937.

The British Ambassador received on December 28 the final
reply of the Minister for Foreign Affairs to the formal British
representations of December l6. The reply stated that the
attacks upon British vessels had been a mistake; that as
soon as the vessels were known to be British, Japanese bomb-
ing and military units had ceased firing and offered medical
assistance; that with reference to the reported statement of
a Japanese officer that he had received orders to fire on
all vessels on the Yangtze River, the orders issued referred
only to vessels used by the Chinese; that in order to ensure
egainst recurrence of similar incidents those responsible
were being dealt with according to law; and that further to
guard against recurrence of such incidents greater caution
would be exercised in areas where there were vessels of third
Powers even if it should entail permitting Chinese forces to
escape.* Pursuant to instructions received from the British
Government, on December 31 Sir Robert Craigie addressed to
Mr. Hirota a note in reply, the substance of which was that
the British Government noted with satisfaction that the
Japanese Government had taken or was prepered to take the
necessary measures to deal suitably with the officers res-
ponsible for the incidents end to prevent any repetition.
The British note ended by stating that the details of the
Japanese Government's measures and their effective applica-
tion might in the opinion of the British Government suitably
form the subject of further conversations in the course of
which the British Government did not doubt that it would be
informed of actual steps decided upon.

(3) Anti-British Feeling in Japan.
Anti-British feeling in Japan was somewhat abated during
the month by expressions of regret over the incident involv-
ing the British naval vessels LADYBIRD and BEE. However, the
Japanese continued to criticize Great Britain for its alleged-
ly un-neutral action in supplying arms and munitions to China,
for the "unsympathetic attitude" of the British authorities
in Chira, and for what the Japanese chose to call Great
Britain's policy of befriending and encouraging China and of
obstructing Japan in its campaign to "establish lasting peace
in the Far East."
(c) Soviet Russia.
Japanese-Soviet Russian relations, in which danger is al-
ways latent, were ruffled on the surface by three controver-
sies: the fisheries issue, the continued detention by Soviet
authorities of seven Japanese subjects, and the forced migration
into remote parts of the Soviet Union of a large number of
Korean residents of the Maritime Provinces.
Despite Japan's insistence that the Soviet Government
reconsider its attitude and agree to sign a new fisheries
convention to replace the convention of l928, the Soviet
Union reiused to come to terms. A comnittee of members of
the Upper and Lower Houses called on the Minister for For-
eign Affairs on December e and presented to him a resolution
to the effect that the Soviet attitude toward a new fisher-
ies convention could not be overlooked from the stendpoint
of international good faith and protection of Japan's rights
and interests. Since the term of duration of the Japanese-
Soviet

=== Page 584 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
Soviet fishery convention signed on January 23, l928, and
prolonged by the protocol of December 28, 1936, was due to
come to an end on December 31, 1937, the two governments
signed a protocol on December 29, 1937, providing that the
convention of 1928 shall remain in force until December 31,
1938. The Russian Waters Fishing Guild, composed of firms
engaged in fishing off Kamchetka, issued a statement through
Domei on December 30 to the effect that the Guild was not
at all satisfied with "such a provisional settlenent achieved
through patchwork" and was resolved to do everything possible
to obtain a new agreement which would respect Japan's rights
and interests accruing from the Portsmouth Treaty.
On December 17 the Foreign Office handed to the Soviet
Embassy in Tokyo a memorandum protesting against the con-
tinuous detention since March 27, 1937, of seven Japanese
subjects on churges of espionage and other "grave offenses"
against the Soviet Union. The Japanese memorandum was ac-
companied by a request for "a responsitle al.swer" at the
earliest possible date.
Mr. Kawai, the Foreign Office spokesnan, issued a state-
mert on Lecember 6 concerning the Soviet Government's dis-
regard of Japan's repeated protests against tne Soviet policy
of forcing Koreans living in the Maritime Province to migrate
to distant parts of the Soviet Union. Mr. Kawai said that
Japan could not recognize the Soviet contention that the Koreans
were Soviet citizens, and that unless the Mosccw authorities
modified their attitude on the issue the Japanese Government
would be obliged to take what it deemed to be appropriate
measures.

(r) Spain.
The Japanese Government formally extended recognition
to the Franco Government of Spain on December 1 and recognized
Mr. F. J. del Castillio as Spanish Chargé d'affaires in Tokyo.
On this occasion the Foreign Cffice spokesman issued the
following statement:
"Although unfortunately the internecine war
in Spain has not yet come to an end, the extension
of formal recognition to the Government under Gen-
eral Franco is in conformity with the realities of
the situation, since that Government not only controls
more than a half of the territory of Spain and all of
the colonies, tut has built up a solid foundation as
a government. The administrative and judicial functions
are being carried on normally in the occupied areas in
sharp contrast to the chaotic and anarchical conditions
prevailing in Valencia and Barcelona.
"That the Spanish disturbance originated as a
result of the activities of the People's Front created
through the Comintern's intrigues and machinations is
clear in the light of the circumstances surrounding
its outbreak. General Franco has been fighting for
the express purpose of expelli.g Communism and Anarchism
since July last year, when the outbreak began there,
and it is generally known that both Germany and Italy,
united in the common aim of guarding against the Comin-
tern, extended recognition to the Franco Government in
November last year.
"It is a matter for sincere congratulation that
through the present recognition, friendly relations have
been e stablished between our country and the Franco
Government with which we are in agreement regarding
the policy to be pursued toward the Comintern. Simul-
taneously we find deep significance in this recognition,
as being certain to promote the intimate relations now
existing between Japan, Germany, and Italy."
Two days later a ceremony at which the flag of the Franco
Government was hoisted was held at the Spanish Legation and
was attended by the Minister for Foreign Affairs and the
German, Italian, and "Manchukuo" Ambassadors.

(g) "Manchukuo".

cerning rights of the Japanese military forces ir "Manchukuo"
territory was signed by General Chang Ching-hui, Premier of

"Manchukuo" and Commander of the Kwantung Army:
Article 1. The "Manchukuo" Government, in
formulating orders and ordinances with regerd to
its army, will endeavor to let the Japanese army
take fuli advantage of these regulations also.
If necessaryk it will consider the regulations
binding the Japanese army as binding equally on
those subject to "Manchukuo" law. Details of the en-
forcement of such a step will be decided on efter
further consultations.
Article 2. The Manchukuo Government will make
it possible for the Japanese military police to apply
Manchukuo laws to those under the jurisdiction of the
Manchukuo Government and duly endorse its military,
judicial and administrative policing rights in order
to carry out its duty of safeguarding the safety and
welfare of the Japane se army in any specified zone
and to ensure peace and crder there.
The action of the Japanese as specified above
shall be e ually as valid as the policine of the proper
organs of the Manchukuo Government.
If, in accordance with the fundamental principle
of common defense, Japanese and Manchukuo organs find
it necessary to take concerted action to prevent the
spread of Communism in Manchukuo, the Japanese organs
shall be empowered to take the steps necessary for
carrying out the control and co-ordinated action decided
on.
Article 3. The Japan-Manchukuo accord regarding
general judicial and police administration shall be
considered valid in regard to the cooperation of the
military, juciciel and police administrations of the
two countries.
Article 4.

be held between the Japanese and Manchukuo officials.
(h) France
(1) Representations Made by the French Embassy
in Connection with the Sino-Japanese Conflict.
On December 6 the French Ambassador made further rep-
resentations to the Foreign Office concerning the Shanghai
customs issue, stating that according to information recent-
ly obtained the Japanese authorities at Shanghai persisted
in wishing to negotiete with the local office of the Chinese
customs there, contrary to the previous arrangement made by
the Japanese authorities with the representatives of the
Powers interested in this question.*
The French Ambassador made representations to the Foreign
Office on December 18 requesting assurance that the French
Concession in Hankow and French shipping in the Hankow area
would be free of attack by Japanese forces.**
(2) French Extraterritorial Rights in Manchuria.
By a note dated December 6, the French Ambassador brought
to the attention of the Minister for Foreign Affairs the fact
that the French Consul at Mukden had informed the authorities
at Hsinking that in the oninion of the French Government the
extraterritorial rights enjoyed by French citizens in Man-
churia by virtue of treaties remained intact and could not
be abolished by the Japanese-Manchukuo Treaty of November 6,
1937. The French Ambassador's note added that his Govern-
ment
Embassy's despatch No. 2690, December 10, 1937.
**
Embassy's telegram No. 672, December 23, and Embassy's
despatch No. 2721, January 6, 1938.

-29-
ment could not admit that international agreements might be
abrogated in so simple a manner, inasmuch as the authorities
of "Manchukuo" could by no means separate themselves from
such agreements by a unilateral decision.*
(i) Australia.
A Japanese-Australian shipping conference concerned
with the ratio of Australian wool shipments carried by
Australian and Japanese ships and with other issues was con-
vened on December l but no decisions were reached before
the close of the month.
(j) The Vatican.
On December 3 the Pope appointed as Archbishop of Tokyo
the Reverend Peter Tatsuo Doi, the first Japane se to hold
the position of Archbishep in Japan.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL CCNDITICNS.
(a) The Cabinet.
Dr. Eiichi Baba, the Home Minister, tendered his resig-
nation on December 13, eiving illness as the reason for this
step. (Dr. Baba died on December 21, 1937). Admiral Nobumasa
Suyetsugu, well-known chauvinist, former Commander-in-Chief
of the Combined Fleet, and former member of the Supreme War
Council, was appointed Home Minister on December 15. Admiral
Suyetsugu, one of whose first official acts as Home Minister
494.612
was to direct the arrest of numerous left wing suspects,
issued a statement on December 22 that nothing tainted with
communism could be tolerated in Japan at the present critical
time.
(b)
* Embassy's despatch No. 2689, December 9, 1937.

=== Page 590 === (confidence 0.99)
-30-
(b) The Navy.
On December 1 Admiral Osami Nagano, Commander-in-Chief
of the Combined Fleet and of the First Fleet, was appointed
a member of the Supreme War Council and Vice Admiral Zengo
Yoshida, Commander-in-Chief of the Second Fleet, was appointed
to succeed Admiral Nagano as Commander of the Combined Fleet
and of the First Fleet.
The naval press section of the Imperial Headquarters
announced on December 1 that the Navy had lost 835 men in
China since the beginning of the hostilities.
(c) Political Parties.
The press reported a movement among the members of the
Minseito and the Seiyukai, the twc major parties in the
House of Representatives, to dissolve these parties and form
a new joint party in view of the resert emergency situation.
In addition, a movement for dissolution of the Shakai Taishuto
(Social Mass Party) was reported to have been initiated by
members of this orgai.izaticn. Cn December le Mr.Mitsuru
Toyama, Admiral Eisuke Yamamoto, and Prince Sanjo issued a
manifesto urging the amalcamat. on of all political groups in-
to a single Nationalist Party. The police arrested from 350
to 400 so-called "communists" on December 22, among them Mr.
Kanju Kato, a member of the House of Representatives and the
leader of the Shakai Taishuto. Subsequently, the Home Office
issued an order dissolving the Japan Proletariat Party and
the National Council of Labor Unions of Japan.
At the close of the month the collapse of party politics
in Japan appeared to be almost complete.*
(a)
* Embassy's despatch No. 2722, January 6, 1938.

=== Page 591 === (confidence 0.99)
-31-
(d) The Diet.
The 73rd Session of the Diet was formally opened by
the Emperor on December 26 with the reading of an Imperial
Rescript, Domei's translation of which follows:
"In opening the Imperial Diet, We take the
opportunity to inform the members of the House of
Peers and the House of Representatives that We are
g
greatly gratified to see the relations between Japan
and its treaty Powers growing in friendship and cor-
diality.
"Our officers and men are winning every battle,
enhancing their military prestige both at home and
abroad, while Our nation is solidly uniting 'behind
the rifle. in overcoming difficulties attending the
94.032
current situation.
"We are confident of attaining Our ultimate ob-
jective, by depending on the unswerving loyalty and
devotion of Our whole nation.
"We have ordered the Ministers of State to
submit to the Imperial Diet the ordinary budget for
1938 and the extraordinary military budget, together
with various legislative bills. In view of the
gravity of the situation, gentlemen, we hope that
you will cooperate in deliberating on those bills
and that you will do your shere in the conduct of State
affairs."
On the following day co'ies of the 1938-39 draft budget,
as approved by the Cabinet, which calls for the expenditure
of Y 2,867 ,796,000, exclusive of the special China Incident
budget, were distributed to members of both Houses of the
Diet.
The Diet was adjourned on December 27 for the customary
New Year recess until January 20.

Nothing to report.

V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The Finance Ministry decided on December 10 to widen
the scope of the existing foreign exchange control regula-
tions as follows: Government permits shall be required for
all transactions in excess of ¥ 50,000 involving the sale of
foreign securities and all other property abroad; for the
purchase of land or vessels for more than ¥ 50,000 a year;
for the purchase of mining, timbering, or industrial rights
in excess of y 50,o00 a year; for the offering of securities
for debts incurred by Japanese abroad; for remittances abroad
in payment of imports without bills of exchange in excess
of ¥100 a month (formerly ¥ 1,000 a month); for remittances
for travelling expenses exceeding 1,000 yen a month (formerly
Y 5,000 a month); and for remittances abroad of insurance
money, excopting premiums for re-insurance and maritime
insurance.*
Toward the end of the month the Finance Ministry issued
¥300,o00,000 worth of new national bonds in connection with
the Sino-Japanese incident.

of only ¥ 1,820,000 as compared with a favorable balance for

=== Page 605 === (confidence 0.99)
I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Sinking of the U.S.S. Gunboat PANAY by Japanese
Airplanes.
On December 1 the Embassy brcught to the attention of
the Japanese Foreign Office a report received from the
Embassy at Nanking that the U.S.S. PANAY was at Nanking
and requested that the appropriate Japanese military and
naval authorities be promptly notified of this report.
On the basis of telegrams received fron. the Embassies
in Nanking and Hankow with regard to the jeopardizing by
the Japanese military forces of the U.S.S. Gunboat PANAY
and of American refugees on Standard Oil Company ships, the
Ambassador called on the Minister for Foreign Affairs on
the morning of December 13 and requested hin to take furth-
er steps to restrain Japanese military forces from continuing
the serious risks to American lives and property involved
in the dropping of shells in the vicinity of these ships.*
Later in the day it became known that the U.S.S.
PANAY and two Standard Oil Company ships had been sunk by
Japanese naval airplanes above Nanking on the Yangtze River
on the afternoon of December 12. Mr. Hirota called per-
sonally on Mr. Grew at the Chancery on the afternoon of
December 13 and expressed the prcfound apologies of the
Japanese Government for the sinking of the PANAY.
A high official of the Foreign Office called on the
Ambassador on the afternoon of Decenber 14 and delivered
a note from the Minister for Foreign Affairs which stated
that
* Embassy's despatch No. 2710, December 23, 1937.

=== Page 606 === (confidence 0.98)

that the sinking of the PAnAY had been due to a mistake aris-

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
(1) Capture of Nanking and Other Cities in the
Shanghai-Nanking Area.
The Japanese forces continued their rapid advance against
Nanking. After several days of fighting ardund the city, in
which the Chinese resistance was described as "stubborn" by
the Japanese news agency, Domei, Japanese troops took possess-
ion of the capital on December 14. Meanwhile, according to
an International News Service despatch from Nanking deted
December 7, General Chiang Kai-shek had on that day fled by
airplane from Nanking to Hankow, accompanied by his wife and
Mr. Donald, his adviser, eluding the Japanese planes which
pursued him. The Japanese Embassy in Nenking was re-opened
on December 18 with the return from Shenghai of the Counselor,
Mr.

Mr. Shinrokuro Hidaka. According to Domei, a "Peace Mainte-
nance Commission" was inaugurated in Nanking on December 23
with a platform based on opposition to communism, severance
of relations with General Chiang Kai-shek's National Govern-
ment, and a policy of friendship toward Japan.
On December 2l Japanese forces operating on the north-
ern bank of the Yangtze River captured Chuhsien, on the
Tientsin-Pukow Railway. Three days later Japanese units
occupied the city of Hangchow, capital of Chekiang Province,
apparently without resistance on the part of the Chinese.
The Japanese took possession of Tienchang on December 26.
(2) Japanese Military Operations in Shantung
Province.
Having secured cor.trol of Shantung Province north of the
Yellow River, Japanese units proceeded south of the River and,
meeting with little Chinese opposition, on December 26 took
possession of Tsinan, capital of the Province and headquarters
of General ian Fu-chu's forces.
Following the destruction by Chinese of Japanese-owned
cotton mills in Tsingtao, on December 23 the Japanese military
h adquarters in Tientsin issued a comnuniqué to the effect
that the Japarese forces were prepered to iflict thorough-
going chastisement on the Chinese for their breach of faith
and intended to demand appropriate compensation for Japanese
property destroyed. Vice Admiral Hasegawa, Commander-in-Chief
of the Japanese Fleet in China waters, made an announcement
on December 26 that Tsingtao would be included in the zone of
Japanese "naval interruption of msritime traffic established
on September 5.

(3) Activities of the Japanese Air Forces.
Japanese Navy and Army airplanes conducted raids through-
out China during the month, bombing points in North, Central,
and South China, including Canton. Domei reported that in
the most outstanding long distance flight yet made by the
Japanese, naval planes had on Lecenber 4 bombed the airdrome
at Lanchow, capital of Kansu Province, and that on December 30
Japanese naval planes had bombed and destroyed the airdrome
at Sianfu, Shansi Province. The navy section of the Imperial
Headquarters announced that durine December Japanese naval
planes had destroyed 123 enemy planes and that since the out-
break of the Sino-Japanese hostilities they had destroyed
from 525 to 575 Chinese planes. According to the announce-
ment, up to Lecember 31 only 63 Japanese naval planes had been
1ost.
(4) Official Japanese Statements.
I a statement setting forth the views of the Japanese
Government on the situation created by the occuration of
Nanking, Premier Konoye declared on December 14 that there
was no longer any substance behind the claim of the Nanking
Governmert to be the central régime cf China; that the régime
was swiftly being reduced to . thing more than a war macnine;
that if a new régime should arise, Japan would have no choice
but to consider concrete measures for co-existence and co-
prosperity with it; that no régime in China could succeed
excepting on & pro-Japanese foundation; that cnly through
the emergence of such a pro-Japenese China could the interests
of European Powers and of the United States in the Far East
be guaranteed; and that in order to prevent a recurrence of
such a tragedy as the present China incident, Japan must not
hesitate

the capture of Nanking, both the War Minister and the military
section of the Imperial Headquarters announced that Japan
must make redoubled efforts for the achievement of its ob-
jectiis. in China and thet the termination of hostilities
must be regerded as a matter for the remote future. In a

of the campaign" had been completely attained. None of the
officials mentioned gave any definite incication of what
"the aims of the campaign" were.

to liht during the month under

consisted in the forma-

be founded in Nanking. The so-called "Provisional Government
of China" was organized in Peiping on Decembef 14 with such

become officials. It was stated that the principles of the

(b) Great Britain.
(1) Japanese Attacks on British Naval Vessels
LADYBIRD AND BEE
Reports were received by the British Embassy that the
British naval vessels LADYBIRD and BEE, as well as British
merchant vessels, all in the vicinity of Wuhu on the Yangtze
River, had on December 12 been attacked by Japanese machine
guns and field pieces; that the LADYBIRD had suffered four
direct hits with one seaman killed, another seaman seriously
wounded, and several men receiving minor injuries, including
the flag captain, and that the BEE had been fired upon but
with no damage resulting. On December 13 the British Ambassa-
dor protested personally in the most emphatic manner to the
Minister for Foreign Affairs against these attacks, stating
that "British public orinion was bound to take a most serious
view of this incident" and urging that the Japanese Govern-
ment make a suitable apology as soon as possible. Mr. Hirota
called in person on Sir Robert Craigie on the afternoon of
December 14 and expressed apology and regrets on behalf of
the Japanese Government.
On December 14 the British Ambassador addressed to the
Minister for Foreign Affairs a letter to the effect that the
Japanese order to fire on all ships moving on the Yangtze
appeared still to be in force and that furthermore the guns
of Japanese batteries were still trained pointblank on the
LADYBIRD and the BEE.
Sir Robert Craigie stated with regard
to the first point that it appeared to him to disclose on the
part of the responsible Japanese military authorities in
China a regrettable disregard for the consequences of their
action of December 12, and with regard to the second point
that

=== Page 623 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
that he emphatically protested and requested that immediate
instructions be sent to the Japanese commander at Wuhu to
desist from "this threatening and wholly unnecessary action."
Acting under instructions received from his Government,
on December 16 the British Ambessador addressed to the Minister
for Foreign Affairs a note protesting against the attacks made
by Japanese aircraft and land forces on the British warships
LADYBIRD and BEE and on British merchant vessels at Wuhu and
near Nanking on December 12. The note expressed the satis-
faction of the British Government at having received the note
of the Minister for Foreign Affairs dated December l4, which
offered the profound apology of the Japanese Government for
the attacks on the British vessels, stated that measures had
been immediately taken to prevent the reourrence of such in-
would
cidents, and added that the Japanese Government/deal suitably
with those responsible for the incidents and would pay the
necessary compensation. In addition, the British note re-
quested that an ar surance be given that all that had been
said in the Japanese note applied equally to Japanese attacks
on British merchant vessels. In conclusion, the British
Government asked to be informed what measures had actually
been taken of a character which would put a definite stop to
these incidents.*
On December 18 the British Embassy re-
ceived the Foreign Office's provisional reply, which stated
that the assurances given in the Foreign Cffice's note of
December 14 with regard to British warships were equally ap-
plicable to the British merchant vessels which were attacked
in similar circumstances on December 12.
The
Embassy's telegram No. 642, December 16, 1937.

=== Page 624 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
The British Ambassador received on December 28 the final
reply of the Minister for Foreign Affairs to the formal British
representations of December 16. The reply stated that the
attacks upon British vessels had been a mistake; that as
soon as the vessels were known to be British, Japanese bomb-
ing and military units had ceased firing and offered medical
assistance; that with reference to the reported statement of
a Japanese officer that he had received orders to fire on
all vessels on the Yangtze River, the orders issued referred
only to veswels used by the Chinese; that in order to ensure
egainst recurrence of similar incidents those responsible
were being dealt with according to law; and that further to
guard against recurrence of such incidents greater caution
would be exercised in areas where there were vessels of third
Powers even if it should entail permitting Chinese forces to
escape.* Pursuant to instructions received from the British
Government, on December 31 Sir Robert Craigie addressed to
Mr. Hirota a note in reply, the substance of which was that
the British Government noted with satisfaction that the
Japanese Government had taken or was prepered to take the
necessary measures to deal suitably with the officers res-
ponsible for the incidents and to prevent any repetition.
The British note ended by stating that the details of the
Japanese Government's measures and their effective applica-
tion might in the opinion of the British Government suitably
form the subject of further conversations in the course of
which the British Government did not doubt that it would be
informed of actual steps decided upon.

(3) Anti-British Feeling in Japan.
Anti-British feeling in Japan was somewhat abated during
the month by expressions of regret over the incident involv-
ing the British naval vessels LADYBIRD and BEE. However, the
Japanese continued to criticize Great Britain for its alleged-
ly un-neutral action in supplying arms and munitions to China,
for the "unsympathetic attitude" of the British authorities
in Chira, and for what the Japanese chose to call Great
Britain's policy of befriending and encouraging China and of
obstructing Japan in its campaign to "establish lasting peace
in the Far East."
(c) Soviet Russia.
Japanese-Soviet Russian relations, in which danger is al-
ways latent, were ruffled on the surface by three controver-
sies: the fisheries issue, the continued detention by Soviet
authorities of seven Japanese subjects, and the forced migration
into remote parts of the Soviet Union of a large number of
Korean residents of the Maritime Provinces.
Despite Japan's insistence that the Soviet Government
reconsider its attitude and agree tc sign a new fisheries
convention to replace the convention of l928, the Soviet
Union retused to come to terms. A comnittee of members of
the Upper and Lower Houses called on the Minister for For-
eign Affairs on December & and presented to him a resolution
to the effect that the Soviet attitude toward a new fisher-
ies convention could not be overlooked from the stendpoint
of international good faith and protection of Japan's rights
and interests. Since the term of duration of the Japanese-
Soviet

=== Page 628 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
Soviet fishery convention signed on January 23, l928, and
prolonged by the protocol of December 28, 1936, was due to
come to an end on December 31, 1937, the two governments
signed a protocol on December 29, 1937, providing that the
convention of 1928 shall remain in force until December 31,
l938. The Russian Waters Fishing Guild, composed of firms
engaged in fishing off Kamchetka, issued a statement through
Domei on December 30 to the effect that the Guild was not
at all satisfied with "such a provisional settlenent achieved
through patchwork" and was resolved to do everything possible
to obtain a new agreement which would respect Japan's rights
and interests accruing from the Portsmouth Treaty.
On December 17 the Foreign Office handed to the Soviet
Embassy in Tokyo a memorandum protesting against the con-
tinuous detention since March 27, 1937, of seven Japanese
subjects on churges of espionage and other "grave offenses"
against the Soviet Union. The Japanese memorandum was ac-
companied by a request for "a responsible an swer" at the
earliest possible date.
Mr. Kawai, the Foreign Office spokesman, issued a state-
ment on Lecember 6 concerning the Soviet Government's dis-
regard of Japan's repeated protests against the Soviet policy
of forcing Koreans living in the Maritime Frovince to migrate
to distant parts of the Soviet Union. Mr. Kawai said that
Japan could not recognize the Soviet contention thet the Koreans
were Soviet citizens, and that unless the Mosccw authorities
modified their attitude on the issue the Japanese Government
would be obliged to take what it deemed to be apprcpriate
measures.

(d) Italy.
According to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of December 12, on
the evening of December ll the Counselor of the Italian Em-
bassy called at the Foreign Office and "had the honor of
especially notifying" the Japanese Government of Italy's
formal withdrawal from the League of Nations. It was said
that the Italian Counselor's call at the Foreign Office co-
incided precisely with the time of Italy's notificaticn to
the League.
A Domei despatch from Rome dated December 2l reported
that Premier Mussolini had on that day given full approval
of Japan's current action in China in the course of a con-
versation with Mr. Seigo Nakano, "unofficial Japanese envoy"
to Italy.
A subsequent despatch of this news agency from
Rome stated that according to an official announcement of
the Italian Government made on December 25, Premier Mussolini
had decided to send shortly to Japan a special commission in-
cluding representatives of the Facist Government, Army and
Navy, with the view to consolidating the Rome-Berlin-Tokyo
axis.
(e) Germany.
Notwithstanding press predictions, Germany's contemplated
de jure recognition of "anchukuo" did not materialize during
the month.*
In a press conference on December 6, the Foreign Office
spokesman asserted that Japan had not been approached by any
country to use its good offices for mediation in the Sino-
Japanese conflict, and denied knowledge of the reported ac-
tivities looking toward mediation of the German Ambassador
to China, Dr. Trautmann.

(r) Spain.
The Japanese Government formally extended recognition
to the Franco Government of Spain on December 1 and recognized
Mr. F. J. del Castillio as Spanish Chargé d'affaires in Tokyo.
On this occasion the Foreign Office spokesman issued the
following statement:
"Although unfortunately the internecine war
in Spain has not yet come to an end, the extension
of formal recognition to the Government under Gen-
eral Franco is in conformity with the realities of
the situation, since that Government not only controls
more than a half of the territory of Spain and all of
the colonies, tut has built up a solid foundation as
a government. The administrative and judicial functions
are being carried on normally in the occupied areas in
sharp contrast to the chaotic and anarchical conditions
prevailing in Valencia and Barcelona.
"That the Spanish disturbance originated as a
result of the activities of the People's Front created
through the Comintern's intrigues and machinations is
clear in the light of the circumstances surrounding
its outbreak. General Franco has been fighting for
the express purpose of expelli.g Communism and Anarchism
since July last year, when the outbreak began there,
and it is generally known that both Germany and Italy,
united in the common aim of guarding against the Comin-
tern, extended recognition to the Franco Government in
November last year.
"It is a matter for sincere congratulation that
through the present recegnition, friendly relations have
been established between our country and the Franco
Government with which we are in agreement regarding
the policy to be pursued toward the Comintern. Simul-
taneously we find deep significance in this recognition,
as being certain to promote the intimate relations now
existing between Japan, Germany, and Italy."
Two days later a ceremony at which the fleg of the Franco
Government was hoisted was held at the Spanish Legation and
was attended by the Minister for Foreign Affairs and the
German, Italian, and "Manchukuo" Ambassadors.
On

(g) "Manchukuo".
The following revised agreement dated December 1 con-
cerning rights of the Japanese military forces in "Manchukuo"
territory was signed by General Chang Ching-hui, Premier of
"Manchukuo", and General Uyede, Japanese Ambassador to
"Manchukuo" and Commander of the Kwantung Army:
Article 1. The "Manchukuo" Government, in
formulating orders and ordinances with regerd to
its army, will endeavor to let the Japanese army
take fuli advartage of these regulations also.
If necessaryk it will consider the regulations
binding the Japanese army as binding equally on
those subject to "Manchukuo" law. Details of the en-
forcement of such a step will be decided on efter
further consultations.
Article 2. The Manchukuo Government will make
it possible for the Japanese military police to apply
Manchukuo laws to those under the jurisdiction of the
Manchukuo Government and duly endorse its military,
judicial and administrative policing rights in order
to carry out its duty of safeguarding the safety and
welfare of the Japane se army in any specified zone
and to unsure peace and order there.
The action of the Japanese as specified above
shall be ecually as valid as the policing of the proper
organs of the Manchukuo Government.
If, in accordance with the fundamental principle
of common defense, Japanese and Manchukuo organs find
it necessary to take concerted action to prevent the
spread of Communism in Manchukuo, the Japanese organs
shall be empowered to take the steps necessary for
carrying out the control and co-ordinated action d ecided
on.
Article 3. The Japan-Manchukuo accord regarding
general judicial and police administration shall be
considered valid in regard to the cooperation of the
military, judiciel and police administrations of the
two countries.
Article 4.

Article 4. For the enforcement of this agreement
in time of war or emergency, further consultations will
be held between the Japanese and Manchukuo officials.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL CONDITICNS.
(a) The Cabinet.
Dr. Eiichi Baba, the Home Minister, tendered his resig-
nation on December 13, giving illness as the reason for this
step. (Dr. Baba died on December 21, 1937). Admiral Nobumasa
Suyetsugu, well-known chauvinist, former Commander-in-Chief
of the Combined Fleet, and former member of the Supreme War
Council, was appointed Home Minister on December 15. Admiral
Suyetsugu, one of whose first official acts as Home Minister
was to direct the arrest of numerous left wing suspects,
issued a statement on December 22 that nothing tainted with
communism could be tolerated in Japan at the present critical
time.
(b)
* Embassy's despatch No. 2689, December 9, 1937.

(b) The Navy.
On December 1 Admiral Osami Nagano, Commander-in-Chief
of the Combined Fleet and of the First Fleet, was appointed
a member of the Supreme War Council and Vice Admiral Zengo
Yoshida, Commander-in-Chief of the Second Fleet, was appointed
to succeed Admiral Nagano as Commander of the Combined Fleet
and of th First Fleet.
The naval press section of the Imperial Headquarters
announced on December 1 that the Navy had lost 835 men in
China since the beginnirg of the hostilities.
(c) Political Parties.
The press reported a movement among the members of the
Minseito and the Seiyukai, the twc major parties in the
House of Representatives, tc dissolve these parties and form
a new joint party in view of the resert emergency situation.
In addition, a movement for dissoluti n of the Shakai Taishuto
(Social Mass Party) was reported to have been initiated by
members of this orgaiizatien. Cn December le Mr.Mitsuru
Toyama, Admiral Eisuke Yamamoto, and Prince Sanjo issued a
manifesto urging the amalgamat: on of all political groups in-
to a single Nationalint Party. The police arrested from 350
to 400 so-called "communists" on December 22, among them Mr.
Kanju Kato, a member of the House of Representatives and the
leader of the Shakai Taishuto. Subsequently, the Home Office
issued an order dissolving the Japan Proletariat Party and
the National Council of Labor Unions of Japan.
At the close of the month the collapse of party politics
in Japan appeared to be almost complete.*

(a) The Diet.
The 73rd Session of the Diet was formally opened by
the Emperor on December 26 with the reading of an Imperial
Rescript, Domei's translation of which follows:
"In opening the Imperial Diet, We take the
opportunity to inform the members of the House of
Peers and the House of Representatives that We are
g
greatly gratified to see the relations between Japan
and its treaty Powers growing in friendship and cor-
diality.
"Our officers and men are winning every battle,
enhancing their military prestige both at home and
abroad, while Our nation is solidly uniting 'behind
the rifle. in overcoming difficulties attending the
current situation.
"We are confident of attaining Our ultimate ob-
jective, by depending on the unswerving loyalty and
devotion of Our whole nation.
"We have ordered the Ministers of State to
submit to the Imperial Diet the ordinary budget for
1938 and the extraordinary military budget, together
with various legislative bills. In view of the
gravity of the situation, gentlemen, we hope that
you will cooperate in deliberating on those bills
and that you will do your share in the conduct of State
affairs."
On the following day co ies of the 1938-39 draft budget,
as approved by the Cabinet, which calls for the expenditure
of Y 2,867 ,796,000, exclusive of the special China Incident
budget, were distributed to members of both Houses of the
Diet.
The Diet was adjourned on December 27 for the customary
New Year recess until January 20.

V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The Finance Ministry decided on December 10 to widen
the scope of the existing foreign exchange control regula-
tions as follows: Government permits shall be required for
all transactions in excess of y 50,000 involving the sale of
foreign securities and all other property abroad; for the
purchase of land or vessels for more than ¥ 50,000 a year;
for the purchase of mining, timbering, or industrial rights
in excess of y 50,000 a year; for the offering of securities
for debts incurred by Japanese abroad; for remittances abroad
in payment of imports without bills of exchange in excess
of ¥100 a month (formerly y 1,000 a month); for remittances
for travelling expenses exceeding 1,000 yen a month (formerly
Y 5,000 a month); and for remittances abroad of insurance
money, excepting premiums for re-insurance and maritime
insurance.*
Toward the end of the month the Finance Ministry issued
¥300,oo0,oo0 worth of new national bonds in connection with
the Sino-Japanese incident.

of only y 1,820,000 as compared with a favorable balance for

According to investigations
by the Labor Section
of the Prefectural Police made public in December, there
